# SAND-T1′ — the table

Synthetic content: invented characters in an invented campaign (Table C's Beregost files, docs/campaigns/baldurs-gate-en). Stage origin/claude/handoff at 23220d6 (docs/stages/GAME_PARTY_1.md, the amendment of 2026-10-27 16:36 LA); design sha256 `a39fed4738bc3e499ae4007e794f2e8f49197f09c09f57e828a13e4192078d91`.

Arm **P-SAND**, triad T1; 25 round(s) of 25; ended by error (r25: the DM's turn failed (conflict)). The DM: Claude Opus 5.5 with the owner's settings (Opus writes — Opus records, depth high → xhigh); the relay: the same model at medium. Started 2026-10-29T07:32:34.375Z.

Seats: 1. Bram Halloway (fighter) — Claude Opus 5.5; 2. Sefa Marrin (warlock) — GPT-6.1 Sol; 3. Pell Dunmore (rogue) — DeepSeek V4 Pro 0813. The players are not told what the others are.

Known condition: Seat 1's character (Bram) is the app's hero record; Sefa and Pell are party companions with full records. Known condition, not corrected: the DM's frame is written around the hero and may give that seat more attention; Heroic Inspiration is the hero's only; the allowed rerolls (2 per session) are the table's, shared by all three. The rotation puts each family in that seat once per triad.

**No hidden layer:** P-SAND plays pure simulation (no prep).

The world's snapshot: sha256 `1649b61c08e027ff44de2bb5065046f872e383a00c5d04803d95553f58568f5a` (the same wizard, sheets and opening for both triads).

## The opening

**The DM's opening (as committed by the wizard):**

````text
Beregost smells of coal smoke and hot iron even in here. Through the shutters comes the steady ring of hammers from the smithies along the Coast Way, and the light is going amber. The Burning Wizard's common room is filling up for the evening: carters, a pair of dusty sellswords, townsfolk with soot still on their forearms. The benches are crowded, and that is how three strangers end up at the same table.

Bram Halloway sits with his back to the wall and his shield propped against the bench, the old company mark painted over. Across from him is Sefa Marrin, a young woman just off the road from Baldur's Gate. Beside her is Pell Dunmore, a lean man from the Lower City with a healer's satchel slung at his hip. He came in by the western road and sat down so quietly that nobody heard him do it.

None of them has said a word to the others yet. Then Orla Tarnwick, the innkeeper, pushes through the crowd with a jug in one hand and three cups hooked on her fingers. She sets them down hard and looks from one face to the next.

"Last free seats in the house, and you three took them together," she says. "So: one tab or three? And what brings each of you into Beregost tonight?"
````

## Round 1

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 1 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram drops a few coins on the table before anyone else can sort it out: three silver, enough for the jug and a little extra.

"That covers the jug. Put the cups on whoever drinks the most," he says, nodding at the other two with a half-smile. "Separate tabs after that. I don't know these two well enough yet to pay for their supper."

He pours for all three without asking, then sits back against the wall.

"Came up the Coast Way from Nashkel. My last caravan paid me off there. Sixteen years on the Trade Way, and the ones who hire guards are getting fewer and nervous-er." He glances toward the dusty sellswords near the hearth, then back at the innkeeper. "So: a bed for the night, a hot meal, and an ear for whoever's hiring. If someone in town is looking for a shield, I'd like to hear it."

He lifts his cup a little toward the young woman and the quiet man with the satchel.

"Bram Halloway. Road's been long and dry. Where'd you two come in from?"

*(Bram is also keeping half an eye on the room, out of habit. Who's drinking hard, who's watching the door, whether anyone looks like trouble.)*

Sefa Marrin: “Three tabs, please. We’ve not yet had time to become bad influences on one another.”

I set my pack beneath the bench, grateful to have its weight off my shoulders.

“Sefa Marrin. Down from the Gate, and tonight I’m mostly here for supper and somewhere to sleep. What’s hot, and what does a bed cost?”

I turn toward my tablemates, with a glance at the healer’s satchel.

“I used to work in a hospice. If either of you has a cure for feet after a day on the road, I’m listening.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell lifts his cup an inch, as if testing its weight, then sets it down without drinking. His eyes move from the woman to the man, unhurried, the way a man reads a doorway before he goes through it.

"Three tabs," he says, mild and with the faintest edge of a Lower City rasp. "No sense in strangers arguing over a bill before they've shared a word."

He half-turns to glance toward the crowded common room, then back to the innkeeper. "And as for me, I came in off the High Hedge road. Looking for a room and a quiet cup before I work out whether there's work here worth staying for." A small pause, a shrug. "Thought Beregost might have use for a man who can get into places — or out of them — without knocking over the furniture."

He lets the remark land without emphasis, then adds, more plainly: "Pell."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla scoops Bram's three silver off the table without counting it and drops it into her apron.

"Jug's paid. Three tabs, then." She taps the table edge once for each of them, as if fixing their places in her head. "Stew's mutton and barley, three silver the bowl with bread. There's a pie too, but it's yesterday's, and I'll not lie about it. Beds: five silver in the loft, where the carters snore, or eight for a room with a door and a bar on it. Pay me when you've eaten. I know where you're sitting."

She glances at Pell when he mentions getting into places, and her eyebrows go up a little.

"Not into my cellar, you won't." Then, to Bram, while she shoves a stray cup further onto the table with her hip: "Hiring. You've timed it well or badly, I can't tell which. There's a wool and salt man, Aldous Venn, with wagons out of the Gate. He's had two loads not turn up in Nashkel since the thaw. No wreck found, no word, nothing. He's been in most nights after the temple bell, asking about guards and complaining about the price. Those two by the fire are waiting on him as well." She tips her head toward the hearth. "And there's a notice board by the door, if you read. Half of it's smiths wanting apprentices. Sit tight. Stew's coming."

She's gone into the crowd before anyone can answer.

From where Bram sits against the wall he can see most of the room. Nothing in it looks like trouble yet, but it's worth knowing. The two dusty sellswords by the hearth are a broad woman with a cropped head and a younger man with a bandaged left hand. They aren't drinking much. The woman looks up every time the street door opens, then looks away again. A carter sits alone at the end of the long bench with a wet cuff, and he's on his third cup by the empties in front of him. He's drinking like a man who wants the evening over with. In the far corner a man in a good grey travelling coat, too clean for Beregost, sits with a soot-armed smith. A lump of dark ore lies on the table between them, and the smith keeps turning it over in his thick fingers and shaking his head. Nobody is watching the three strangers at the corner table. Everyone in here has their own evening to get through.

Outside, the hammers along the Coast Way stop one by one as the smithies close up for the night.
````

## Round 2

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 2 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram catches the innkeeper's eye as she goes and raises two fingers. *Stew.* Then he points up. *Room with a door.* Eight silver is steep, but he's had enough of carters snoring for one lifetime.

To Sefa, dryly: "Feet? Dry socks, every night, no matter how tired you are. And walk the first hour slow. Wish someone had told me at sixteen." He glances at Pell's untouched cup. "And a man who gets into places quietly. Well. As long as it isn't my room."

He turns his cup a slow circle on the table, eyes drifting to the fire.

"Two loads gone between here and Nashkel, and no wreck." He says it low, half to himself. "That's not wolves. Wolves leave wagons. Bandits leave wagons, and bodies as well. Something that takes the whole load, oxen and all, and leaves the road clean..." He shakes his head. "I just came up that road. I'd like to know what I walked past."

He nods toward the hearth. "Those two are waiting on Venn as well. The woman jumps every time the door goes. Either she's owed money or she's scared of someone. Bandaged hand on the lad, too. They may have tried the road already."

His glance moves to the corner, the clean grey coat and the lump of ore. He keeps that to himself for now, but he watches the smith turn the stone over once more.

"I'll eat, then I'll wait for this Venn after the bell and hear his price." To both of them, plainly: "If either of you is short of coin and not afraid of the road, a merchant pays better for a party than for one guard. A healer and a quiet man aren't the worst company to bring. No promises. Just something to think on over your stew."

*(While he waits for the food: can Bram tell, from where he sits, what kind of ore that is, or what the grey coat is offering the smith? Just a passive look. He won't go over.)*

Sefa Marrin: “Stew for me, please—and a room with a door,” I call after her, if she’s still within earshot.

I lift my cup to Bram. “Thank you. I’ll stand the next jug.”

Then I look between them. “Guard work and a knack for getting out of places. I can tend wounds, though I’d rather earn my supper preventing them. We might be worth more to this Venn together—if his offer’s any good.”

I take a small drink. “Two whole loads gone without word sounds like more than a shortage of guards. I’d want to hear where they were last seen, and whether anyone’s actually gone looking.”

While we wait for supper, I go over to read the notice board.

Pell Dunmore: (Pell's player wrote nothing this round.)
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla is halfway to the kitchen door when she catches Bram's two fingers and the thumb jerked at the ceiling. She points back at him, *stew, door*, and is gone into the steam.

Sefa's call follows her a moment later. Orla doesn't turn round, but one hand goes up over the heads of the crowd to show she heard.

From his place against the wall, Bram gets a good look at the far corner while the room roars around him. He grew up in a smithy's yard, so the lump on the table is easy to read even from here. It's iron ore, a fist-sized piece. It's dark, nearly black, and the faces where it was broken are dull and close-grained. It isn't the rusty red-brown stone that comes out of the Nashkel pits; he has watched enough of that go by in carts. He can't tell where this came from. The smith clearly can't stop handling it, though. He weighs it, turns it to the lamp, rubs his thumb along an edge, then sets it down and pushes it back across the table. The man in the grey coat pushes it back again. Then a small leather purse goes on the table beside the stone. He doesn't open it. He holds up three fingers, and then all five. The smith shakes his head and doesn't touch the purse. Nothing either of them says carries over the noise.

---

The notice board by the street door is a slab of planking studded with nails and old pin-holes. Sefa reads it under the door lamp while carters squeeze past her shoulder.

Orla wasn't wrong about the smiths. Four notices want apprentices. Two are in the same blunt hand, from the Thunderhammer forge: *strong lad or lass, must not mind heat or early hours.* One is in Dwarvish runes, and Sefa reads it as easily as the rest: *Wanted: an apprentice who can keep his mouth shut and his fingers on.*

Below those hangs a neat card from the Song of the Morning. *Healing at the temple after dawn prayers. An offering according to means.*

There is an older Flaming Fist notice, sun-yellowed: *Bandits on the Coast Way, five gold a head, proof required.* Someone has pasted a strip of paper across the middle: *Payment held until the paymaster comes down from the Gate.* Someone else has written *again* beside it in charcoal.

There is a tanner's warning, misspelt and urgent. Something has been digging in the fields south of town. Two sheep are gone, and the holes are *deep as a well, with no earth thrown up beside them.* Keep children and dogs off the south fields.

Near the top, pinned with two nails as if the writer meant it to stay up, is a merchant's notice in a tidy clerk's hand:

*GUARDS WANTED. Wagons, Beregost to Nashkel and back. Sound men or women with their own kit and some experience of the road. Apply to A. Venn, the Burning Wizard, after the temple bell.*

It says nothing about pay.

---

When Sefa gets back to the corner, the stew is already there. Orla puts the bowls down hard and sets bread beside them: two bowls of mutton and barley, steaming, with a slick of fat on top and bread torn rather than cut. One is for Bram and one for Sefa's empty place. She taps the table once beside each bowl.

"Two rooms with doors, top of the stairs, left side. They're the last two with bars," she says. Then, to Pell, whose cup is still full: "You eating, or just guarding that drink?"

 (Out of game: none of these needed a roll. Both orders went through, Bram's question was talk for the other two players to answer, and his passive look at the ore and the purse was already told in the scene.)
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Sefa calls after Orla, "Stew for me, please, and a room with a door," if she's still within earshot.

She lifts her cup to you. "Thank you. I'll stand the next jug."

Then she looks between you and Pell. "Guard work and a knack for getting out of places. I can tend wounds, though I'd rather earn my supper preventing them. We might be worth more to this Venn together, if his offer's any good."

She takes a small drink. "Two whole loads gone without word sounds like more than a shortage of guards. I'd want to hear where they were last seen, and whether anyone's actually gone looking."

Pell says nothing. His cup is still full.

Sefa gets up and makes her way through the crowd toward the notice board by the street door.

---

Orla is halfway to the kitchen door when she catches your two fingers and the thumb jerked at the ceiling. She points back at you, *stew, door*, and is gone into the steam.

Sefa's call follows her a moment later. Orla doesn't turn round, but one hand goes up over the heads of the crowd to show she heard.

From your place against the wall you get a good look at the far corner while the room roars around you. You grew up in a smithy's yard, so the lump on the table is easy to read even from here. The smith shakes his head and doesn't touch the purse. Nothing either of them says carries over the noise.

---

Across the room, Sefa stands reading the notice board under the door lamp while carters squeeze past her shoulder. After a while she comes back to the corner.

The stew is already there by then. Orla puts the bowls down hard and sets bread beside them: two bowls of mutton and barley, steaming, with a slick of fat on top and bread torn rather than cut. One is for you and one for Sefa's empty place. She taps the table once beside each bowl.
````

````text
He turns his cup a slow circle on the table, his eyes drifting to the fire.

"Two loads gone between here and Nashkel, and no wreck." He says it low, half to himself. "That's not wolves. Wolves leave wagons. Bandits leave wagons, and bodies as well. Something that takes the whole load, oxen and all, and leaves the road clean..." He shakes his head. "I just came up that road. I'd like to know what I walked past."

He nods toward the hearth. "Those two are waiting on Venn as well. The woman jumps every time the door goes. Either she's owed money or she's scared of someone. Bandaged hand on the lad, too. They may have tried the road already."

His glance moves to the far corner and stays there a moment.

"I'll eat, then I'll wait for this Venn after the bell and hear his price." To you and Pell, plainly: "If either of you is short of coin and not afraid of the road, a merchant pays better for a party than for one guard. A healer and a quiet man aren't the worst company to bring. No promises. Just something to think on over your stew."

Pell says nothing. His cup is still full.

---

Orla is halfway to the kitchen door when she catches Bram's two fingers and the thumb jerked at the ceiling. She points back at him, *stew, door*, and is gone into the steam.

Your call follows her a moment later. Orla doesn't turn round, but one hand goes up over the heads of the crowd to show she heard.

---

The notice board by the street door is a slab of planking studded with nails and old pin-holes. You read it under the door lamp while carters squeeze past your shoulder.

Orla wasn't wrong about the smiths. Four notices want apprentices. Two are in the same blunt hand, from the Thunderhammer forge: *strong lad or lass, must not mind heat or early hours.* One is in Dwarvish runes, and you read it as easily as the rest: *Wanted: an apprentice who can keep his mouth shut and his fingers on.*

Below those hangs a neat card from the Song of the Morning. *Healing at the temple after dawn prayers. An offering according to means.*

There is an older Flaming Fist notice, sun-yellowed: *Bandits on the Coast Way, five gold a head, proof required.* Someone has pasted a strip of paper across the middle: *Payment held until the paymaster comes down from the Gate.* Someone else has written *again* beside it in charcoal.

There is a tanner's warning, misspelt and urgent. Something has been digging in the fields south of town. Two sheep are gone, and the holes are *deep as a well, with no earth thrown up beside them.* Keep children and dogs off the south fields.

Near the top, pinned with two nails as if the writer meant it to stay up, is a merchant's notice in a tidy clerk's hand:

*GUARDS WANTED. Wagons, Beregost to Nashkel and back. Sound men or women with their own kit and some experience of the road. Apply to A. Venn, the Burning Wizard, after the temple bell.*

It says nothing about pay.

---

When you get back to the corner, the stew is already there. Orla puts the bowls down hard and sets bread beside them: two bowls of mutton and barley, steaming, with a slick of fat on top and bread torn rather than cut. One is for Bram and one for your empty place. She taps the table once beside each bowl.

"Two rooms with doors, top of the stairs, left side. They're the last two with bars," she says. Then, to Pell, whose cup is still full: "You eating, or just guarding that drink?"
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram catches the innkeeper's eye as she goes. He raises two fingers, then points up.

To Sefa, dryly: "Feet? Dry socks, every night, no matter how tired you are. And walk the first hour slow. Wish someone had told me at sixteen." He glances at your untouched cup. "And a man who gets into places quietly. Well. As long as it isn't my room."

He turns his cup a slow circle on the table, his eyes drifting to the fire.

"Two loads gone between here and Nashkel, and no wreck." He says it low, half to himself. "That's not wolves. Wolves leave wagons. Bandits leave wagons, and bodies as well. Something that takes the whole load, oxen and all, and leaves the road clean..." He shakes his head. "I just came up that road. I'd like to know what I walked past."

He nods toward the hearth. "Those two are waiting on Venn as well. The woman jumps every time the door goes. Either she's owed money or she's scared of someone. Bandaged hand on the lad, too. They may have tried the road already."

His glance moves to the far corner and stays there a moment.

"I'll eat, then I'll wait for this Venn after the bell and hear his price." To you and Sefa, plainly: "If either of you is short of coin and not afraid of the road, a merchant pays better for a party than for one guard. A healer and a quiet man aren't the worst company to bring. No promises. Just something to think on over your stew."

Sefa calls after Orla, "Stew for me, please, and a room with a door," if she's still within earshot.

She lifts her cup to Bram. "Thank you. I'll stand the next jug."

Then she looks between you and Bram. "Guard work and a knack for getting out of places. I can tend wounds, though I'd rather earn my supper preventing them. We might be worth more to this Venn together, if his offer's any good."

She takes a small drink. "Two whole loads gone without word sounds like more than a shortage of guards. I'd want to hear where they were last seen, and whether anyone's actually gone looking."

---

Orla is halfway to the kitchen door when she catches Bram's two fingers and the thumb jerked at the ceiling. She points back at him, *stew, door*, and is gone into the steam.

Sefa's call follows her a moment later. Orla doesn't turn round, but one hand goes up over the heads of the crowd to show she heard.

Sefa gets up and makes her way through the crowd to the notice board by the street door. She stands reading it under the door lamp while carters squeeze past her shoulder, and after a while she comes back to the corner.

The stew is already there by then. Orla puts the bowls down hard and sets bread beside them: two bowls of mutton and barley, steaming, with a slick of fat on top and bread torn rather than cut. One is for Bram and one for Sefa's empty place. She taps the table once beside each bowl.

"Two rooms with doors, top of the stairs, left side. They're the last two with bars," she says. Then, to you, whose cup is still full: "You eating, or just guarding that drink?"
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 3

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 3 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram tears a piece of bread, wipes it through the fat, and eats like a man who's had hardtack for a week. When Sefa sits back down he nods at her bowl.

"Eat while it's hot. Orla doesn't strike me as someone who reheats." Between mouthfuls: "Anything on the board besides smiths wanting boys to pump bellows?"

He keeps his voice low and tips his head a fraction toward the far corner, without looking.

"Our clean friend in the grey coat. That's iron ore he's selling, and it's not Nashkel stone. Nashkel's red and rusty. That's black and close-grained. Never seen it come down the road. He's pushing it on a smith who keeps touching it like he wants it and won't take the purse." He chews and swallows. "A smith who won't take money for good iron either doesn't trust where it came from, or is scared of who's asking."

He shrugs and goes back to the stew.

"Not my business. Not yet." A glance at Pell's full cup. "You going to drink that or just keep it company? It's paid for."

When the bowl is nearly empty he sets the spoon down, turns so he can see the street door, and settles in to wait for the temple bell and Aldous Venn.

*(He'll stay at the table until Venn comes in. If the grey coat leaves first, Bram notes which way he goes and whether the smith follows. He just watches, doesn't move.)*

Sefa Marrin: I count out eleven silver for Orla. “For supper and the room. Thank you.”

Settling back onto the bench, I tell the others, “Venn’s notice promises work, but doesn’t mention pay. There’s also a warning about deep holes in the south fields—two sheep missing, and no earth piled beside the holes. Worth remembering if we leave that way.”

I tear off some bread, then glance toward the pair by the hearth.

“If the lad’s hurt, I can at least offer to look. Easier to ask about a bad journey once you’ve introduced yourself.”

I take a few spoonfuls of stew before approaching them, keeping enough distance not to crowd their seats.

“Evening. I’m Sefa. I worked in a hospice in the Gate.” I nod toward the bandaged hand. “Is that being tended? I’d be happy to have a look, if you’d like.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell gives the cup a slow quarter-turn on the table, then nods at Orla. "Stew. And I'll keep the drink a while yet."

He finally picks up the cup and takes a short sip, as if to prove he wasn't guarding it. Then he lowers it and lets his shoulders settle back against the wall—there's no wall behind him, so he half-turns on the bench to take in the room while he speaks.

To Bram: "Eight silver for a door that bars. Might be the best bargain in town if the two by the fire are who they look like." He keeps his voice under the noise of the common room. "I didn't come up the Trade Way, so I've no idea what two missing loads leaves behind. But if they're still hiring after the wagons stop showing, it means Venn hasn't got enough men to keep the next one on the road. That says more than his price will."

He shows the room a thin, quick smile that isn't meant to be charming. "I'll hear him. No harm in listening. But I don't sign with a man until I've counted the silver and looked at the map." He inclines his head toward the notice board. "Anything else pinned there—or is it all smiths missing apprentices?"
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram eats steadily, the way a man eats after a week of hardtack. Pell's sip is short, but it's a sip, and the cup goes back on the table a finger lower than it was.

Orla comes back through the crowd with a cloth over her shoulder in time to catch Sefa's eleven silver. They go into her apron uncounted, the same as Bram's did.

"Supper and a door. You're square," she tells Sefa. Then to Pell, already turning: "Stew, then. Loft's all that's left. Five silver if you want it, and you'll want it before the carters start choosing." She hears his question about the board on her way past and answers it over her shoulder without stopping. "Smiths. The temple. The Fist's bounty they're not paying. And the tanner's holes. That's your lot."

She's back with a third bowl before Sefa has crossed the room. She sets it in front of Pell with bread torn off the same loaf.

---

From his seat against the wall, Bram watches the far corner while he eats.

It ends quietly. The smith pushes the black stone back across the table one last time and stands up, wiping his palms on his apron as if they're dirty. He says something short. Bram can't hear it, but he can read the shake of the head that goes with it. Then the smith walks out through the street door into the dusk. He doesn't take the purse and he doesn't look back.

The man in the grey coat sits a moment longer. Then he puts the stone into one coat pocket and the purse into the other, unhurried, like a man who has heard no before and expects to hear yes somewhere else. He catches a serving girl's sleeve and orders another cup. He isn't leaving.

---

The two by the hearth watch Sefa come. The woman sizes her up with one slow look, from her hands to her boots. The young man tucks his bandaged hand under his arm.

"Hedda," the woman says, giving a name for a name and nothing more. Up close she's older than she looked from across the room. There's grey in the cropped hair, and an old Flaming Fist burn-brand has faded to a smudge on the side of her neck. She tips her chin at the lad. "Tob. Show her."

"It's nothing," Tobin says. He's maybe twenty, with sunburn peeling across his nose, and he's plainly embarrassed. "Caught it on an iron hasp loading ore carts in Nashkel. Four days back. Hedda wrapped it."

"I wrapped it on a wagon bed," Hedda says. "Not the same thing."

The street door opens behind Sefa and lets in a breath of cold air and coal smoke. Hedda's eyes go straight to it. It's a carter, not the man she's waiting for, and her eyes go back to the fire.

"She does that every time," Tobin says, unwinding the cloth. "Venn's late every night, and she reckons if we're not first at him those two from Amn'll undercut us." He nods at nobody in particular.

The bandage comes away stiff and stained. Under it is a ragged cut across the heel of his left palm, about two inches long. Sefa has seen a hundred like it on the hospice benches. The edges are puffed and shiny red, and the skin around them is hot even before she touches it. A thin yellow crust has formed where the cut ought to have closed. There's no red line climbing toward the wrist yet. She's seen what usually follows when nobody cleans a cut like this, and it starts with that line.

Hedda looks at the hand and then at Sefa. She's a practical woman asking a practical question.

"What do you charge?"

 (Out of game: none of these four needed a roll. Bram's watch was routine for him and is told in the scene: the smith walked out the street door alone and the grey coat stayed. His question to Pell, Sefa's news from the board and her payment to Orla stand as the players wrote them.)
````

## Round 4

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 4 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram finishes the stew and mops the bowl clean with the last of the bread. He's still watching the street door where the smith went out.

"He'll be back in that coat tomorrow, at the next forge down," he says quietly to Pell. "Man doesn't pocket a no like that unless he's got plenty of smiths left to ask." He lets it go and turns his cup again.

"Sefa's holes. Deep, no spoil heap, sheep gone." He frowns at the table. "I've seen that once, down near Nashkel. Farmer lost a cow, then his boy nearly went the same way. Ankheg. Big burrowing thing, all shell and mandibles. It spits acid. It doesn't throw the earth up, it packs it and swallows it. If that's what's in the south fields, nobody walks them at dusk. And it's worth more than whatever the tanner's offering." He shrugs. "Might be nothing. Might be a big badger with ambitions."

He glances over at the hearth, where the lad is unwinding his bandage for Sefa, and watches how the cropped-haired woman takes it: wary, grateful, or neither.

"She's quick," he says to Pell, nodding toward Sefa. "Half an hour in town and she's got a wound out of a stranger. That's how you get a story out of a sellsword." A beat. "You did well too, didn't knock over a single piece of furniture coming in."

He settles back against the wall and keeps his eyes on the door, waiting for the temple bell and Venn.

*(If Sefa looks at that hand and learns anything, Bram will listen when she comes back. And: does Bram get any clear look at the wound from here — a blade cut, a bite, a burn?)*

Pell Dunmore: Pell pulls the bowl toward him, breaks the bread in half, and sets one piece on the table by the others in case anyone wants it. He eats without hurry, keeping his eyes moving the way he's done since he came in: doorway, hearth, the corner table, the shutters, back.

At Bram's aside about the ore he gives a short, slight nod. "A man with ore that isn't Nashkel's, selling it quiet to a smith who won't take his purse." His voice is low, almost idle. "Where I'm from, that's the start of a story, not the middle. But you're right—it's not ours."

He takes a longer drink this time and sets the cup down nearly empty. "I'll take the loft. Five silver, and I've slept in worse company than carters. Lot better than some company I've known." He glances at the room. "I'd rather keep a little silver back until I know if Venn's paying or just complaining."

His attention drifts once to the hearth where Sefa sits with the two strangers, then back. He doesn't stare. After a pause he says, "If the boy's hand's got a story on it, she'll come back with it. She's got that look—asks with her hands before she asks with her mouth."

He works at the stew, sopping the last of the broth with bread, then sits back and listens to the hammers gone quiet outside.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram's spoon scrapes the bottom of the bowl, and the bread takes care of the rest. Across the table Pell eats at his own pace. The half-loaf he set out sits by the jug, and nobody has touched it yet.

From the corner table the hearth is thirty feet off through elbows, pipe smoke and carters' backs. Bram can't make out much. The lad is holding his left hand palm-up to the firelight. The bandage hangs off his wrist, stiff and brown-yellow, the way cloth goes after a few days on a wound. Even from here the heel of the hand looks swollen, red against the pale of the rest. Whether it's a cut, a burn or a bite he can't tell at this distance. It's a hand that has gone bad, and the lad is holding it the way a man holds something that hurts. The cropped-haired woman doesn't look at the wound at all. She's watching Sefa's hands, arms folded, and her face gives nothing away yet.

In the far corner the grey coat is still on his second cup. He drinks slowly and watches nobody in particular. When the serving girl passes he says something to her. She shakes her head, and he lets her go without fuss.

At the end of the long bench the lone carter lifts his head at the word *ankheg*. He looks over at Bram for a moment with red-rimmed eyes, as if he means to say something. Then he goes back to his cup. A big badger with ambitions draws a snort out of the man beside him, a smith's boy with burnt eyebrows, and nothing more.

Orla comes through the crowd with an armful of empty bowls stacked to her chin. She stops at the corner long enough to look at the two clean ones.

"Loft, then," she says to Pell. She heard him from three benches off without seeming to listen. "Five for the bed, three for the stew. Eight." She tips her chin at Bram's bowl, wiped so clean it hardly needs washing. "And you're eleven, door and supper. You've both eaten, so I'll take it now, before the bell. Once Venn's in, I won't see the floor till midnight."

She waits, the bowls balanced on her forearm.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Pell pulls his bowl toward him, breaks the bread in half and sets one piece on the table by the jug in case anyone wants it. He eats without hurry. His eyes keep moving: doorway, hearth, the corner table, the shutters, and back.

At your aside about the ore he gives a short, slight nod. "A man with ore that isn't Nashkel's, selling it quiet to a smith who won't take his purse," he says, his voice low, almost idle. "Where I'm from, that's the start of a story, not the middle. But you're right—it's not ours."

He takes a longer drink and sets the cup down nearly empty. "I'll take the loft. Five silver, and I've slept in worse company than carters. Lot better than some company I've known." He glances at the room. "I'd rather keep a little silver back until I know if Venn's paying or just complaining."

His eyes go once to the hearth where Sefa sits with the two strangers, then come back. He doesn't stare. After a pause he says, "If the boy's hand's got a story on it, she'll come back with it. She's got that look—asks with her hands before she asks with her mouth."

He works at the stew, sops up the last of the broth with bread, then sits back.

Your spoon scrapes the bottom of the bowl, and the bread takes care of the rest. Across the table Pell eats at his own pace. The half-loaf he set out sits by the jug, and nobody has touched it yet.

From the corner table the hearth is thirty feet off through elbows, pipe smoke and carters' backs, and you can't make out much. The lad is holding his left hand palm-up to the firelight. The bandage hangs off his wrist, stiff and brown-yellow, the way cloth goes after a few days on a wound. Even from here the heel of the hand looks swollen, red against the pale of the rest. At this distance you can't tell whether it's a cut, a burn or a bite. It's a hand that has gone bad, and the lad is holding it the way a man holds something that hurts. The cropped-haired woman doesn't look at the wound at all. She's watching Sefa's hands, arms folded, and her face gives nothing away yet.

In the far corner the grey coat is still on his second cup. He drinks slowly and watches nobody in particular. When the serving girl passes he says something to her. She shakes her head, and he lets her go without fuss.

At the end of the long bench the lone carter lifts his head at the word *ankheg*. He looks over at you for a moment with red-rimmed eyes, as if he means to say something. Then he goes back to his cup. Your big badger with ambitions draws a snort out of the man beside him, a smith's boy with burnt eyebrows, and nothing more.

Orla comes through the crowd with an armful of empty bowls stacked to her chin. She stops at the corner long enough to look at the two clean ones.

"Loft, then," she says to Pell. She heard him from three benches off without seeming to listen. "Five for the bed, three for the stew. Eight." She tips her chin at your bowl, wiped so clean it hardly needs washing. "And you're eleven, door and supper. You've both eaten, so I'll take it now, before the bell. Once Venn's in, I won't see the floor till midnight."

She waits, the bowls balanced on her forearm.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram finishes his stew and mops the bowl clean with the last of the bread. His eyes stay on the street door the smith went out by.

"He'll be back in that coat tomorrow, at the next forge down," he says quietly to you. "Man doesn't pocket a no like that unless he's got plenty of smiths left to ask." He lets it go and turns his cup again.

"Sefa's holes. Deep, no spoil heap, sheep gone." He frowns at the table. "I've seen that once, down near Nashkel. Farmer lost a cow, then his boy nearly went the same way. Ankheg. Big burrowing thing, all shell and mandibles. It spits acid. It doesn't throw the earth up, it packs it and swallows it. If that's what's in the south fields, nobody walks them at dusk. And it's worth more than whatever the tanner's offering." He shrugs. "Might be nothing. Might be a big badger with ambitions."

He glances over at the hearth, where the lad is unwinding his bandage for Sefa, and looks there a while.

"She's quick," he says to you, nodding toward Sefa. "Half an hour in town and she's got a wound out of a stranger. That's how you get a story out of a sellsword." A beat. "You did well too, didn't knock over a single piece of furniture coming in."

He settles back against the wall and keeps his eyes on the door.

Bram's spoon scrapes the bottom of the bowl, and the bread takes care of the rest. You eat at your own pace. The half-loaf you set out sits by the jug, and nobody has touched it yet.

In the far corner the grey coat is still on his second cup. He drinks slowly and watches nobody in particular. When the serving girl passes he says something to her. She shakes her head, and he lets her go without fuss.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 4 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “Nothing for looking. You were sensible to wrap it, but it needs more attention now.”

I crouch beside Tobin’s chair rather than looming over him. “May I? Tell me if the tenderness goes beyond the cut, or if you’ve had chills.”

If he agrees, I gently examine the hand without squeezing the swollen edges, checking how far the heat and redness extend and whether he can move his fingers.

I catch Orla’s eye when she next passes. “Could I have some clean water and a clean cloth? For this hand—tell me what I owe you.”

To Hedda, quietly: “It’s good you made him show me. Let me clean it and see what it needs before we wrap it again.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tobin looks at Hedda first. She gives him the smallest nod, and he holds the hand out to Sefa, palm up.

"Doesn't hurt past the cut," he says. "Not really. It's nothing. Throbs a bit at night, that's all."

"He shook last night," Hedda says flatly. "Under two blankets. Said it was the wagon."

"It *was* the wagon. That bed's all draughts."

Sefa doesn't need long; she's done this on the hospice benches more times than she could count. The heat runs about a finger's width out from the cut into the meat of the palm and stops there. It doesn't reach the wrist or the back of the hand. When she asks him to spread his fingers he can do it. When she asks for a fist he gets three-quarters of the way and stops, jaw tight, because closing the hand pulls on the wound. Near the middle of the cut, under the yellow crust, there's a spot that gives under her fingertip, soft and full. Something has collected under there with nowhere to go. A wash and a fresh bandage won't be enough for that. It has to be opened and drained, or it will keep going bad from the inside.

Orla comes past the hearth on her way to the kitchen with bowls stacked up her forearm. Sefa catches her eye.

"Water's free, there's always a kettle on," Orla says without slowing down. "Clean rag's a copper. It goes on your tab, and if it comes back clean I'll forget it." She's through the kitchen door before Sefa can answer. A minute later the serving girl brings a chipped basin of hot water and a folded square of linen and sets them on the hearthstone at Sefa's knee.

When Sefa says it was good she made him show her, Hedda doesn't thank her. She watches the steam come off the basin.

"He needs two hands for the road," she says. "What does it need?"

Tobin keeps his hand out on his knee, palm up, and looks at the floor.
````

## Round 5

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram digs out a gold piece and a silver and sets them in Orla's free hand.

"Eleven. Supper was worth it." Before she can go he adds, low: "The carter at the end of the bench. Wet cuff, three cups down. Who is he? He looked at me like he knew the word *ankheg*."

Whatever she answers, he waits until she's moved on. Then he picks up the jug, still half full, and Pell's spare bread, and nods to Pell.

"Keep my seat."

He works his way down the long bench and sets the jug and bread in front of the lone carter. He doesn't sit until the man looks up.

"Bram Halloway. Came up from Nashkel today." He keeps his voice easy, not loud. "You looked over when I said ankheg. If you've seen one, or seen what it leaves, I'd like to hear about it. Jug's yours either way."

He lowers himself onto the end of the bench, half-turned so he can still see the street door and the hearth, and waits. He doesn't push.

*(Bram's watching the carter's face and hands as he talks. Is he scared, grieving, or just drunk? And is that cuff wet with water, or with something else?)*

Pell Dunmore: Pell fishes in his pouch, counts out eight silver onto the table, and slides them toward Orla with two fingers. "Stew and loft. Put me somewhere I can hear the stairs, and I'll sleep better."

He sits back, cup in hand but not drinking from it now, and says to Bram in a lower voice, "Ankheg. If that's what's burrowing the south fields, it explains the tanner's offer and a few missing sheep. Also means the fields are worth more than the notice says—if a man's got the stomach for acid and a shell."

He glances toward the hearth again, then the door. "So we wait for Venn. What's this one like when he's not drunk in a tavern—have you heard of him? A wool and salt man with two loads gone and a purse he's guarding. That's a man who'll bargain hard and then ask for twice what he's paying."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla shifts the stack of bowls up her forearm and takes Bram's gold and silver with her free hand. The coins go into the apron uncounted.

"Square," she says. She follows his look down the long bench, and her face softens a little. "Col Fenner. He carts hides for Ruddock the tanner and lives out on the south lane, past the last forge. Harmless. Two cups is his usual. Something's knocked him tonight." She turns to Pell before Bram can say more. "Loft's right over the stairs. You'll hear every boot that comes up them, and wish you didn't."

She waits a moment for Pell's coins, the bowls swaying.

---

Bram gets to his feet with the half-full jug in one hand and Pell's spare bread in the other. Pell's low question about Venn follows him as he goes. The bench is packed, and he has to turn sideways past two carters arguing about axle grease.

Col Fenner doesn't look up when the jug lands in front of him. He looks up when the bread does. He's somewhere past fifty, weathered the colour of the hides he hauls, and his eyes are red the way a man's go after he's been crying, not drinking. He's drunk, but not as drunk as the three empties suggest. His hands are around the cup, and the left one is the one Bram noticed from across the room. The cuff of that sleeve is soaked to the elbow and has gone dark. Up close it smells of lye soap, strong enough to cut through the ale. Above the cuff the skin of his wrist and forearm is red and raw-looking, angry and shiny. It's like a scald, but there's no blister. He has scrubbed that arm hard, and not long ago.

He hears Bram out, then looks at the jug for a while.

"Not seen it," he says finally. His voice is thick and plain, south-country. "Seen where it went." He turns the red wrist over on the table without seeming to notice he's doing it. "My dog. Bess. Old bitch, half blind. This afternoon she went down a hole by Ruddock's lower pasture after something. That hole weren't there yesterday, I'd swear to it. Went in and didn't yelp. Just went." He swallows. "I got down on my belly and put my arm in to the shoulder. Couldn't find bottom. The sides in there are smooth as the inside of a jug, packed hard. And it come out stinging. Like I'd put it in a vinegar vat. I scrubbed it at Ruddock's pump till I couldn't feel it."

He pulls the jug toward him. He doesn't pour.

"That word you said. That thing." He looks straight at Bram for the first time. "Does it come up for people? I've a girl. Eleven. She walks that lane to the Thunderhammer every morning with her brother's dinner."

*(Out of game: Pell's purse holds only six silver, so the other two would have to come from copper or a gold piece. Which does he give Orla?)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “There’s infection trapped beneath the cut. Cleaning the surface won’t reach it, and the shaking worries me.”

I look up at Tobin. “I can offer healing magic, if you’re willing. No charge. You don’t have to let this get worse to prove you can work.”

With his permission, I wash my hands and gently clean away the loose grime around the wound. Then I draw the Book of Shadows into my lap and rest my fingertips against his wrist.

“Lantern, let this hurt go no further.”

I cast **Lesser Restoration**, seeking to cleanse the infection. Then I spend **one die of Healing Light** to help mend the injured hand.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tobin looks at Hedda before he answers. He always does.

Hedda doesn't look at him. She looks at the book in Sefa's lap, then at Sefa's face, the same slow look she gave her boots.

"No charge," she says. "Then it goes on some other tab." It isn't quite a question. When Sefa doesn't take it back, she makes up her mind. "Tob. Let her."

"It's—" Tobin stops himself and holds the hand out properly, palm up on his knee.

Sefa washes her hands in the chipped basin and works the grime off with the corner of the linen, careful to stay clear of the swollen edges. Tobin hisses once through his teeth and then keeps still. She opens the Book of Shadows on her lap. The pages are warm under her fingers, and she lays two fingertips against the inside of his wrist, where the pulse runs too fast.

*Lantern, let this hurt go no further.*

It answers the way it always does, like a wick taking the flame somewhere just behind her eyes. The light doesn't show in the room, but it moves through her hand into his. Under her fingers the fever lets go of him. The heat drains back out of the meat of his palm. The puffed, shiny skin round the cut loses its angry colour and goes the dull pink of a wound that is only a wound. The soft, full pocket under the crust slackens as she watches. Whatever was gathering in there has nothing left to feed on.

Then she lets one measure of the Lantern's warmth go after it, gentler this time.

The light closes the cut most of the way. The ragged edges draw together and seal into a raised pink seam across the heel of his palm, scabbed over at the middle where it was deepest. It's no longer an open wound. It will be tender for a few days and leave a scar, but it is mending, and it's clean.

Tobin stares at it. He closes his hand slowly, all the way this time, and opens it again.

"Oh," he says. Then, louder than he means to: "*Oh.* Hedda, look—"

"I'm looking."

A couple of carters on the nearest bench have turned round on their seats. One of them makes a small sign against his chest and goes back to his cup a little too quickly. A smith's wife by the fire just watches with her mouth pressed shut. Nobody says anything. In Beregost the healing comes from the temple after dawn prayers. It doesn't come from a stranger with a book by the hearth.

Hedda takes Tobin's wrist and turns his hand to the firelight, the way Sefa did. She presses her thumb on the seam. He doesn't flinch. She lets go.

"That's not nothing," she says. She keeps her eyes on Sefa. "Whose light is that?"

Somewhere up the street, the bell at the Song of the Morning begins to ring for the evening. Hedda's head turns to the street door at the first stroke, and stays there.

 (Out of game: Lesser Restoration needs no roll. It just works: it ended Tobin's infection and fever, as the scene showed, and it used one of Sefa's two pact slots.)
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**


## Round 6

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't soften it, but he keeps his voice low and steady.

"Yes. It'll take a man if it's hungry enough. A child sooner." He lets that sit a moment, then goes on before Col can sink into it. "But they hunt like a spider. They wait under soft ground and come up at whatever walks over them. They don't go roaming the lanes looking. So keep her off that lane. Tomorrow, and every day until it's dealt with. Send the dinner another way, or walk with her on the hard road where it's cobbled or packed, away from the pasture edge. Nobody stands still on soft ground near those holes. Keep the stock penned up near the house."

He nods at the raw forearm.

"That's its spit, what's left of it on the walls of the tunnel. You got off light. Don't put that arm in anything again." He tips his head back toward the hearth. "The woman by the fire is a healer. When she's done with the lad, let her look at it. Tell her I sent you."

He pours Col a cup from the jug himself and pushes it over.

"I'm sorry about Bess." A pause. "Where's the hole, exactly? Ruddock's lower pasture. How far off the lane, and is there water near it, a ditch or a stream? And what's Ruddock offering on that notice?"

He listens to all of it, then puts it plain:

"I've a merchant to hear tonight. In the morning, in daylight, I'll walk out and look at it. Not down it, at it. I'll tell you what I see. I'm making no promises past that until I know what's in the ground." He gets up and puts a hand briefly on the man's shoulder. "Keep your girl home in the morning. That much you can do tonight."

On his way back to the corner table he stops by the hearth, just long enough to say to Sefa, quietly, "When you're done here, the carter at the end of the bench. Col. Acid burn on his left forearm, scrubbed raw with lye. Lost his dog down one of those holes today." Then he goes back to his seat against the wall.

As he sits he tells Pell, low: "Ankheg, near enough sure. Took a dog this afternoon, by the tanner's lower pasture. The man's got a girl who walks that lane." He picks up his cup. "Venn first. Then we'll see which pays and which needs doing."

*(Bram watches the door for Venn and the temple bell. He'll keep half an eye on the grey coat too, in case he moves.)*

Pell Dunmore: Pell counts out six silver, then digs two copper from the bottom of the pouch and adds them to the pile, then slides the lot toward Orla with the same quiet motion. "Eight. Keep the change in your pocket and not in my throat."

He waits until she's taken the coins, then settles back. His hand rests near his cup, but his face is easy, a man with nowhere to be until the bell. As Bram moves off down the bench toward Col Fenner, Pell lets his eyes follow without turning his head. He watches the room in the way of a man who has learned to count exits and cups both.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Col Fenner goes very still when Bram tells him yes. He looks at the cup in front of him for a long while, the way he looks at everything before he answers. While Bram explains how the thing hunts, Col keeps his eyes on the table, but he's listening to every word.

"Hard road," he says at last. "Coast Way, then. It's longer. She'll grumble." He pulls the poured cup toward him and holds it without drinking. "She'll go."

He looks down at his forearm when Bram nods at it, as if he's only now noticing the raw shine of it. Then he looks past Bram's shoulder at the hearth, where the lad is still holding his healed hand up to the firelight and turning it over. Col has heard the carters muttering on the near bench.

"Saw what she done for the boy," he says. That's all he says about it.

On the hole he's exact. He's walked that pasture every working day for years.

"Lower pasture runs down off the lane to Ruddock's ditch. That's where he lets the pit water off from the yard. Hole's down the bottom end, near the ditch. Forty paces off the lane, maybe." That's about a hundred and twenty feet. "Ground's always soft down there. Wet. Sheep won't graze it, mostly, on account of the smell off the ditch." He turns the cup a quarter round. "That paper of his don't offer nothing. It's a warning, is all. He's lost two ewes and he's sore about it. He'd pay to be shut of it, I reckon. Not much. He's at the yard from first light. Ask him."

When Bram's hand comes down on his shoulder, Col looks up and meets his eyes properly. He holds the look a moment.

"Morning, then," he says. "I'll keep her in."

---

Bram stops at the hearth on his way back, just long enough to say his piece to Sefa, quiet enough that it goes no further than her. Hedda doesn't look round. Her eyes are on the street door.

---

Back at the corner, Orla is still standing with the bowls on her forearm when Pell's coins slide across to her. She takes them with her free hand, the same as always, and is halfway to dropping them in the apron when her thumb stops. She opens her palm and looks at it: six silver and two brown coppers.

"Six silver and two copper isn't eight," she says. There's no heat in it. "You're short one silver and eight copper. Eighteen copper, or a silver and eight. I'm not fussy which." She waits with her palm still open, the bowls swaying.

Pell watches Bram come back up the bench, and Bram drops onto the seat against the wall. He gives Pell the ankheg, the dog, the tanner's pasture and the girl in a few low words, then picks up his cup.

He's hardly drunk from it when the bell up at the Song of the Morning gives its last stroke. The street door opens.

A narrow-shouldered man of nearly sixty comes in out of the dusk, stamping coal grit off his boots. The boots have the white tide-marks that salt leaves on leather. He wears a good sheepskin-collared coat, old but cared for, and has a ledger tied with string tucked under one arm. He catches Orla's eye across the room over the heads of the crowd.

"Wine, Orla. The cheap one. I've not earned better." He has a Gate accent, tired and dry. Then he looks the room over the way a man counts sheep he's already paid for. His eyes go to the hearth and the two sellswords there, then on to the corner table and the round shield propped against the bench.

In the far corner the man in the grey coat lifts his head when the door opens. He looks at the newcomer for a moment, then goes back to his cup.

By the fire, Hedda's head has already turned toward the door, and she keeps it there.

*(Out of game: two copper isn't two silver, so Pell still owes Orla eighteen copper; he has fifty-three copper and gold left in his purse.)*
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore, Sefa Marrin]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “I call it the Lantern.” I close the book. “It answered me during a fever outbreak in the Gate. I don’t have a grander name to give you.”

I give Tobin room to test his hand, then look back to Hedda.

“And there’s no other tab with me. You don’t owe me service, or a share of your wages.”

I fold the used linen beside the basin and stand.

“My tablemates and I mean to hear Venn’s offer too. Bram’s a caravan guard; he’s just come up from Nashkel. Have you two worked for Venn before? We’ve heard about the missing loads, but little else.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
"The Lantern," Hedda repeats, flat, the way she'd repeat a price she doesn't like. She takes a moment over it. "Not Lathander, then."

She doesn't ask anything more about it. Tobin has stopped listening anyway. He keeps closing his hand into a fist and opening it again, pressing his thumb along the pink seam as if he expects it to split.

"It doesn't even pull," he says. "Four days it's pulled. Hedda, it doesn't—" He catches her look and lowers the hand to his knee. "Thanks. Truly. I'd have said it was nothing right up till they took it off at the wrist."

When Sefa says there's no other tab, Hedda shakes her head once.

"That's yours to say. Doesn't mean I've stopped counting." She keeps her eyes on the stained linen Sefa has folded beside the basin.

When Sefa mentions Venn and the caravan guard from Nashkel, Hedda's attention changes. She turns her head and looks across the room at the man in the sheepskin collar. He has found a stool at the end of the bar, put the string-tied ledger down next to his wine and started undoing the knot. Then she looks back at Sefa, and this time she's taking her measure.

"Never worked for him," she says. "Never met him. We came up from Nashkel on the back of an ore cart, and his name was all over the market there."

"His salt," Tobin puts in. He can't help it. "The Amnian factor at the Nashkel market's been waiting on it since spring. Shouting about it in the street, he was. Said Venn's first load left the Gate with four wagons and two guards, and the second had six and five. Neither came in. That's when folk started saying it's not bandits, because you don't lose five guards to bandits without one of them running back to tell it." He nods at nobody in particular. "That's what they said, anyhow. We only heard it."

"We heard it," Hedda says, to close the subject. Then she gets up off the hearth bench, slow and stiff from sitting, and stands half a head taller than Sefa.

"Your three and my two, all bidding for the same wagons? He'll play us off against each other and pay the cheapest." She tips her chin at the bar, where Venn is wetting his thumb to turn a page. "So we don't undercut each other. That's the tab. Agreed?"

Tobin is already on his feet behind her, flexing his good new hand.
````

Together after: [Sefa Marrin]

## Round 7

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram glances at the coins in Orla's palm and then at Pell, eyebrows up. *Short?* He says nothing about it, though. That's Pell's to settle. He gets to his feet as the old man in the sheepskin collar looks over at the shield.

He doesn't wave. He just meets Venn's eye across the room and nods at the empty end of the bench beside him, the way you'd show a man where the wagon's parked.

When Venn comes over, Bram stays standing until he's sat.

"Master Venn. Bram Halloway. Orla says you're hiring." He sits back down against the wall. "Sixteen years on the Trade Way. Six with a free company, ten on caravans between the Gate and Amn. Last one paid me off in Nashkel. I came up the road today." He nods at Pell, and toward the hearth where Sefa is. "Those two might be interested as well. I'll let them speak for themselves."

He leaves a beat for Venn's wine to arrive, then gets to it. His voice is plain, the way a guard talks about work.

"Before we talk price, tell me about the two loads. How many wagons each, how many guards, and who were they? Where were they last seen for certain? A waystation, a farm, someone who waved them past? Did anyone go out along the road looking afterward? And did anything else go missing with them? Oxen, drivers, the wagons themselves?"

He turns his cup.

"I walked that stretch today and saw nothing. No wreck, no burned wagon, no birds circling. If two loads vanished on it, they didn't do it on the open road in daylight. So either they left the road, or someone took them off it. I'd like to know which before I sign to walk the third one into the same place."

*(While Venn answers, Bram keeps half an eye on Hedda by the fire. Does she know Venn, or is she scared of him? And does the grey coat in the corner show any interest in this conversation?)*

Sefa Marrin: “Agreed for my part—not as payment for his hand. It’s sensible on its own. But Bram and Pell speak for themselves. Give me a moment to ask them.”

I return to our table, leaning in so we needn’t raise our voices.

“Tobin’s hand is mending. They’ve never worked for Venn; what they know of the missing wagons is market talk. Four wagons and two guards on the first load, six and five on the second, they heard.”

I nod toward the bar. “Venn’s here. Hedda proposes we don’t undercut one another. I’ve agreed for myself, provided you two are willing. We can hear his terms together without committing to take the road.”

I glance at my unfinished stew. “And I’d like to ask whether he’s hiring escorts—or people to find his missing guards. Those are different jobs.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell looks at the coins as if they've insulted him, then dips into his pouch again and counts out eighteen copper onto Orla's open palm with more care than he'd like to show. "Slept too long in the rain," he says, only half an excuse. "Count it twice. I don't owe strangers."

Once she's gone, he sits up a little and watches the newcomer at the door with the same mild, sidelong attention he gives every other man who pauses at a threshold.

"Narrow shoulders, good coat, salt-bleached boots and a ledger tied with string." He says it low to Bram. "That's your man." He turns his cup upright between two fingers and leaves it there. "He's already counted the room. Saw the shield. Saw the two at the fire. Now we see who he crosses to first."

Pell shifts his weight, not rising, but ready to. "If he sits with them, we stay here and finish the jug. If he comes this way, we bargain."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla looks at Pell's eighteen coppers and doesn't count them twice. She doesn't count them at all.

"Rain'll do that," she says. "Square." The coins go into the apron and she goes off into the crowd with her bowls.

Across the room, Venn has seen the big man with the shield get to his feet. He doesn't hurry. He finishes untying the string on his ledger, takes his cup from the serving girl and tastes the wine with a small grimace. Pell watches him the whole time. Venn's eyes go to the hearth again, then to the corner, as if he's weighing two lots.

Sefa comes back to the table before Venn decides. She keeps her voice low and gives Bram and Pell the hand, the market talk and Hedda's pact. She doesn't wait for an answer before she adds her own question: escort, or a search? Then Venn picks up his cup and ledger and starts across the room. He passes the hearth on the way and raises one finger to Hedda, the way a man asks someone to wait their turn. Then he keeps walking. Hedda folds her arms and sits back down on the hearth bench. She says something short to Tobin and keeps her eyes on Venn's back. Bram knows that look from a hundred hiring yards. It isn't fear. She's a woman who has lost her place in the line and means to get it back.

Venn sets his cup at the end of the bench and lowers himself onto it the way a man does after a day of sitting on wagon boards. Only then does Bram sit down.

He listens to all of it, Bram's sixteen years and the two names offered without a sales pitch. When Bram gets to *before we talk price*, one corner of Venn's mouth moves.

"Well. That's a first this week." He puts the ledger on the table and leaves his hand on it. "Since you ask properly. The first load was four wagons of salt, sixteen oxen and four drivers. Two guards: Dace Morrow and his sister's boy. Dace walked for me six years and never lost me so much as a sack. They left my yard here, behind the Thunderhammer, eight weeks back. The second was six wagons, twenty-four oxen, six drivers and five guards under a woman called Marta Rusk, hired out of the Friendly Arm and well spoken of there. I paid for five because I'd lost the first. I thought that was the lesson."

He drinks.

"Last seen for certain, both times, at Lark's Ford."

Bram knows the place. Everyone who has worked this road waters there: an inn yard at the ford, a day and a half south of Beregost, on the road he has just come up.

"Old Pennick keeps the yard. He counts every wheel that waters, because he charges by the wheel. He watched Dace go up the south bank before noon. Five weeks later he watched Rusk do the same. After that I can't find a soul who saw either of them." Venn turns the cup a little on the table. "After the first, I paid a lad from the Fist post to ride to Nashkel and back. Nothing. After the second I rode it myself, with the same lad and a better horse than I could afford. Nothing on the road. No wheel in a ditch, no dead ox, no birds. You're right about the birds. I looked for them as well."

He opens the ledger and takes out a folded scrap of paper from between the pages. It's soft from being unfolded too many times. He lays it in front of Bram.

"Rusk sent that back from the ford with a carter going north."

The writing is a guard's hand, blunt and square:

*Master Venn. At Lark's by midday, oxen sound. Ford road's cut up south of here, so a Fist patrol at the yard has put us on the old drove track west of Tallow Hill. They say it comes back to the Way below Kesh's and saves half a day. Nashkel in two. M. Rusk.*

Bram knows the drove track by sight from the road, as anyone who has worked this road would. It's a sheep road that goes round the west side of the hill, toward the coast and the dark line of the Cloakwood along the horizon. Nobody takes wagons on it.

"I asked the Fist here and the Fist in Nashkel," Venn says. "Neither of them had a patrol at Lark's Ford that week. Neither of them has a patrol south of here most weeks, by the look of their notice. And the ford road wasn't cut up when I rode it." He takes the paper back, folds it along its old creases and puts it back in the ledger. "So either somebody lied to Rusk, or Rusk lied to me. I'd sooner it was the first. I liked her. I rode the drove track too. It's grass and stone, and it had three weeks of rain on it by then. It comes back to the Way where she said it would. There was nothing on it but sheep."

"Anything else gone? All of it. Wagons, oxen, salt and seventeen men. Their families on the Gate road come and ask me where they are." He says it flatly, the way he'd read out a loss in the ledger. "The salt was worth less than the oxen, if you want the truth."

He looks at Bram, then at Pell, then at Sefa, who is still standing at the end of the table.

"I'm hiring to get the third load to Nashkel. That's the job. If whoever walks it finds out on the way where the other two went, I'll pay for that as well. What it's worth, we'd argue about." He picks up his cup again. "The wagons go when I have guards. So. What does sixteen years cost? And what do these two cost on top of it?"

In the far corner the man in the grey coat hasn't turned his head once. He's on his second cup, with his back half to the room. He cleans a thumbnail with the point of a small knife, like a man with nowhere to be.
````

````text
In the far corner the man in the grey coat hasn't turned his head once. He's on his second cup, with his back half to the room. He cleans a thumbnail with the point of a small knife, like a man with nowhere to be.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
When you come back to the table, the big man, Bram, is on his feet by the wall. Pell sits beside him, cup upright between two fingers. Across the room, Venn has finished untying the string on his ledger and is tasting his wine with a small grimace. His eyes go to the hearth, then to the corner, as if he's weighing two lots.

You give Bram and Pell your news. Then Venn picks up his cup and ledger and starts across the room. He passes the hearth on the way and raises one finger to Hedda, the way a man asks someone to wait their turn. Then he keeps walking. Hedda folds her arms and sits back down on the hearth bench. She says something short to Tobin and keeps her eyes on Venn's back.

Venn sets his cup at the end of the bench and lowers himself onto it the way a man does after a day on wagon boards. Only then does Bram sit down, back against the wall.

"Master Venn. Bram Halloway. Orla says you're hiring," Bram says. "Sixteen years on the Trade Way. Six with a free company, ten on caravans between the Gate and Amn. Last one paid me off in Nashkel. I came up the road today." He nods at Pell, and at you. "Those two might be interested as well. I'll let them speak for themselves."

He gives Venn a moment with his wine, then gets to it, plainly, the way a guard talks about work. "Before we talk price, tell me about the two loads. How many wagons each, how many guards, and who were they? Where were they last seen for certain? A waystation, a farm, someone who waved them past? Did anyone go out along the road looking afterward? And did anything else go missing with them? Oxen, drivers, the wagons themselves?" He turns his cup. "I walked that stretch today and saw nothing. No wreck, no burned wagon, no birds circling. If two loads vanished on it, they didn't do it on the open road in daylight. So either they left the road, or someone took them off it. I'd like to know which before I sign to walk the third one into the same place."

One corner of Venn's mouth moves.

"Well. That's a first this week." He puts the ledger on the table and leaves his hand on it. "Since you ask properly. The first load was four wagons of salt, sixteen oxen and four drivers. Two guards: Dace Morrow and his sister's boy. Dace walked for me six years and never lost me so much as a sack. They left my yard here, behind the Thunderhammer, eight weeks back. The second was six wagons, twenty-four oxen, six drivers and five guards under a woman called Marta Rusk, hired out of the Friendly Arm and well spoken of there. I paid for five because I'd lost the first. I thought that was the lesson."

He drinks.

"Last seen for certain, both times, at Lark's Ford. Old Pennick keeps the yard. He counts every wheel that waters, because he charges by the wheel. He watched Dace go up the south bank before noon. Five weeks later he watched Rusk do the same. After that I can't find a soul who saw either of them." Venn turns the cup a little on the table. "After the first, I paid a lad from the Fist post to ride to Nashkel and back. Nothing. After the second I rode it myself, with the same lad and a better horse than I could afford. Nothing on the road. No wheel in a ditch, no dead ox, no birds. You're right about the birds. I looked for them as well."

He opens the ledger, takes a folded scrap of paper from between the pages, soft from being unfolded too many times, and lays it in front of Bram. "Rusk sent that back from the ford with a carter going north."

Bram reads it. "I asked the Fist here and the Fist in Nashkel," Venn says. "Neither of them had a patrol at Lark's Ford that week. Neither of them has a patrol south of here most weeks, by the look of their notice. And the ford road wasn't cut up when I rode it." He takes the paper back, folds it along its old creases and puts it back in the ledger. "So either somebody lied to Rusk, or Rusk lied to me. I'd sooner it was the first. I liked her. I rode the drove track too. It's grass and stone, and it had three weeks of rain on it by then. It comes back to the Way where she said it would. There was nothing on it but sheep."

"Anything else gone? All of it. Wagons, oxen, salt and seventeen men. Their families on the Gate road come and ask me where they are." He says it flatly, the way he'd read out a loss in the ledger. "The salt was worth less than the oxen, if you want the truth."

He looks at Bram, then at Pell, then at you, still standing at the end of the table.

"I'm hiring to get the third load to Nashkel. That's the job. If whoever walks it finds out on the way where the other two went, I'll pay for that as well. What it's worth, we'd argue about." He picks up his cup again. "The wagons go when I have guards. So. What does sixteen years cost? And what do these two cost on top of it?"

In the far corner the man in the grey coat hasn't turned his head once. He's on his second cup, with his back half to the room, cleaning a thumbnail with the point of a small knife, like a man with nowhere to be.
````

````text
"Rain'll do that," she says. "Square." The coins go into her apron and she goes off into the crowd with her bowls.
````
