Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 8

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't answer right away. He sits with the paper's words for a moment, turning his cup.

"A Fist patrol nobody sent," he says at last, quietly. "Standing in a yard where every carter on the road waters, telling a careful woman to take her wagons off the Way, toward the Cloakwood. That's not luck. Somebody went to the trouble of a tabard." He looks up at Venn. "And they knew Rusk would believe it, because Dace had already vanished and she'd want the safer road. Whoever it is knows this road and knows your business."

He sets the cup down.

"Price. Two gold a day for me, start to finish, back to Beregost included. I lead the guard and I say where the wagons go and where they stop. Two a day for each of these two, if they sign." He tips his head at Pell and Sefa. "Half the first week up front, so I know you're good for it. Whatever we learn about the other two loads, we argue over afterward, like you said."

He nods toward the hearth without looking.

"And hire those two as well. Hedda and the lad. Same terms, if they'll take them. Rusk had five and you'd sooner have too many than too few. A healer's worth more with enough blades in front of her that she isn't needed." A short, dry beat. "We've agreed between us not to cut each other's throats on price. I'd rather walk with them than past them."

He holds up a hand before Venn can speak.

"One more thing, and it isn't for sale. We stay on the Way. I don't care who's at Lark's Ford, in a Fist tabard or a priest's robe or your own coat. Nobody turns those wagons west. If someone tries, I'd very much like a word with him."

Then, plainer:

"When do the wagons go? I've promised a man I'll look at a hole by Ruddock's pasture at first light. That won't take long. And eight weeks back, then five. Has anyone else on this road lost a load in that time? Or is it only you somebody's watching?"

*(Bram watches Venn's face at the haggle, and at the question about whether he's the only one. Does he seem to be holding anything back?)*

Sefa Marrin: “Sefa Marrin. I can tend wounds and work spells, including at a distance. But before I name a price—Hedda and Tobin are interested too. We’ve agreed not to bid against each other.” I gesture toward the hearth, inviting them over.

“May I read Rusk’s message? I haven’t seen what she wrote about the patrol or the drove track.”

I take a seat, leaving room for the others.

“And I’d rather settle the payment for finding your missing people here, not argue over it on our return. Finding a wagon, bringing back survivors, and delivering this next load are different things. If we find someone alive who needs carrying home, I want us clear on what happens to the delivery.”

My voice softens a little. “Do you have the drivers’ names as well as the guards’? If we have to ask after them, names may get us further than a count of wagons.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets Bram work his side of the table and says nothing until he has heard Venn out. When the ledger closes and Venn asks what the three of them cost, Pell doesn't answer at once. He refills his cup only halfway from the jug and drinks with the air of a man to whom the number is not the interesting part.

"Before my price," he says, "I've a few of my own."

He sets the cup down and leans forward just enough to mean it. "The job's to walk a third load into the same stretch that swallowed the first two. Sixteen years on the road says nothing drowned those wagons by accident—and you've the look of a man who knows it." He lets that settle. "I'm not a shield. I don't stand on the box and frown at the treeline. I ride ahead, a little off the road, and I see trouble before it sees the wagons. I also see which of your drivers cut his own traces and which one lights a pipe where he's told not to. That's what I cost."

He ticks the air with one finger, not like a clerk, more like a man numbering loose boards. "Questions, then. First: who drives the third load? Were any of these six drivers new to you, or behind on their pay, or spoken for by somebody in Nashkel? Second: what does Lark's Ford look like now—has anyone been back since to watch it, or is it just the same old man counting wheels? And third, and this is the one that matters: the Fist post tried, and you tried, and nobody looked in the fields or the lanes between the ford and the Way. Nobody asked the farms."

He sits back. "Someone took two whole caravans off a road and left nothing. We both know that is not starved wolves. So I don't take the job as a guard. If I take it, I take it as a scout. And my number is higher than a guard's because I'll be the first to find whatever did it."

He glances at Bram and Sefa, just hard enough to include them, but he is still speaking to Venn. "Hear their prices. If we agree, you get three who aren't wasting your silver. If we don't, you can hire the two by the fire and hope the bad thing is short of breath."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Venn hears Bram out without interrupting. At *somebody went to the trouble of a tabard* he stops turning his cup and leaves it still.

When the price comes his face doesn't change, but his hand settles flat on the ledger cover, the way a man covers a purse in a crowd. Bram has watched merchants hear prices for sixteen years. This one isn't shocked by the number. He is working out whether he can pay it.

"Two a day is the going rate for a man who knows the road," Venn says. "I won't pretend otherwise. You lead, you say where the wagons stop." He pauses. "And staying on the Way is the one thing I'd have paid you extra for. You can have it free." The hand stays on the ledger. "Half the first week up front, though. That's five of you, if the two by the fire come in. Thirty-five gold before an ox has moved. I'll give you each two days' pay in the yard the morning we roll, and the rest at Nashkel, out of the factor's money. Better than that I haven't got. Not after this spring."

He answers the last questions in order, as if ticking them off.

"We go the day after tomorrow, at first light. The oxen come in off grazing tomorrow, so look at your hole." Then, more slowly: "Am I the only one? No. I've heard of three others, and I'd only stake money on one of them. Garrow, a wool man out of Nashkel. He sent two wagons north about when Rusk went south, and they didn't come in either. He found me in the Gate last month and asked whether I'd hired anyone strange. I asked him the same." His mouth twists. "We parted no wiser."

Bram can see no shifting and no hesitation. The only thing the man seems to be guarding is his purse.

---

When Sefa beckons, Hedda doesn't need asking twice. She crosses from the hearth with Tobin a step behind her and stands at the end of the bench. Venn looks up at her.

"Thank you for waiting," he says dryly. "I've not earned the courtesy."

"Two a day," Hedda says. "Same as him. Tob too."

"The lad's green."

"The lad's mended," Tobin says, and holds up the pink seam on his palm before he can stop himself. Then he puts the hand away, red to the ears.

Venn looks at the seam, then at Sefa, and says nothing about it. He takes Rusk's note out of the ledger again and holds it out to her. It's the same blunt, square hand Bram read: *At Lark's by midday, oxen sound... a Fist patrol at the yard... the old drove track west of Tallow Hill...* Up close Sefa can see the letters are even and unhurried, and nothing is crossed out. Whoever wrote it was sitting down and had time to choose her words. Maybe she was easy in her mind, or maybe she was careful.

To the question of finding his people, Venn gives the plainest answer he's given all evening.

"If you find any of mine alive, you bring them home, and the salt can sit in a ditch till I fetch it. The delivery doesn't come first. Five gold a head for each one you bring back alive. That's what the Fist offers for a bandit's head, and unlike the Fist I'll pay. Twenty more for knowing for certain where they went, with something I can show their families." He pauses. "That's my price for it, settled here, as you asked. You'll find I don't go back on a thing I've said in front of Orla's whole room."

He opens the ledger to a page near the back and turns it round on the table for her, keeping a finger on the corner. "Names. Copy them if you like. I'll not have the page torn out."

It's a list in a clerk's tidy hand, in two blocks with dates. *Dace Morrow. Jory Morrow.* Four drivers under them. Then *M. Rusk* and four guards, and six drivers. Two of those six have *Gate stockyards, hired on the day* written beside them in a different ink: *Harl Dobbs. Wick, no other name given.*

---

Venn turns to Pell last. He's plainly been saving him.

"Who drives the third load: Tam Hesketh, twenty years with me, and his two boys. Nobody else in Beregost will take my reins now, so I'll drive the fourth wagon myself." He taps the ledger page under Sefa's hand. "New men on the first load, none. On the second, those two. I was short, and the stockyards had men standing about. Nobody spoke for them and nobody asked after them since, which you may make of what you like. I've made several things of it, and none of them hold.

"The ford: nobody's watching it but Pennick, and Pennick watches wheels, not faces.

"The farms." His mouth tightens. "You've caught me there. I asked at one, Kesh's, where the drove track comes back down to the Way. Kesh swears nothing came down past his gate either time. Either the wagons left the track somewhere between the hill and his gate, or Kesh wasn't looking, or Kesh is lying to me. I rode on to Nashkel instead of finding out which. I was angry."

He sits back on the bench and looks at Pell properly for the first time. He looks the way he looked at the room when he came in, like a man counting sheep he's already paid for.

"A scout. Ahead, off the road, the first to find it." He picks up the cheap wine. "Higher than a guard's, you said. How much higher?"
````

A healer's worth more with enough blades in front of her that she isn't needed." A short, dry beat. "We've agreed between us not to cut each other's throats on price. I'd rather walk with them than past them."

````text
He holds up a hand before Venn can speak.

"One more thing, and it isn't for sale. We stay on the Way. I don't care who's at Lark's Ford, in a Fist tabard or a priest's robe or your own coat. Nobody turns those wagons west. If someone tries, I'd very much like a word with him."

Then, plainer:

"When do the wagons go? I've promised a man I'll look at a hole by Ruddock's pasture at first light. That won't take long. And eight weeks back, then five. Has anyone else on this road lost a load in that time? Or is it only you somebody's watching?"

Pell lets Bram work his side of the table and says nothing until Venn has been heard out. When Venn asks what the three of you cost, he refills his cup only halfway from the jug and drinks as if the number isn't the interesting part.

"Before my price," he says, "I've a few of my own."

He sets the cup down and leans forward just enough to mean it. "The job's to walk a third load into the same stretch that swallowed the first two. Sixteen years on the road says nothing drowned those wagons by accident—and you've the look of a man who knows it." He lets that settle. "I'm not a shield. I don't stand on the box and frown at the treeline. I ride ahead, a little off the road, and I see trouble before it sees the wagons. I also see which of your drivers cut his own traces and which one lights a pipe where he's told not to. That's what I cost."

He ticks the air with one finger, like a man numbering loose boards. "Questions, then. First: who drives the third load? Were any of these six drivers new to you, or behind on their pay, or spoken for by somebody in Nashkel? Second: what does Lark's Ford look like now—has anyone been back since to watch it, or is it just the same old man counting wheels? And third, and this is the one that matters: the Fist post tried, and you tried, and nobody looked in the fields or the lanes between the ford and the Way. Nobody asked the farms."

He sits back. "Someone took two whole caravans off a road and left nothing. We both know that is not starved wolves. So I don't take the job as a guard. If I take it, I take it as a scout. And my number is higher than a guard's because I'll be the first to find whatever did it."

He glances at you and Bram, just enough to include you both, though he's still speaking to Venn. "Hear their prices. If we agree, you get three who aren't wasting your silver. If we don't, you can hire the two by the fire and hope the bad thing is short of breath."

---

Venn hears Bram out without interrupting. At *somebody went to the trouble of a tabard* he stops turning his cup and leaves it still.

When the price comes his face doesn't change, but his hand settles flat on the ledger cover, the way a man covers a purse in a crowd.

"Two a day is the going rate for a man who knows the road," Venn says. "I won't pretend otherwise. You lead, you say where the wagons stop." He pauses. "And staying on the Way is the one thing I'd have paid you extra for. You can have it free." The hand stays on the ledger. "Half the first week up front, though. That's five of you, if the two by the fire come in. Thirty-five gold before an ox has moved. I'll give you each two days' pay in the yard the morning we roll, and the rest at Nashkel, out of the factor's money. Better than that I haven't got. Not after this spring."

He answers Bram's last questions in order, as if ticking them off.

"We go the day after tomorrow, at first light. The oxen come in off grazing tomorrow, so look at your hole." Then, more slowly: "Am I the only one? No. I've heard of three others, and I'd only stake money on one of them. Garrow, a wool man out of Nashkel. He sent two wagons north about when Rusk went south, and they didn't come in either. He found me in the Gate last month and asked whether I'd hired anyone strange. I asked him the same." His mouth twists. "We parted no wiser."

---

When you beckon, Hedda doesn't need asking twice. She crosses from the hearth with Tobin a step behind her and stands at the end of the bench. Venn looks up at her.

"Thank you for waiting," he says dryly. "I've not earned the courtesy."

"Two a day," Hedda says. "Same as him. Tob too."

"The lad's green."

"The lad's mended," Tobin says, and holds up the pink seam on his palm before he can stop himself. Then he puts the hand away, red to the ears.

Venn looks at the seam, then at you, and says nothing about it. He takes Rusk's note out of the ledger again and holds it out to you. It's the same blunt, square hand Bram read: *At Lark's by midday, oxen sound... a Fist patrol at the yard... the old drove track west of Tallow Hill...* Up close you can see the letters are even and unhurried, and nothing is crossed out. Whoever wrote it was sitting down and had time to choose her words. Maybe she was easy in her mind, or maybe she was careful.

To your question about finding his people, Venn gives the plainest answer he's given all evening.

"If you find any of mine alive, you bring them home, and the salt can sit in a ditch till I fetch it. The delivery doesn't come first. Five gold a head for each one you bring back alive. That's what the Fist offers for a bandit's head, and unlike the Fist I'll pay. Twenty more for knowing for certain where they went, with something I can show their families." He pauses. "That's my price for it, settled here, as you asked. You'll find I don't go back on a thing I've said in front of Orla's whole room."

He opens the ledger to a page near the back and turns it round on the table for you, keeping a finger on the corner. "Names. Copy them if you like. I'll not have the page torn out."

It's a list in a clerk's tidy hand, in two blocks with dates. *Dace Morrow. Jory Morrow.* Four drivers under them. Then *M. Rusk* and four guards, and six drivers. Two of those six have *Gate stockyards, hired on the day* written beside them in a different ink: *Harl Dobbs. Wick, no other name given.*

---

Venn turns to Pell last. He's plainly been saving him.

"Who drives the third load: Tam Hesketh, twenty years with me, and his two boys. Nobody else in Beregost will take my reins now, so I'll drive the fourth wagon myself." He taps the ledger page under your hand. "New men on the first load, none. On the second, those two. I was short, and the stockyards had men standing about. Nobody spoke for them and nobody asked after them since, which you may make of what you like. I've made several things of it, and none of them hold.

"The ford: nobody's watching it but Pennick, and Pennick watches wheels, not faces.

"The farms." His mouth tightens. "You've caught me there. I asked at one, Kesh's, where the drove track comes back down to the Way. Kesh swears nothing came down past his gate either time. Either the wagons left the track somewhere between the hill and his gate, or Kesh wasn't looking, or Kesh is lying to me. I rode on to Nashkel instead of finding out which. I was angry."

He sits back on the bench and looks at Pell properly for the first time, the way he looked at the room when he came in, like a man counting sheep he's already paid for.

"A scout. Ahead, off the road, the first to find it." He picks up the cheap wine. "Higher than a guard's, you said. How much higher?"
````

## Round 9

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram lets Pell's question hang. That number is Pell's to name, and Bram doesn't fill the silence for him. When it's settled, one way or the other, he nods to Venn.

"Two days in the yard, the rest at Nashkel from the factor. That'll do. You've said it in front of the room, and so have I." He puts his hand out across the table and shakes once, firmly.

Then he leans in, keeping his voice under the noise.

"A few things before we roll. First, keep the day quiet. You've told this room the day after tomorrow, and that can't be helped now. But don't tell anyone the hour or the route past Lark's Ford. Tell your drivers the morning we leave, not before." He turns his cup. "Somebody knew Rusk would be at that yard around midday. They knew it well enough to have a tabard waiting."

"Second, those two new men on Rusk's load. What did they look like? Ages, accents, anything. If they turn up alive somewhere, I'd like to know them before they know me."

"Third, Lark's Ford. Pennick watches wheels, fine. But a 'Fist patrol' standing in his yard would have watered horses and maybe paid him for it. We'll ask him how many there were, what they rode, which way they went after. A man who charges by the wheel remembers who didn't pay."

He glances at Pell, then back at Venn.

"So here's how I'd walk it. The wagons stay on the Way. Pell goes ahead from the ford and has a look at the drove track where it leaves the road, and again where it comes back down by Kesh's. If somebody's been turning loads off there, the ground between will show it, even after rain. Twenty-four oxen don't walk soft. And I'd like a word with Kesh myself, about his gate. Politely, the first time."

A beat, then dryly, to Venn:

"And Garrow's two went north while Rusk went south. Whoever this is takes from both directions. That's a lot of oxen to hide, and a lot of salt and wool to sell. Somebody's buying." He doesn't look at the grey coat in the corner, but he thinks about it.

He sits back against the wall.

"Hole at Ruddock's at first light. Then I'll come by your yard behind the Thunderhammer and look at the wagons and the axles before Tam's boys load them." To Hedda, with a short nod: "Glad to have you. If you're up early, I wouldn't say no to a second pair of eyes in that pasture."

Sefa Marrin: “Two gold a day suits me, with four paid when we leave. And thank you for being clear about the missing people.”

I read both blocks of names carefully, including the dates, then return the note and turn the ledger back toward Venn.

“One point to settle: Bram’s rate included the journey back to Beregost. Does payment at Nashkel cover that return as well? I don’t want us discovering we understood different things once we’re there.”

I leave Pell room to negotiate his own rate, then speak to the others.

“Tomorrow gives us time to get acquainted before we’re responsible for four wagons. Bram, I’ll come to the pasture with you, if you’d like company.”

To Venn: “Where should we find you tomorrow? I’d like to meet Hesketh and his boys before we leave. They should know whose voice to listen for if there’s trouble—and I’d rather learn their faces here than in a scramble on the road.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets the question sit for the space of a breath, then says it plainly, without any show.

"Three gold a day."

He doesn't reach for the wine, doesn't look to Bram or Sefa for leave. "A guard earns his two standing beside the wagons, with blades on either side and a fire at night. I'll be out ahead, on ground somebody's already used twice, with nobody to spend but myself. If I do the work right, you never see what I saw. If I do it wrong, you won't owe me tomorrow." A thin, mirthless smile. "So three. Same two days in hand as the others. And if we find your missing people or learn the where of it, I take my share of the twenty like the rest."

He leans back a fraction, still watching Venn's face the way a man reads a ledge for purchase. "I don't ask for a guard's wage when I'm the one who meets the thing first."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Venn hears Pell's number without blinking. He turns his cup a quarter round and sits with it for a moment.

"Three," he says. "Going down, where it happened, where you're out ahead and I'm paying you to find the thing first. That's fair, and I'll not pretend it isn't." He taps one finger on the ledger cover. "Coming back you'll be riding behind light wagons on a road we'll know by then. Two, the same as the others. And twenty is twenty. It's split among however many of you there are, not paid out once to each. I'm not paying it six times over." His mouth twists. "That's the best a man who's lost ten wagons can do for you."

He doesn't wait for Pell to take it. Bram is already reaching across the table, and Venn shakes his hand: a dry grip, short and firm, the way he'd seal a bill of sale.

"Two days in the yard, the rest from the factor. In front of the room." He glances along the benches, where the nearest carters are pretending not to listen. "They'll remember it better than I will."

When Bram leans in and lowers his voice, Venn leans in with him.

"The hour I'll keep. Tam hears it when he's on the box, not before. The route past Lark's is yours to choose, and I'll not ask you for it." He lets out a breath through his nose. "The day's out, as you say. I said it like a fool with Orla's whole room listening."

On the two stockyard men he thinks for a while before he answers.

"I saw them for one morning, in my yard, while Rusk loaded. Dobbs was a big man, forty or so, Gate-voiced. He knew oxen, and I'd guess that's why she took him. He yoked a pair of my worst without being told twice. Wick was younger and thin, a lad not much older than that one." He tips his head at Tobin. "He said nothing all morning. I took him for shy. I'd not know either of them in a crowd, and that's my fault, not theirs."

He grunts at the plan for the ford and the track.

"Pennick'll talk if you water there. He talks to anyone who pays by the wheel. And Kesh..." He stops himself. "Politely the first time. Yes. I wasn't polite."

At *somebody's buying*, his eyes go flat.

"Salt sells anywhere from here to Calimport. Nobody looks twice at it. Twenty-four oxen and Garrow's lot on top of them, though. Somebody's feeding those, or eating them."

Bram doesn't look at the far corner. He doesn't need to. The man in the grey coat has finished his cup at last. He sets a coin by it for the serving girl, gets up without hurrying and buttons his coat to the throat. He doesn't go to the street door. He goes to the stairs and climbs them at an even pace, one hand on the rail, and his boots knock on the boards overhead. He turns left at the top, toward the rooms with doors. Right over the stairs, in the loft, is where Pell will be sleeping.

---

Sefa turns the ledger back to Venn. He asks nothing about the names she has read, and he closes the book over them.

"Four gold in hand for you when we roll, the same as the rest." He answers her question about the return just as plainly. "The factor pays at Nashkel for the road down. That's his money, owed on delivery. The road back I pay in my own yard, the day we're home. I'll not pay a man in Nashkel for days he hasn't walked yet." A dry look. "Not because I think you'd run. Because I'd want the same if I were you, and I'd sooner hand it over where I can see the oxen."

To where he'll be: "My yard, behind the Thunderhammer, from the middle of the morning. The oxen come in off the grazing about then, and Tam and his boys with them. Come and see their faces, by all means. Tam won't say much to you. He never says much to anyone. But he'll remember you."

---

When Bram invites her to the pasture, Hedda looks at him with the same slow look she gave Sefa, from face to shield.

"First light," she says. She jerks a thumb over her shoulder. "Tob. Coming."

"I'll bring a pole," Tobin says at once. "A long one. For poking."

"You'll bring your feet. Nobody pokes anything."

Venn has heard all of it, and he looks round the table at them: five, with the scout. He moves his lips once, the way a man does a sum he doesn't like.

"Eleven gold a day going down." He picks up the cheap wine and finishes it. "I've had cheaper years. I've not earned a dearer one, but it seems I'm buying it."

Then he turns to Pell and waits. The three-and-two is on the table, and it's Pell's answer he's waiting for.

 (Out of game: none of Bram's actions this round needed a roll. His handshake, his requests to Venn and his invitation to Hedda were all talk, and the story answered each of them. Watching the grey coat go upstairs was a routine look for him.)
````

````text
Pell lets Venn's question sit for a breath, then says it plainly, without any show. "Three gold a day." He doesn't reach for the wine and doesn't look to you or Bram for leave. "A guard earns his two standing beside the wagons, with blades on either side and a fire at night. I'll be out ahead, on ground somebody's already used twice, with nobody to spend but myself. If I do the work right, you never see what I saw. If I do it wrong, you won't owe me tomorrow." A thin, mirthless smile. "So three. Same two days in hand as the others. And if we find your missing people or learn the where of it, I take my share of the twenty like the rest." He leans back a fraction, still watching Venn's face. "I don't ask for a guard's wage when I'm the one who meets the thing first."

Bram nods to Venn. "Two days in the yard, the rest at Nashkel from the factor. That'll do. You've said it in front of the room, and so have I." He puts his hand out across the table.

Then he leans in, keeping his voice under the noise of the room. "A few things before we roll. First, keep the day quiet. You've told this room the day after tomorrow, and that can't be helped now. But don't tell anyone the hour or the route past Lark's Ford. Tell your drivers the morning we leave, not before." He turns his cup. "Somebody knew Rusk would be at that yard around midday. They knew it well enough to have a tabard waiting."

"Second, those two new men on Rusk's load. What did they look like? Ages, accents, anything. If they turn up alive somewhere, I'd like to know them before they know me."

"Third, Lark's Ford. Pennick watches wheels, fine. But a 'Fist patrol' standing in his yard would have watered horses and maybe paid him for it. We'll ask him how many there were, what they rode, which way they went after. A man who charges by the wheel remembers who didn't pay."

He glances at Pell, then back at Venn. "So here's how I'd walk it. The wagons stay on the Way. Pell goes ahead from the ford and has a look at the drove track where it leaves the road, and again where it comes back down by Kesh's. If somebody's been turning loads off there, the ground between will show it, even after rain. Twenty-four oxen don't walk soft. And I'd like a word with Kesh myself, about his gate. Politely, the first time."

A beat, then dryly: "And Garrow's two went north while Rusk went south. Whoever this is takes from both directions. That's a lot of oxen to hide, and a lot of salt and wool to sell. Somebody's buying."

He sits back against the wall. "Hole at Ruddock's at first light. Then I'll come by your yard behind the Thunderhammer and look at the wagons and the axles before Tam's boys load them." To Hedda, with a short nod: "Glad to have you. If you're up early, I wouldn't say no to a second pair of eyes in that pasture."

Venn hears Pell's number without blinking. He turns his cup a quarter round and sits with it for a moment.

"Three," he says. "Going down, where it happened, where you're out ahead and I'm paying you to find the thing first. That's fair, and I'll not pretend it isn't." He taps one finger on the ledger cover. "Coming back you'll be riding behind light wagons on a road we'll know by then. Two, the same as the others. And twenty is twenty. It's split among however many of you there are, not paid out once to each. I'm not paying it six times over." His mouth twists. "That's the best a man who's lost ten wagons can do for you."

He doesn't wait for Pell to take it. Bram is already reaching across the table, and Venn shakes his hand: a dry grip, short and firm, the way he'd seal a bill of sale.

"Two days in the yard, the rest from the factor. In front of the room." He glances along the benches, where the nearest carters are pretending not to listen. "They'll remember it better than I will."

When Bram leans in and lowers his voice, Venn leans in with him.

"The hour I'll keep. Tam hears it when he's on the box, not before. The route past Lark's is yours to choose, and I'll not ask you for it." He lets out a breath through his nose. "The day's out, as you say. I said it like a fool with Orla's whole room listening."

On the two stockyard men he thinks for a while before he answers.

"I saw them for one morning, in my yard, while Rusk loaded. Dobbs was a big man, forty or so, Gate-voiced. He knew oxen, and I'd guess that's why she took him. He yoked a pair of my worst without being told twice. Wick was younger and thin, a lad not much older than that one." He tips his head at Tobin. "He said nothing all morning. I took him for shy. I'd not know either of them in a crowd, and that's my fault, not theirs."

He grunts at the plan for the ford and the track.

"Pennick'll talk if you water there. He talks to anyone who pays by the wheel. And Kesh..." He stops himself. "Politely the first time. Yes. I wasn't polite."

At *somebody's buying*, his eyes go flat.

"Salt sells anywhere from here to Calimport. Nobody looks twice at it. Twenty-four oxen and Garrow's lot on top of them, though. Somebody's feeding those, or eating them."

In the far corner, the man in the grey coat has finished his cup at last. He sets a coin by it for the serving girl, gets up without hurrying and buttons his coat to the throat. He doesn't go to the street door. He goes to the stairs and climbs them at an even pace, one hand on the rail, and his boots knock on the boards overhead. He turns left at the top, toward the rooms with doors. Right over the stairs, in the loft, is where Pell will be sleeping.

You turn the ledger back to Venn. He asks nothing about the names you have read, and he closes the book over them.

"Four gold in hand for you when we roll, the same as the rest." He answers your question about the return just as plainly. "The factor pays at Nashkel for the road down. That's his money, owed on delivery. The road back I pay in my own yard, the day we're home. I'll not pay a man in Nashkel for days he hasn't walked yet." A dry look. "Not because I think you'd run. Because I'd want the same if I were you, and I'd sooner hand it over where I can see the oxen."

To where he'll be: "My yard, behind the Thunderhammer, from the middle of the morning. The oxen come in off the grazing about then, and Tam and his boys with them. Come and see their faces, by all means. Tam won't say much to you. He never says much to anyone. But he'll remember you."

When Bram invites her to the pasture, Hedda looks at him with the same slow look she gave you, from face to shield.

"First light," she says. She jerks a thumb over her shoulder. "Tob. Coming."

"I'll bring a pole," Tobin says at once. "A long one. For poking."

"You'll bring your feet. Nobody pokes anything."

Venn has heard all of it, and he looks round the table at you: five, with the scout. He moves his lips once, the way a man does a sum he doesn't like.

"Eleven gold a day going down." He picks up the cheap wine and finishes it. "I've had cheaper years. I've not earned a dearer one, but it seems I'm buying it."

Then he turns to Pell and waits. The three-and-two is on the table, and it's Pell's answer he's waiting for.
````

## Round 10

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram waits out Pell's answer. He doesn't look at Pell while he does it. That bargain is Pell's.

Once it's settled, he turns to Tobin with the ghost of a smile.

"Bring the pole. Hedda's right that nobody pokes the hole. But a long pole tapped on the ground in front of you tells you where it's gone soft before your boot finds out." He nods to Hedda. "First light, at the lane by the last forge. We'll go down the pasture edge in a line, ten feet apart, slow, and nobody stands still. If the ground moves, everyone gets back to the lane. No heroics over a dog that's already gone."

Then, lower, to Sefa and Pell together, as the talk around the table breaks up:

"Our friend in the grey coat just went up to the rooms with doors. Left side." A short pause. "Ours are left side as well. Could be nothing. A man with a purse and a black stone needs a bed like anyone." He turns his cup over, finished. "Bar your door, Sefa. Pell, you're over the stairs. If you hear boots that don't sound like carters, I'd take it kindly if you knocked."

On the way to the stairs he stops at the bar when Orla passes and keeps his voice easy, like idle talk.

"The gentleman in the grey coat. Staying long? He was trying to sell my old trade back to me. Ore, from somewhere." He leaves it there. If she says something, he'll take it. If she doesn't, he won't press.

Then he goes up. He sets his shield by the bed and the longsword where his hand falls on it. He bars the door and checks the shutter. Before he lies down he stands a moment and listens to the corridor, to learn which door the grey coat went into.

*(Bram sleeps in his clothes, mail off and laid out where he can get into it fast. He'll wake before first light. Old habit.)*

Sefa Marrin: “That’s clear, then. I accept.” I offer Venn my hand. “We’ll see you in the yard tomorrow.”

I settle back to let Pell give his own answer and finish what’s left of my supper.

Once the business is concluded, I turn to Bram and Hedda. “What did Ruddock tell you about the pasture? The notice mentioned missing sheep and deep holes, but not whether anyone actually saw what made them. I’ll come at first light. Better to look together.”

Before going upstairs for the night, I pay Orla the copper for the linen and ask her to point out which room is mine.

Pell Dunmore: Pell gives a short nod before the silence can harden into something else.

"Done. Three down, two back, and the twenty split among all of us who earn it." He puts his hand out to Venn with none of the show he's put into his words. "I'll be in your yard at the middle of the morning to see the wagons and the drivers. And I'll walk them before you pay me to."

He releases the hand and lets his eyes move once to the stairs, to the place where the grey coat vanished toward the rooms and the loft.

Then, lower, to the table: "One more thing while we're all bought and paid for. The man in the grey coat who sat himself in the corner all evening, selling black ore and asking quiet questions nobody answered." He says it to Venn as much as to the others. "He left no better than five minutes before you walked in, and he went up the stairs, not out. The loft's over the stairs, and the rooms with doors are on the left. He turned left."

Pause. "Maybe he's only tired. But if I were moving a thing worth more than salt and wool, I'd want a room above the common room, where I could hear who's hiring and for how much." He shrugs, light. "Or he's a smith with a weak head. Either way, I'll sleep with my doorless loft and keep one ear on the floorboards."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Venn hears Pell out and takes the offered hand. It's the same short, dry grip he gave Bram.

"Three down, two back, and the twenty for whoever earns it." He lets go. "Walk them all you like. Tam'll think you're counting his spokes."

Sefa's hand comes next, and he shakes it as well. Then he ties the string round the ledger again, two turns and a knot, and pushes the empty wine cup away.

---

Tobin's face lights up when Bram gives him the pole back.

"There's a pike shaft in the Thunderhammer's scrap pile," he says. "No head on it. I'll ask for it."

"Ask," Hedda says. "Don't take." She hears out the rest of it: the lane by the last forge, ten feet apart, slow, back to the lane if the ground moves. She nods once. "First light. We'll be there."

When Sefa asks what Ruddock told them, Hedda shakes her head.

"Never met him. Only heard the name tonight." She tips her chin at Bram. "His man's the carter."

---

When Pell lowers his voice about the grey coat, Venn looks at the stairs. Only the stairs, and only for a moment.

"Never set eyes on him before tonight," he says. "But there's been a few like him at the forges since the thaw. Good coats, clean boots, wanting iron sold to them or smiths to work it. I'm salt and wool. They've no use for me." He gets up off the bench slowly, a hand on the table edge. "My yard, middle of the morning. Goodnight to you."

He goes out by the street door into the dark with the ledger under his arm.

---

Orla is behind the bar wiping out cups when Bram stops there. Sefa comes up a moment later and lays one copper on the wood.

"For the rag," she says.

Orla scoops it up. "Now you're square." When Sefa asks about her room, Orla points with the cloth at the ceiling. "Top of the stairs, turn left, go right to the end. Yours is the last door. His is the one before it." She means Bram. "Bars are on the inside. Use them."

She hears Bram's idle question with her hands still moving, and doesn't answer at once.

"Ansel Corby, he wrote in my book. This is his third night, paid through tomorrow. Tidy, pays ahead, doesn't break anything." She sets the cup down. "And there's three smiths drink in here who've told him no this week. You just watched the fourth. They don't say why. Not to me." She picks up the next cup. "That's all I know about him, and it's more than I'd tell most."

---

The stairs creak under Bram's weight. At the top a short corridor runs left under the eaves. There are four doors, and one rushlight sits in a dish on the floor at the far end. The ladder to the loft goes up out of the dark right over the stairhead.

Bram stands still and listens.

The door nearest the stairs is dark, and somebody behind it is snoring evenly. The second door has a line of lamplight under it. A man is walking in there, four steps, a turn, four steps back, the floorboards giving under him. Then a chair scrapes, and after that nothing moves. The last two doors are dark. Those are his and Sefa's.

So the grey coat's room is the one next to Bram's, with a plank wall between them.

Bram's room is narrow. It has a cot, a peg, a shutter on a hook and a bar in iron brackets. He props the shield by the bed and lays the longsword where his hand will find it. He drops the bar into its brackets and tries the shutter. He lays the mail out on the floor with the arms open, ready to shrug into. Through the wall the lamp next door is still burning.

Below, the common room is getting louder as the carters settle in for the night. Up in the loft over the stairs, Pell will hear every boot on those boards.

Nothing more happens before first light unless one of you wants to be awake for it.

*(Out of game: the grey coat went upstairs a few minutes ago, during the bargaining, after Venn had sat down, not before he came in. Venn saw him go as well.)*

 *(Out of game: all three of Bram's actions happened in the scene above, and none needed a roll. Asking Orla was plain talk, and she answered. Listening at the corridor was routine for an old guard, and it found Corby's door, the one next to Bram's. Barring the door and laying out his kit is just what he does every night on the road.)*
````

But if I were moving a thing worth more than salt and wool, I'd want a room above the common room, where I could hear who's hiring and for how much." A light shrug. "Or he's a smith with a weak head. Either way, I'll sleep with my doorless loft and keep one ear on the floorboards."

````text
You saw it the same way. The grey coat went up a few minutes ago, during the bargaining, after Venn had sat down. Venn saw him go too.

When Pell speaks, Venn looks at the stairs. Only the stairs, and only for a moment. "Never set eyes on him before tonight," he says. "But there's been a few like him at the forges since the thaw. Good coats, clean boots, wanting iron sold to them or smiths to work it. I'm salt and wool. They've no use for me." He gets up off the bench slowly, a hand on the table edge. "My yard, middle of the morning. Goodnight to you." He goes out by the street door into the dark with the ledger under his arm.

As the talk breaks up, Bram speaks low to you and Pell together. "Our friend in the grey coat just went up to the rooms with doors. Left side." A short pause. "Ours are left side as well. Could be nothing. A man with a purse and a black stone needs a bed like anyone." He turns his cup over, finished. "Bar your door, Sefa. Pell, you're over the stairs. If you hear boots that don't sound like carters, I'd take it kindly if you knocked."

Orla is behind the bar wiping out cups. Bram has stopped there. You come up a moment later and lay the copper on the wood. Orla scoops it up. "Now you're square." When you ask about your room, she points at the ceiling with the cloth. "Top of the stairs, turn left, go right to the end. Yours is the last door. His is the one before it." She means Bram. "Bars are on the inside. Use them."

Bram keeps his voice easy, like idle talk. "The gentleman in the grey coat. Staying long? He was trying to sell my old trade back to me. Ore, from somewhere."

Orla keeps her hands moving and doesn't answer at once. "Ansel Corby, he wrote in my book. This is his third night, paid through tomorrow. Tidy, pays ahead, doesn't break anything." She sets the cup down. "And there's three smiths drink in here who've told him no this week. You just watched the fourth. They don't say why. Not to me." She picks up the next cup. "That's all I know about him, and it's more than I'd tell most."

Bram goes up the stairs. They creak under his weight.

Below, the common room is getting louder as the carters settle in for the night.

Nothing more happens before first light unless you want to be awake for it.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Sefa offers Venn her hand. "That's clear, then. I accept. We'll see you in the yard tomorrow." Then she settles back and finishes her supper.

Venn hears you out and takes your hand. It's the same short, dry grip he gave Bram. "Three down, two back, and the twenty for whoever earns it." He lets go. "Walk them all you like. Tam'll think you're counting his spokes." He shakes Sefa's hand as well. Then he ties the string round the ledger again, two turns and a knot, and pushes the empty wine cup away.

Bram didn't look at you while you answered. Now he turns to Tobin with the ghost of a smile. "Bring the pole. Hedda's right that nobody pokes the hole. But a long pole tapped on the ground in front of you tells you where it's gone soft before your boot finds out." He nods to Hedda. "First light, at the lane by the last forge. We'll go down the pasture edge in a line, ten feet apart, slow, and nobody stands still. If the ground moves, everyone gets back to the lane. No heroics over a dog that's already gone."

Tobin's face lights up. "There's a pike shaft in the Thunderhammer's scrap pile," he says. "No head on it. I'll ask for it."

"Ask," Hedda says. "Don't take." She nods once. "First light. We'll be there."

Sefa turns to Bram and Hedda. "What did Ruddock tell you about the pasture? The notice mentioned missing sheep and deep holes, but not whether anyone actually saw what made them. I'll come at first light. Better to look together."

Hedda shakes her head. "Never met him. Only heard the name tonight." She tips her chin at Bram. "His man's the carter."

The grey coat went up a few minutes ago, during the bargaining, after Venn had sat down, not before he came in. Venn saw him go too.

When you lower your voice about the grey coat, Venn looks at the stairs. Only the stairs, and only for a moment. "Never set eyes on him before tonight," he says. "But there's been a few like him at the forges since the thaw. Good coats, clean boots, wanting iron sold to them or smiths to work it. I'm salt and wool. They've no use for me." He gets up off the bench slowly, a hand on the table edge. "My yard, middle of the morning. Goodnight to you." He goes out by the street door into the dark with the ledger under his arm.

As the talk breaks up, Bram speaks low to you and Sefa together. "Our friend in the grey coat just went up to the rooms with doors. Left side." A short pause. "Ours are left side as well. Could be nothing. A man with a purse and a black stone needs a bed like anyone." He turns his cup over, finished. "Bar your door, Sefa. Pell, you're over the stairs. If you hear boots that don't sound like carters, I'd take it kindly if you knocked."

Bram stops at the bar, where Orla is wiping out cups, and Sefa goes up to it a moment later. Then Bram goes up. The stairs creak under his weight.

The loft is over the stairhead, and you'll hear every boot on those boards. Below, the common room is getting louder as the carters settle in for the night.

Nothing more happens before first light unless you want to be awake for it.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway] [Sefa Marrin] [Pell Dunmore]

## Round 11

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram sits on the edge of the cot for a while in the dark, boots still on, and listens to the wall.

He doesn't press an ear to the planks. That isn't his trade, and a man who's paid through tomorrow and broken nothing has earned his privacy. Bram just listens the way he would at a campfire on a bad stretch of road: for a door opening, boots in the corridor, the ladder to the loft creaking. If the lamp next door goes out he notes roughly when. If someone comes or goes he notes that too.

Then he lies down on top of the blanket with one hand near the sword hilt and sleeps the way sixteen years taught him: lightly, and all at once.

He's up before the window greys. He shrugs into the mail, buckles the belt, slings the shield and takes two of the javelins. They're no use against something underground, but useful if it comes up. He leaves the other two, the torches and the bedroll in the room. He takes the healer's kit and the rope, since a pasture with holes in it is a good place to need fifty feet of hemp.

On his way past he knocks once, softly, on Sefa's door. "First light. I'll be in the lane by the last forge." He doesn't knock on Corby's.

Downstairs, if Orla or a kitchen girl is up, he buys a heel of bread and eats it walking.

At the lane he waits for Hedda and Tobin. Before anyone steps off the hard road, he looks the pasture over from the edge: the slope down to Ruddock's ditch, any holes he can see, how the ground lies, whether anything is out on it.

*(Out of game: did Bram hear anything in the night, Corby leaving, voices, the ladder? And at the lane, what can he see of the lower pasture from the road in the first light?)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The common room goes on roaring under the floor for another hour. Bram sits on the edge of the cot in the dark with his boots on and listens the way he would at a fire on a bad stretch of road.

The ladder over the stairhead creaks again and again as the carters go up in ones and twos to the loft, swearing at the rungs. A door down the corridor opens and shuts. It's the snorer nearest the stairs, back from the privy, and his snoring starts up again inside a minute. Through the plank wall the man next door is mostly quiet. Now and then a chair creaks, and once there's a short dry cough. A thin seam of lamplight shows where two planks don't quite meet under the eaves. It stays lit long after the noise below has died down to Orla's broom and the bar of the street door going home. Somewhere near midnight, by the feel of it, the seam goes dark. Nobody goes into that room and nobody comes out.

Bram lies down on top of the blanket with his hand near the hilt and sleeps.

He wakes before the shutter greys, all at once, the way he always does. The corridor is black except for the rushlight, burned down to a stub in its dish. He gets into the mail by feel, buckles the belt, slings the shield and takes two javelins. He coils the rope at his hip and hooks the healer's kit on the belt. The other two javelins, the torches and the bedroll stay by the cot.

He has the last buckle done when the door next to his opens.

It's quiet, the way a careful man opens a door, but not sneaking. Boots go past his room toward the stairs at an even pace. They're good boots by the sound, with no hobnails, and there's no hurry in them. The stairs give their creaks one after another and then go quiet.

Bram goes out a minute later. He knocks once, softly, on the last door. "First light. I'll be in the lane by the last forge." Then he goes down.

Orla isn't up yet. A kitchen girl in a sacking apron is on her knees at the hearth, blowing yesterday's embers back to life. Yesterday's loaves sit on the board beside her under a cloth. She sells him a heel for a copper without getting up, holding out a sooty palm for the coin. While he's paying, the back door to the stable yard stands ajar on the cold, and he hears a horse being led across the yard cobbles. A man's voice says something low to the ostler. Then the yard gate drags on its hinge, and the hooves go out at a walk and fade into the street. The girl doesn't look round. Out here people leave early all the time.

---

Beregost is grey and cold and smells of last night's coal. A few chimneys are smoking along the Coast Way, and somewhere a forge has started early, a single hammer at a slow, warming-up pace. Bram eats the bread as he walks. The south lane leaves the Way past the last forge. It's hard-packed and rutted from Ruddock's hide carts, with a hurdle fence along its west side.

Hedda is already there, leaning on the fence with her arms folded. Tobin is beside her, holding a headless pike shaft upright like a standard. It's eight feet of grey ash, split a little at the top end.

"Thunderhammer's lad was lighting up," Tobin says before anyone asks. "Said take it, it's firewood."

"He asked," Hedda says, to settle it.

From the lane, in the thin light, Bram looks the pasture over before anyone sets foot off the hard road.

It slopes away west, rough grass gone silver with dew, down to a line of reeds and a low mist that hangs where Ruddock's ditch must run. The smell comes up the slope on the cold air, sour and rank like the bottom of a tan pit. The top end near the lane is cropped short. The bottom end, toward the ditch, has grown long and lank and darker green. That's the wet ground Col talked about, and the sheep have left it alone.

The sheep are here, a dozen or so, packed against the hurdle at the top corner of the field, as close to the lane as they can get. None of them is grazing. They stand with their heads up.

Down near the reeds, about where Col said, there's a dark mark in the long grass that the dew hasn't silvered. It's round, roughly the width of a cartwheel, and too regular to be a shadow. From this distance Bram can't see into it. There's no spoil heap beside it, just as the notice said. The grass grows right up to the lip, and then there is nothing.

There's a second thing, and it takes the slant of the early light to show it. Thirty feet or so up the slope from the hole, a shallow line runs across the pasture, crooked and no deeper than a plough furrow. The grass along it lies a shade lower than the grass on either side, as if the ground underneath has settled. It runs from somewhere near the ditch toward the middle of the field and fades out before it gets there.

Nothing moves out on the grass. The mist lies still on the ditch. Behind them on the Way, a second hammer joins the first.

Hedda follows his look down the slope and says nothing for a while.

"That line," she says at last. "Was that there for the carter yesterday?"

Nobody here can answer that. Col might. His lane runs on past the pasture to the south, and there's smoke going up from a chimney at the far end of it.
````


### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I bar my door and sleep until dawn.

In the morning I wash, put on my armour, and check that my book and dagger are secure. I take my pack with me and meet the others by the lane at the last forge.

“Morning. Before we spread out—what did the carter actually see, Bram? I’d rather know whether we’re watching for something burrowing or ground giving way.”

I keep to the lane while we get our bearings, looking over the pasture for holes, disturbed turf, and any people or livestock still out there.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram's knock on the door at the end of the corridor comes in the dark. It's one soft knock, and his voice carries through the planks: *First light. I'll be in the lane by the last forge.* Then his boots go off down the corridor and the stairs creak under him. Sefa slept with the bar down, and nothing woke her before the knock.

The shutter is only just going grey at the edges. She washes in last night's cold water from the jug on the peg shelf, buckles on the studded leather by feel and checks the Book of Shadows at her belt. The pages are warm, as they always are. The dagger is at her hip. She takes the pack and lifts the bar.

The corridor is empty and the rushlight has burned out in its dish. The door next to Bram's stands a hand's width open, and the room behind it is dark and cold. The cot is stripped down to the ticking.

Downstairs the kitchen girl is still on her knees at the hearth. The fire is going now and she has a pot hung over it. She looks up when Sefa comes down. Her eyes go to Sefa's hands before her face, and then she looks back at the pot. Orla's voice comes from somewhere behind the kitchen door, already scolding the ostler about something. Nobody stops Sefa on the way out.

---

Outside it's cold and grey, and Beregost smells of old coal smoke. Two hammers are going on the Coast Way, slow and out of step. The south lane leaves the Way past the last forge, hard-packed and rutted, with a hurdle fence along its west side. Bram is at the fence, where he said he'd be. Hedda is leaning on the fence with her arms folded. Tobin stands beside her with eight feet of grey ash held upright like a standard.

Tobin sees Sefa first. His left hand comes up before he can stop it, palm out, showing her the pink seam in the morning light. Then he remembers himself and grips the pole with it instead.

"Slept right through," he says. "First night since Nashkel."

Hedda gives her a nod. That's all she offers by way of good morning.

Sefa asks her question and stays on the hard lane, looking out over the pasture while the answer comes.

The light is a little stronger now. The pasture slopes away west, the grass silver with dew, down to a line of reeds where a low mist sits on Ruddock's ditch. A sour stink like the bottom of a tan pit drifts up the slope. The upper part of the field near the lane is cropped short. The bottom end is long, lank and darker green, and nothing has grazed it.

There are no people in the field. The only stock is a dozen or so sheep packed tight against the hurdle in the top corner, as close to the lane as they can get. None of them is grazing. Their heads are up, and one ewe stamps a forefoot and goes still again.

Down by the reeds there's a round dark patch in the long grass about the width of a cartwheel. It's the one place the dew hasn't silvered. Grass grows right up to its edge, and there's no heap of earth anywhere beside it.

Hedda unfolds one arm and points, low, so the sheep won't notice the movement. "Up from the hole. See the line."

Sefa sees it once she knows where to look. It's a shallow crooked trough across the slope, no deeper than a plough furrow, about thirty feet above the hole. The grass along it lies a shade lower than the grass on either side, as if the ground underneath had settled. It starts somewhere near the ditch and runs toward the middle of the field, then fades out before it gets there.

Nothing moves on the grass. At the far south end of the lane, smoke is rising straight up from a single chimney into the still air.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell sleeps in his boots, with his back to the wall and his satchel under his head. He wakes before the carters do, in the blue dark, and lies still a minute listening to the house settle. Then he pads down the stairs without a light and steps out into the yard behind the inn to splash water on his face.

He eats a strip of dried meat from his pack, checks the draw of the two daggers, and loosens the shortsword in its sheath. By the time the sky goes grey over the chimneys of Beregost, he is at the lane by the last forge, standing on the edge of the road where a man can see both the way down to the pasture and the lights coming on in the town.

When the others come, he'll nod once, not too warmly, and fall into step at the outside of the group, where he can watch the grass.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell wakes in the loft in the blue dark. The rafters are close overhead and the carters snore all around him. He lies still and listens to the house.

The first boots come before anything else stirs. Down the corridor a door opens carefully, but nobody is trying to sneak. The boots come past the foot of the loft ladder at an even pace and go down the stairs one creak at a time. They aren't a carter's boots. There are no hobnails and no scuffing, and the man in them is in no hurry. A minute later a heavier tread follows, with the soft chink of mail under it. One quiet knock sounds at the far end of the corridor, and Bram's voice says *first light*, *the lane by the last forge*. Then that tread goes down the stairs too. The carters sleep through all of it.

Pell gives it another minute, then climbs down the ladder without a light.

The kitchen girl is on her knees at the hearth in a sacking apron, coaxing the fire. She glances at him and goes back to her blowing. The back door to the stable yard stands ajar. Outside, the cobbles are wet and grey, and the air is cold enough to make his eyes water. A pile of horse dung is still steaming in the middle of the yard. One stall door hangs open on clean straw. The ostler, a stooped man with a lantern hooked on his wrist, is putting his shoulder to the yard gate. It drags shut on its bad hinge and he drops the bar across it. He doesn't look round at Pell. At this hour people come and go all the time.

The trough water is cold enough to sting. Pell splashes his face, eats a strip of dried meat standing up, and checks both daggers and the shortsword. Each one draws cleanly.

---

Beregost is just waking. A single forge is going on the Coast Way, then a second, slow and out of step. Pell walks south past the last forge to where the south lane leaves the Way, hard-packed and rutted, with a hurdle fence along its west side.

He isn't first.

Hedda is leaning on the fence with her arms folded, and Tob stands beside her holding eight feet of grey ash upright. Bram's shield is there at the hurdle. They are all looking down the slope.

From the edge of the road Pell can see the pasture past them. The dew-silvered grass runs down to a line of reeds and a low mist. A dozen sheep are packed into the top corner against the fence with their heads up, and not one of them is grazing. Down by the reeds there's a dark round patch in the long grass. The sour tan-pit stink comes up the slope on the cold air.

Nothing moves on the grass.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text

The first boots come before anything else stirs. Down the corridor a door opens carefully, but nobody is trying to sneak. The boots come past the foot of the loft ladder at an even pace and go down the stairs one creak at a time. They aren't a carter's boots. There are no hobnails and no scuffing, and the man in them is in no hurry. A minute later a heavier tread follows, with the soft chink of mail under it. One quiet knock sounds at the far end of the corridor, and Bram's voice says *first light*, *the lane by the last forge*. Then that tread goes down the stairs too. The carters sleep through all of it.

You give it another minute, then climb down the ladder without a light.

The kitchen girl is on her knees at the hearth in a sacking apron, coaxing the fire. She glances at you and goes back to her blowing. The back door to the stable yard stands ajar. Outside, the cobbles are wet and grey, and the air is cold enough to make your eyes water. A pile of horse dung is still steaming in the middle of the yard. One stall door hangs open on clean straw. The ostler, a stooped man with a lantern hooked on his wrist, is putting his shoulder to the yard gate. It drags shut on its bad hinge and he drops the bar across it. He doesn't look round at you. At this hour people come and go all the time.

The trough water is cold enough to sting. You splash your face, eat a strip of dried meat standing up, and check both daggers and the shortsword. Each one draws cleanly.

---

Beregost is just waking. A single forge is going on the Coast Way, then a second, slow and out of step. You walk south past the last forge to where the south lane leaves the Way, hard-packed and rutted, with a hurdle fence along its west side.

You aren't first.

Hedda is leaning on the fence with her arms folded, and Tob stands beside her holding eight feet of grey ash upright. Bram's shield is there at the hurdle. They are all looking down the slope.

From the edge of the road you can see the pasture past them. The dew-silvered grass runs down to a line of reeds and a low mist. A dozen sheep are packed into the top corner against the fence with their heads up, and not one of them is grazing. Down by the reeds there's a dark round patch in the long grass. The sour tan-pit stink comes up the slope on the cold air.

Nothing moves on the grass.
````

Together after: [Pell Dunmore, Bram Halloway]

## Round 12

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stands at the hurdle a long moment with the bread forgotten in his hand, reading the slope the way he'd read a ford before putting wagons in it.

"That's its road," he says quietly to Hedda. "The line. It runs just under the turf, close enough that the roof sags behind it. That's fresh. Ground that's been settling a week would show it in the grass colour, and that hasn't." He nods at the sheep pressed against the hurdle. "And they know it's down there. Sheep don't stand like that for a dog."

He looks along the line to where it fades out in the middle of the field. That's the part that interests him.

"It stops short of the dry ground. Likes the wet end, by the ditch. Soft digging." He finishes the bread. "Nobody goes down there this morning. Not with four of us and no plan. Tobin, you can tap the ground along the hurdle here if you like, up where it's cropped and hard. Hear what solid sounds like. Then you'll know what hollow sounds like when you find it."

He waits for Sefa a minute or two, watching the reeds. Then he says to Hedda, "By the way, our friend in the grey coat. Corby. He left before dawn, on a horse, out the stable yard. Paid through tonight, Orla said. Might be back for supper, might not." A shrug. "Just so you know his face."

Then he points down the lane at the chimney smoke.

"I'm going to ask Col whether that line was there yesterday, and make sure his girl's indoors. Then Ruddock, at the yard. He's lost two ewes and a dog in his field. He'll want it gone before it learns there are children on the lane." He looks back at the dark round mark by the reeds. "If he'll pay something fair, this is an afternoon's work for five, before the wagons go. They come up at noise and weight, so you give them something to come up at, and you're standing where you want to be when they do. Hard ground at your back, spears out." Dryly: "Not my first rule of the road, but it's in the first ten."

He sets off down the lane on the packed ruts, keeping to the hard middle and well off the pasture side. Hedda and Tobin can follow or wait for Sefa. On the way he watches the field edge for any more of those sagging lines, especially any that run toward the lane.

*(At Col's door: Bram asks about the furrow line, whether it was there yesterday, and which way it ran. Then on to Ruddock's yard to ask what he'll pay to be shut of it.)*

Sefa Marrin: “Glad to hear it, Tobin.”

I follow Hedda’s pointing finger along the shallow trough, then look back at the sheep pressed against the fence.

“Something may be tunnelling under there. Let’s not spread out across it yet. That pole won’t give much warning if the ground beneath us is hollow.”

I stay on the hard-packed lane. “Can we get the sheep out through a gate from this side? They seem keen enough to leave. And has anyone spoken to Ruddock this morning? He ought to know we’re here before anyone goes into his field.”

I study the hole and sunken line from the fence, trying to recall whether I’ve read of burrowing creatures that leave this sort of sign, particularly with that sour smell.

Pell Dunmore: Pell comes up to the fence without a word and stands a little off from Hedda and the boy, on the side away from the town. He does not jump the hurdle.

He lets his eyes run along the edge of the pasture first, the way a man reads a page: the fence line, the dew, where the grass is bent, where it isn't. Then the dark patch by the reeds. The sheep huddled at the top corner, not grazing, tell him more than the hole does.

He looks to Bram and keeps his voice low, pitched for the four of them only. "I'll stay this side of the fence and go along the lane a way. Before we walk that grass, I want to see if whatever made the hole came up from below and went back down, or whether it came across the lane in the night. The edge will tell." He tips his head toward a low run of grass by a fence post, noticeably flattened. "No one goes through the gate yet. If the ground moves, I'd rather be on the path than in among the sheep."

The morning dew glitters too much to hide tracks from a careful eye; he studies the verge and fence line as he moves, hands loose at his sides, stealing silent steps along the lane outside the pasture. Something about the way the reeds sit and the sheep refuse the lower grass keeps his shoulders square.
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Intelligence (Nature), untrained, +1: placing a burrower that leaves no spoil heap, sour ground and stinging tunnel walls · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Intelligence (Nature), untrained, +1: placing a burrower that leaves no spoil heap, sour ground and stinging tunnel walls · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram stands at the hurdle with the heel of bread forgotten in his hand while he reads the slope.

Hedda hears him out. At *that's fresh* she looks at the line again, longer this time, then at the sheep.

"No plan, no field," she says. "Suits me." She tips her head at Tobin. "Tob. Hard ground only."

Tobin is already moving, delighted to have a job. He walks the cropped strip inside the hurdle's shadow and stays on the lane side of it, reaching over the top rail to thump the butt of the pike shaft on the turf. On the high ground it sounds dull and solid, like knocking on a table.

When Bram mentions the grey coat, Hedda gives it one nod.

"Corby." She files it away. "Paid through tonight and gone before light. Fair enough. I've done it." She doesn't say when.

---

Sefa stays on the packed lane and follows the trough down the slope by eye, from the hole to where it fades out. She tries to place it among everything she has read or heard on the hospice benches.

What she knows of fields is little, and what comes back to her is no use. She remembers moles, and a story of a collapsed well in a Rivington yard. There was a ward sister who swore by vinegar for wasp stings. The hospice books were full of fevers, fractures and burns, and none of them said a word about anything that digs. She can name the stink, the smell of the tan pits, and nothing more. She doesn't know what made the hole.

To her question about the sheep, Hedda points with her chin at the top corner, where the flock is packed tightest. A hurdle gate hangs on a post there, tied shut with a twist of withy.

"There's the gate," she says. "Ruddock's sheep. Ruddock's gate. I'd ask him first." She glances after Bram, who is already a dozen strides down the lane. "He's asking."

Tobin works his way along the hurdle, thumping the turf. It's dull and solid at every post. The sheep shuffle away from the noise without leaving the corner, and one ewe gives a thin, cross bleat. Down the slope nothing stirs in the long grass. The line lies where it lay, and the mist sits on the ditch.

---

Pell goes south along the lane on the pasture side, slow, his eyes on the verge.

The lane gives him nothing, and that is worth knowing too. The ruts are packed hard as brick. Along the edge the dew lies on the grass in an unbroken grey skin. The only marks in it are his own, and Bram's well out in the middle of the lane ahead. Nothing came across in the night, from either side.

The flattened grass by the fence post isn't the thing's work. Pell sees that as soon as he's close. Last night's dew has settled into it the same as everywhere else, so it's older than that. The top rail of the hurdle is scuffed pale, and a crust of dried mud sits on it where a boot went over. From there a single trodden path goes straight down the cropped slope toward the dark patch by the reeds. Someone heavy walked it once, in a hurry, and didn't come back up the same way. The path crosses the place where the sunken line now runs. Where the two meet, the trodden grass sags with the rest.

Bram is watching the field edge as he goes, and he sees no other sagging lines. Only the one runs across the pasture, and it doesn't come anywhere near the lane.

---

Col Fenner's place is the last on the south lane: a low cottage of mud and timber with a hide shed leaning against it and a chimney smoking straight up. The lane runs on past it, rutted, toward the next field. Bram has barely reached the step when the door opens. Col has been watching the lane from the window.

He's sober and grey in the face, in yesterday's shirt. The left sleeve is rolled up, and the forearm under it is still red and shiny but no worse. Behind him a girl of eleven or so sits on a stool by the fire with her chin on her fists and a basket on her knees. She is sulking with her whole body.

"She's in," Col says, before Bram can ask. "Says the Coast Way's twice as far. It is." He comes out onto the step and pulls the door to behind him.

He hears the question about the line and looks back up the lane at the pasture for a long time before he answers.

"Weren't there." He's sure of it. "I went straight down to that hole after Bess, running. Straight down the slope. Ground was hard under me all the way to the wet. If that had been there I'd have gone in up to my knee." He shakes his head slowly. "It come after. In the night, or this morning. I couldn't tell you which way it ran. I never saw it."

He rubs his good hand over his face.

"Ruddock's at his pits by now. Top of the lane, behind the last forge, where the ditch starts." He looks at Bram straight. "What did you see down there?"

*(Out of game: Sefa had already reached the fence by the time Bram spoke, so there was no need to wait for her.)*

 Sefa's memory fails her. She goes through all she knows and can't name the creature that dug that hole. She'll have to go by what the others say about it, or what Ruddock and the field show her.

*(Out of game: none of Bram's four actions needed a roll. He read the slope, told Hedda about Corby, set Tobin to tapping and walked to Col's door, and the scene above already told each of them.)*
````
