## Round 13

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram tells it plainly, standing on the step with his back to the lane.

"A hole by the reeds, the width of a cartwheel. No spoil, same as you said. And a line thirty feet up from it, running across the slope toward the middle of the field, where the ground's sunk behind something moving just under the turf." He keeps his voice level. "If you went down that slope on hard ground yesterday, then it's moved since. It's moving uphill, and that's a beast getting bolder. Your sheep know it. They're packed in the top corner and none of them is grazing."

He glances past Col at the girl on the stool and lowers his voice a little.

"It hasn't come near the lane. I watched the whole field edge walking down, and so is the lad behind me. Your house sits on hard ground. Keep her off the grass and on the packed ruts, and keep yourself off it too. Don't go looking for Bess's collar." A beat. "I'm sorry. I'd want to. Don't."

Then, practical:

"One thing. Your hides. Anything that stinks of fresh blood sitting out in that shed tonight, put it up off the ground or shut it in. No sense ringing the dinner bell."

He leans round the doorframe just enough for the girl to see him, and nods to her gravely, one road hand to another.

"Coast Way's twice as far, aye. It's also the way the caravans go. Mind your feet on it."

Back on the step, to Col: "I'm going to Ruddock now. If he'll pay something fair, we'll deal with it today, in daylight, before the wagons go tomorrow. If he won't..." He shrugs. "We'll see. Either way, I'll stop back and tell you."

He walks back up the lane on the hard middle. When he reaches the pasture he whistles once, short, to call Pell, Sefa, Hedda and Tobin together. Then he goes on with whoever comes, toward the top of the lane behind the last forge, where the ditch starts and the tan pits stink.

*(At Ruddock's: Bram wants to know what he'll pay, and whether he can spare anything useful: a couple of old ewes past breeding, or a dead one, for bait, and planks or hurdles to stand on. He'll also ask whether Ruddock has seen the thing itself, and how deep the ditch runs.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Col hears it all without moving. He looks at the step between his boots when Bram says *moving uphill*, and again at *don't go looking for Bess's collar*. His jaw works once, and he doesn't argue. A man doesn't need to argue with something he'd already half decided to do.

"Wasn't going to," he says after a while. Neither of them believes it, and he lets it lie.

When Bram mentions the hides he looks round at the shed leaning on the cottage wall. "Three green ones from the knacker in there, come last night. Salted, not scraped." He rubs his good hand down his face. "I'll get them up on the beam. Shut the door on them."

Bram leans round the doorframe. The girl on the stool has been listening to every word with her chin still on her fists. She looks back at the big man in mail for a moment, decides he counts, and sits up.

"Caravans go that way?" she says.

"Wenna," Col says, without heat.

"I'm only asking." She looks at Bram again, and the sulk is gone from her shoulders. "I'll mind my feet."

On the step Col nods once at *I'll stop back*. "Aye. Do." He goes inside and shuts the door. Through it Bram hears him say something low to the girl, and then the scrape of the shed bolt round the side as he starts on the hides.

---

Bram walks back up the lane on the hard middle of the ruts. The pasture looks the same as it did. The sheep are still packed into the top corner, the line still lies across the slope and the mist still hangs on the ditch. When he reaches the hurdle he gives one short whistle, and it carries over the wet grass. Then he goes on north toward the last forge.

You find Ruddock's yard by the smell before you see it. Behind the last forge the lane opens into a muddy square fenced with hurdles. There's a long open shed hung with hides, a row of brick-lined pits sunk in the ground with a scum of bark and lime on the water, and a pump. At the low corner a trench takes the overflow away downhill. That's where Ruddock's ditch begins. The stink here is thick enough to taste.

A man is standing in the nearest pit up to his thighs in leather waders, working a hide over with a pole. He's square and bald and fifty or so, and his arms are stained brown to the elbow, the colour tanners never wash out. He sees the mail and the shield come into his yard and doesn't stop working.

"If it's leather, I've none cured till the month's end," he calls. His voice is loud and flat from years of shouting over the yard. "If it's the Fist, I've paid."

He listens while he works. At *the lower pasture* the pole stops. He heaves himself up out of the pit onto the brick edge, water running off the waders, and wipes his hands on a sacking apron that makes them no cleaner.

"Col's dog." It isn't a question. "He told me at the pump. Daft old bitch." He says it gently.

He answers the questions in order, a man used to answering buyers.

"Seen it? No. Seen what it leaves. First ewe went eleven nights back. I found a hole where she'd been lying, and I thought the ground had let go under her. Old workings, my father used to say, there's clay pits all under that end. Second ewe, four nights back. Second hole. That's when I wrote the paper." He jerks his head at the trench. "The ditch is three foot deep where it leaves here, maybe four by the reeds, and four foot across. It's what comes off my pits. Lime and bark and worse. Nothing drinks from it. Nothing lives in it. If you go in it, you'll want to burn your boots."

To the bait he gives a short bark that might be a laugh.

"Old ewes I've got. There's one with foot rot that's had her last lamb. She's yours, and welcome, and I'll throw in the one that keeps getting through the hurdle. Hurdles, aye, there's a stack behind the shed. Planks, there's the old pit boards, oak, soaked through, heavy as sin. Bring them back."

Then he folds his stained arms and looks at Bram the way he'd look at a hide brought to him for curing, weighing what it's worth against what it'll cost him.

"Pay. I'm a tanner, not a patriar. The Fist offers five a head for bandits and doesn't pay it, so I'll not insult you with that." He thinks. "Eight gold, from me, when it's dead and I can see it. And the shell." His eyes sharpen a little. "If that's what you say it is, an armourer in the Gate'll pay for the plates off its back. I'd want first offer on it at a fair price. That, or you take the shell and I pay you six. Your choice."

He waits, dripping onto the bricks.

"Today, you said. Before your wagons. Where do you want my ewes?"

 (Out of game: none of these needed a roll, since all four were talk or walking and the scene played each one: Bram warned Col and Wenna, gave his whistle at the hurdle, walked up to Ruddock's yard and promised Col he'd stop back. Ruddock's offer is waiting on Bram's answer.)
````

````text
"Seen it? No. Seen what it leaves. First ewe went eleven nights back. I found a hole where she'd been lying, and I thought the ground had let go under her. Old workings, my father used to say, there's clay pits all under that end. Second ewe, four nights back. Second hole. That's when I wrote the paper." He jerks his head at the trench. "The ditch is three foot deep where it leaves here, maybe four by the reeds, and four foot across. It's what comes off my pits. Lime and bark and worse. Nothing drinks from it. Nothing lives in it. If you go in it, you'll want to burn your boots."

To the bait he gives a short bark that might be a laugh.

"Old ewes I've got. There's one with foot rot that's had her last lamb. She's yours, and welcome, and I'll throw in the one that keeps getting through the hurdle. Hurdles, aye, there's a stack behind the shed. Planks, there's the old pit boards, oak, soaked through, heavy as sin. Bring them back."

Then he folds his stained arms and looks at you the way he'd look at a hide brought to him for curing, weighing what it's worth against what it'll cost him.

"Pay. I'm a tanner, not a patriar. The Fist offers five a head for bandits and doesn't pay it, so I'll not insult you with that." He thinks. "Eight gold, from me, when it's dead and I can see it. And the shell." His eyes sharpen a little. "If that's what you say it is, an armourer in the Gate'll pay for the plates off its back. I'd want first offer on it at a fair price. That, or you take the shell and I pay you six. Your choice."

He waits, dripping onto the bricks.

"Today, you said. Before your wagons. Where do you want my ewes?"
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “I can’t place it,” I admit to Hedda. “Nothing I learned at the hospice is much help with burrows.”

I watch Tobin tapping along the fence. “That’s enough to know what solid sounds like. Let’s keep the rest quiet until Bram’s back.”

I move a few paces away from the hurdle, staying on the packed lane, with a clear way back toward town. From there I keep watch on the trough and the dark patch by the reeds while we wait for word from Bram and Pell.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tobin's pole stops at the last post.

"Solid all along," he reports, and props the shaft on his shoulder. He sounds a little sorry there's nothing else to tap.

Hedda takes Sefa's admission with one nod. "He knows," she says, tipping her chin down the lane. "Said ankheg last night like he'd met one." She stays at the fence.

Sefa steps back a few paces onto the hard middle of the ruts, where the lane runs straight back toward the forges and the town. From there she can see the whole slope at once: the cropped top, the long dark grass, the trough lying crooked across it and the round patch by the reeds where the dew won't settle.

Nothing out there moves while she watches. The mist lies on the ditch as still as a sheet on a line. A crow comes down out of the grey and lands in the short grass halfway down the field, walks a few steps with its head cocked, then lifts off again without finding anything and flaps away toward the forges. In the top corner the sheep shift against the hurdle, and settle, and shift again. They still haven't put their heads down to graze. Behind her on the Coast Way a third hammer starts up, then a fourth, and Beregost finds its working rhythm.

Bram comes back up the lane from Col's end. He keeps to the middle of the ruts, well off the pasture side. As he draws level with the hurdle he gives one short whistle that carries over the wet grass, and he goes on north toward the last forge without stopping, as if he expects whoever wants to come to fall in behind him.

Tobin straightens at once. "That's us."

"That's *him*," Hedda says. She unfolds her arms, takes one last look down the slope at the trough, and pushes off the fence. Then she turns to Sefa to see what she'll do.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell comes back up the lane at an easy pace, but his eyes are still on the grass. When he reaches the scuffed post, he stops and rests one hand lightly on the top rail, not climbing it.

"Nothing crossed the lane last night. The dew's unbroken both ways." He nods at the pale scuff on the rail and the crust of mud. "But somebody went over here—boot, heavy, in a hurry—and walked straight down toward that dark patch. Straight-line path, no doubling back." He lets his eyes follow the trodden grass down to where it cuts across the sunken line. "It crosses the sag. Where the two meet, the ground's settled together. So whoever came over went down either to look, or to fetch something, or to see the hole. And whatever tunneled there had already been through."

He steps back from the rail, still outside the fence. "It doesn't tell us if the thing took the dog before or after he went down. But a man who goes into a wet field straight at a fresh sink and leaves his trail on the dew... he wasn't hunting a lost sheep with a lantern. He went because he already knew."

Pell looks at Bram then, with the first real edge to his voice. "I can walk the edge down to the reeds along the lane side, quiet. The ground near the hole's the question. If it's hollow, we'll see the roof move before we're on it. But I'd rather have the two of you at my back with a pole and a bow than find that out alone."

He looks to Sefa, "If you can see the field from here, watch the sheep and the grass. They'll know before we do."

A pause.

"And maybe ask Col—your carter—whose boot that was."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell walks back up the lane at an easy pace and stops at the scuffed post. He rests one hand on the top rail and doesn't climb it.

He says his piece low, the way he pitched it at the fence earlier, for a few people close by. Nobody is close by now. While he was reading the verge further down, Bram came back up the middle of the ruts from the cottage at the far end. He passed on the far side of the lane, a broad back in mail with the shield slung, and didn't stop. At the hurdle where the others stood he gave one short whistle that carried across the wet grass. Now he's well up the lane toward the last forge, walking like a man who expects to be followed. Up at the hurdle, Hedda has pushed herself off the fence. Tob has the pike shaft on his shoulder and is already turned north. From where Pell stands, a low voice doesn't reach either of them.

The pasture doesn't answer him either. From this post the trodden path runs straight down the cropped slope, darker green in the silver of the dew. It sags where it crosses the sunken line and ends at the round patch by the reeds where the dew won't settle. The mist is still lying on the ditch. In the top corner a ewe stamps, and the flock shifts against the hurdle and goes still. None of them has put its head down.

Behind him on the Coast Way, the hammers have found their rhythm.

*(Out of game: Bram had already gone past on his way to Ruddock's yard, so nobody heard Pell's words; the whistle calls everyone up the lane after him, and the field is still there if Pell wants it.)*

 *(Out of game: Pell only offered to walk the edge and said he'd rather have Bram and Sefa behind him first, so he hasn't gone; nobody heard the offer, and he can still go now, alone or after the others.)*
````

## Round 14

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram looks round the yard at whoever has followed him up from the pasture before he answers.

"Eight gold and first offer on the shell, at a fair price," he says to Ruddock. "Fair means what the armourer in the Gate would give us, less the cart to get it there. You'll see it dead before you pay a copper." He puts out his hand, stain and all, and shakes on it. "Keep the ewes penned here till we come for them. We're due at Venn's yard mid-morning. We'll be back after, in good light, early afternoon. Lend me a mallet and a stake as well, and a few feet of cord for the ewes."

Then he turns to the others, low and plain, the way he'd brief a guard before a bad ford.

"Here's how I'd do it. We carry the oak boards down and lay them on the last of the hard ground, above where the line fades out. That's our floor. We stake the old ewe twenty-odd feet below it, on the soft, right across the line it's been running. She'll bleat. That's what we want. Then we stand still and quiet on the boards and let it come to her."

He scratches a rough arc in the mud with his boot.

"Don't bunch up and don't stand in a row. I've heard they spit acid in a line, and a row of people is a gift. Spread out in a curve, ten feet apart. I'll stand nearest the ewe with the shield. Hedda, on my left. Tobin, keep that pole, and the moment anything under you sounds hollow, step back onto the boards and shout. Javelins first when it breaks the turf. Then close in before it goes back down. If it grabs someone, everybody hits it. They let go when they're hurt. Sefa, stay back on the hard ground where you can see all of us. Pell, wherever you like, as long as it's not on the grass."

He looks at the trench of filth running downhill.

"And nobody fights with that ditch behind them. If it drags anyone, it'll drag them toward the wet."

To Ruddock, as he turns to go: "The second ewe, the one that gets through hurdles. Keep her. If one doesn't bring it up, she can try. If neither does, we'll think again."

*(Out of game: if anyone objects or has a better plan, Bram listens. Then he heads for Venn's yard behind the Thunderhammer to see the wagons, check the axles and meet Tam Hesketh and his boys, meaning to be back at Ruddock's by early afternoon.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ruddock hears the terms standing on the brick lip of the pit, water still running off his waders. Bram glances round the yard while he talks, looking for whoever answered the whistle. The gateway behind him is empty. The lane bends round the back of the last forge, and nobody has come round that bend yet.

"What the Gate armourer gives, less the cart," Ruddock repeats. That short bark comes out of him. "And who tells me what the Gate armourer gives? You?" He weighs it the way he'd weigh a hide with a flaw near the shoulder, then decides the flaw can be worked round. "Fair enough. I'll write and ask him myself, and you'll see the letter." He takes the hand. His grip is wet and hard, and it leaves a brown smear on Bram's palm that won't come off for a day. "Eight when it's dead and I've seen it. First offer on the plates. Done."

He has no trouble with the ewes. "They'll be in the pen by the shed. The old girl won't mind. She's minded nothing since lambing." The mallet, stake and cord are fine too. "They'll be by the pump when you come back. I'll not have you carrying my mallet round Venn's yard and leaving it there."

Bram turns to brief the others and gives it to the yard. He sets out the boards on the last of the hard ground, the ewe staked twenty-odd feet below on the soft, across the line, and the curve ten feet apart, not a row. He scratches the arc in the mud with his boot heel. Ruddock is the only one who hears it. He doesn't pretend not to listen. He leans on his pole and follows the boot through the mud.

At the part about the ditch he nods slowly.

"Good. That ditch'll take the skin off you without any help from the beast." He points his pole down the trench, toward the lower pasture out of sight beyond the hurdles. "And mind where you put my boards. My father always said the hard ground stops where his clay pits started. It's a straight line, more or less, across the middle of the field. Below it the old workings were filled in with whatever came to hand. Above it there's nothing but clay and stone, the way the gods left it." He shrugs. "That's what he said. I never dug to see."

He hears the last of it, keep the second ewe, and nods once.

"She stays. She'll be through the hurdle by noon whether I want her or not." He lowers himself back into the pit up to his thighs and takes up his hide again. "Early afternoon, then. I'll be here. I'm always here."

The pole goes back to work. Behind the forge the hammers have their full rhythm now, and the lane past the gateway is still empty. The smoke over the town has only just begun to thin. The sun is barely clear of the roofs.

*(Out of game: it's about half past six, and Venn said he'd be at his yard from mid-morning, when the oxen come in, so it's three hours or so before he's there. Does Bram go now, wait here for the others, or do something else first?)*

 *(Out of game: none of the three needs a roll. Shaking on the deal with Ruddock and borrowing the mallet, stake and cord were plain talk, and the story settled both. Bram described the board-and-ewe plan for the afternoon; he hasn't laid anything out yet, and only Ruddock heard him because the others haven't reached the yard. The ending question about what Bram does with the three hours before Venn arrives is still his to answer.)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I fall in beside Hedda, following Bram toward the forge along the hard lane.

“Nothing stirred while you were gone,” I tell him when we’re close enough. “Did Col see anything? And is his girl safely indoors?”

I glance back to check that Pell has seen us moving before continuing. “Hedda says you think it’s an ankheg. Before we agree to draw it out, I’ll need to know what to expect—and where you want me standing.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa falls in beside Hedda on the hard middle of the lane. Tobin takes the lead without anyone telling him to, carrying the pike shaft over his shoulder like a pikeman on parade. Hedda lets him.

Halfway to the forge Sefa looks back. Pell is still down at the scuffed post south of the hurdle with one hand on the top rail, a lean shape against the silver grass. He's a good stone's throw behind them. Whether he saw them set off, she can't tell from here.

The smell reaches them before the yard does. By the time the lane bends round the back of the last forge it is thick enough to taste: lime, wet bark and something rotten under both. Tobin pulls the collar of his shirt up over his nose and breathes through it, loudly.

"Don't," Hedda says. "You'll only taste it longer."

Past the bend the lane opens into a muddy square fenced with hurdles. There's a long open shed hung with hides, a row of brick-lined pits with scum on the water, a pump, and a trench at the low corner carrying the overflow off downhill toward the pasture. A square bald man is standing in the nearest pit up to his thighs, working a hide over with a pole. He doesn't stop when three more armed strangers come into his yard. He just looks at them, the way a man looks at a wagon that turns in through his gate.

Bram is standing in the middle of the yard. A rough curved line has been scratched in the mud at his feet with a boot heel, and the mud around it is churned.

Sefa says her piece as they come up: nothing stirred while he was gone, did Col see anything, is the girl indoors, what should she expect, and where does he want her standing.

The man in the pit answers before anyone else does, and doesn't look up from his hide.

"Your man's drawn it already." He points the dripping end of his pole at the arc in the mud. "I've had it once. I'll not have it twice. Mind the ditch."

Then he goes back to the hide.

Hedda stops at the edge of the scratched curve and looks down at it the way she looked at the trough across the pasture. She doesn't step on it. Tobin leans on the pike shaft and reads it upside down with his lips moving.

Sefa's questions are still waiting for Bram.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell lingers only a moment longer, then pushes off the post and follows the others at a loose, soundless trot.

He catches up where the lane meets the road, and speaks before Bram can say why he called them off.

"Post at the south end. Scuff where a boot went over the rail, mud still on it, and a straight trodden track from there to the dark patch—crosses the sag and doesn't come back." He keeps his voice low, his breathing even. "Dew went over it clean, so it's last night's work, not the thing's. Somebody went down already knowing where to look." A beat. "Whose boot, we don't know yet. But it went over the fence, not round to the gate."

Then, with a glance back toward the field: "The sheep are still packed in the corner. Whatever's under there hasn't gone far."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell pushes off the post and goes up the lane at a loose, quiet trot. He keeps to the hard middle of the ruts the whole way, with the pasture sliding past on his left: silver grass, the trough, the sheep packed into their corner. None of them turns a head to watch him go.

Nobody is waiting where the lane meets the Coast Way. Here the road is just the road. A carter's empty dray rattles north toward the Gate, a forge on the far side is throwing sparks out of its open front, and a woman in a doorway tips a pail of slops into the gutter and goes back inside. The ruts are packed too hard to hold a footprint. Pell doesn't need one, though. The stink tells him where the others went. It comes thick off a turning that runs round the back of the last forge, the lane bending behind the soot-black wall: lime, wet bark, and something rotten under both. Ahead, round the bend, a man's voice is shouting in a flat, loud yard-tone. Pell can't make out the words over the hammers, only that someone is talking and someone else isn't arguing.

He has his piece ready, the post, the scuff, the straight track over the sag. It's still unsaid. There was nobody at the junction to hear it, and he's only now rounding the forge wall to the hurdle gateway of the yard.

*(Out of game: Bram was never at the junction. He's already in Ruddock's yard round the bend, so Pell's words wait until he walks in; next round the three of you are in one scene.)*
````

## Round 15

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram looks at the empty gateway, then at his brown-smeared palm, and snorts quietly.

"Briefed the tanner. Well, he's the one with boards in it."

Before he goes, he steps to the edge of the pit.

"Your father's line, where the hard ground stops. Is there anything on the surface that marks it? A fence post, a stone, a change in the grass? I'd rather lay oak on clay your father left alone than on whatever he filled the pits with." He waits for the answer and fixes it in his head. "Early afternoon. Keep the old girl fed. She's got one more job to do."

Then he walks back down the lane toward the pasture, on the hard middle of the ruts, to find the others. Whoever is at the hurdle, he gives them the plan the way he gave it to Ruddock, without the boot-scratching this time. Boards on the hard ground above the clay-pit line. The ewe staked below it, across the furrow. A curve, not a row. Javelins when it breaks the turf, close in before it goes down, and nobody with the ditch at their back. He adds what Ruddock said about the old workings.

"So the beast's been living in soft filled ground. It hasn't been digging new earth. That's why there's no spoil, maybe. And why the line stops where it does." He nods at the middle of the field. "If Ruddock's father was right, it can't come up under our boards without breaking clay, and we'll hear that."

Then, to all of them:

"We've three hours before Venn's yard. I'd spend one of them eating something hot and one looking at a wagon. I'm going back to the Burning Wizard for whatever Orla's frying, then on to the Thunderhammer early. Anyone who wants to look at axles with me is welcome. Anyone who'd rather sleep, sleep." A glance at Pell. "And if you went along that verge and found anything, I'd hear it over breakfast."

*(Out of game: Bram heads back to the inn for breakfast with whoever comes. On the way he keeps an eye out for Corby's horse, in case it's back in the stable yard.)*

Sefa Marrin: I stop beside Hedda, keeping clear of the pits and overflow trench.

“Mind the ditch because of the runoff, or because you’ve seen the creature there?” I ask the tanner.

Then I look to Bram and the marks at his feet. “Show me what you’ve worked out. I can strike from well back and push it away from someone if my spells land—but I need a clear line, and I can’t promise to hold it off.”

I glance toward the lane. “Pell was still inspecting the fence when we left. Let’s hear what he found before we settle on a plan.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram looks at the empty gateway, then at the brown smear on his palm, and snorts quietly.

"Briefed the tanner. Well, he's the one with boards in it."

He steps to the brick edge of the pit. Ruddock doesn't stop working the hide, but he listens to the question about his father's line. When Bram gets to *whatever he filled the pits with*, the pole goes still.

"Fence post, no. Stone, yes." He points the pole at a gap in the yard hurdles, out toward the pasture. "On the north hurdle, about halfway down, there's a flat grey stone the size of a door-step. My father set it at the top corner of the last pit so he'd not put a plough in it. The grass has half grown over it, but you'll find it if you kick about. From there it runs straight across, near enough." He takes up the hide again. "And look where the sheep stop eating. They knew where it was before my father did."

"Early afternoon," Bram says. "Keep the old girl fed. She's got one more job to do."

"She's always fed. It's the only thing she's good at."

Bram turns to go back down the lane. Before he's taken three strides, boots and a pole butt come round the bend behind the forge. Tobin is first, with his collar up over his nose, then Hedda, then Sefa. They come in through the hurdle gateway, Ruddock calls out his piece about the arc and the ditch, and Bram's walk to the pasture is cut short. The people he meant to find are standing in the mud with him.

---

Sefa stops beside Hedda, well clear of the pits and the trench, and puts her question to the man in the waders.

Ruddock answers without turning round.

"The water. I've not seen your beast, not there nor anywhere else. That ditch is lime and bark and what comes off a hide. It'll eat the skin off your shins, and if you're lying in it with a cut it'll eat the cut. That's all I meant." He heaves the hide over. "That's enough, mind."

---

Bram gives them the plan standing over his own boot-marks, the way he gave it to the tanner but shorter, and he doesn't scratch it out again. He tells them about the oak boards on the last of the hard ground and the ewe staked below them on the soft, right across the furrow. They stand in a curve ten feet apart, not in a row. Javelins go in when it breaks the turf, then everyone closes in before it can go back down, and nobody has the ditch at their back. Then he adds the old clay workings and the grey stone on the north hurdle, and what he takes from them.

Hedda listens with her arms folded and her eyes on the arc in the mud. When he finishes she nods once.

"Curve's right. I've seen a thing spit in a line, at Nashkel. A man stood behind his mate to be safe." She leaves it there. "I've a spear. No javelins."

"I've got this," Tobin says, lifting the pike shaft.

"You've got a stick."

"It's a long stick."

When Sefa says she can hit it from well back and shove it off someone, Bram has her answer in the arc already: the hard ground behind the curve, where she can see all of them and nothing stands between her and the furrow. Hedda looks sideways at Sefa when the word *push* comes up. It's the same slow look she gave the book last night, but she doesn't say anything.

Sefa glances toward the lane and says they should hear what Pell found before the plan is settled.

Hedda tips her chin at the gateway behind them, where the lane bends round the forge wall.

"He was still at that post when we left. He'll come or he won't."

Bram tells them all how he means to spend the three hours: something hot at the Burning Wizard, then Venn's yard early to look at axles, with anyone welcome who wants to come and anyone who'd rather sleep left to sleep. He adds that if Pell found anything along the verge, he'd hear it over breakfast. Tobin brightens at *something hot* like a lamp being turned up. Hedda considers it.

"Venn's yard," she says. "Middle of the morning. We'll eat first." She doesn't say where, or whether it's with them.

Behind them, in the pit, Ruddock's pole goes on slapping the hide. The sun is well clear of the roofs now, and the smoke over Beregost is thinning toward the hills.

*(Out of game: Pell is reaching the gateway right now in his own message, so the walk back to the inn starts next round with all of you together. The stone and the grass line are things Bram can check this afternoon. No rolls were needed this round.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the three needed a roll, since each was talk and the scene settled it. Ruddock named the grey stone and the grazing edge, Bram briefed the group on the plan, and his breakfast-and-axles plan is waiting on the walk to the inn next round, once Pell has joined you.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't announce himself at the gate. He steps through it the way he came along the lane—soft over the hard ground—and stops just inside, letting his eyes get the count of the place before he lets anyone get a count of him.

The yard is flat and pounded chalk and gravel, with high planks along one side above reeking lime pits. Beyond a railed walk, hides are stretched. He notices the outlines of the others: Bram's broad back, the pike shaft over Tobin's shoulder, Hedda a pace off, Sefa somewhere past them. His report can wait until the shouting breaks.

He walks toward them, unhurried, and when he gets within hearing he speaks low to Bram's shoulder, the words tucked under the hammer-noise from the street.

"Nobody was at the junction. Lucky, since what I found wants saying once." He tells it tight: the south post, the scuff, the boot mud, the trodden track running straight to the dark patch, how it crosses the sag and doesn't come back, how the dew settled over the man-trail but not over the thing's run. Last: "Sheep hadn't grazed. They know it's under them."

Then he lets his eyes go past Bram to whoever is shouting, and waits for the scene to order itself.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The lane bends round the soot-black back of the forge and opens into Ruddock's yard. It's no chalk-and-gravel floor. The square is fenced with hurdles and trodden to mud. There's a long open shed hung with dripping hides and a row of brick-lined pits with grey scum on the water. A pump stands in the middle, and at the low corner a trench carries the overflow away downhill. The stink here is thick enough to chew. Pell stops inside the hurdle gateway and takes the count before anyone takes his.

He sees five people. Bram stands in the middle of the yard with a rough curve scratched in the mud at his feet. Hedda is a pace off it with her arms folded. Tobin leans on the pike shaft with his collar up over his nose. Sefa is a little back from them, well clear of the pits. The fifth is a square bald man up to his thighs in the nearest pit, working a hide over with a pole. The loud flat voice Pell heard from the lane is his. It has stopped now, and the pole slaps the hide in its place.

The tanner notices Pell before the others do. He looks him over once from the pit, the way a man looks at one more wagon turning in at his gate, and goes back to work.

"That's the lot of you, then," he says, to nobody in particular. "Mind my ditch."

Tobin turns at the voice and brightens. "There he is. Told you he'd come," he says to Hedda.

"You told me nothing," Hedda says. She looks at Pell, at his boots, wet to the ankle with dew, and at the hand that's nowhere near a blade. Then she gives him one short nod.

Pell crosses the mud and gives Bram his report close at his shoulder, tucked under the hammers from the Coast Way. He covers the south post, the scuff on the top rail and the crust of boot mud. He describes the single trodden track running straight down to the dark patch, crossing the sag and never coming back up, and the dew lying over the man's trail but not over the run. Last, the sheep, which haven't put their heads down once.

Hedda is close enough to catch the end of it. She doesn't ask for the rest. She looks down at Bram's curve in the mud, then out through the gap in the hurdles toward the pasture you can't see from here, and keeps her own counsel.

In the pit, the tanner heaves the hide over. The sun is clear of the roofs and the morning is getting on. Pell's report is waiting on Bram.

 *(Out of game: neither action needed a roll. Pell looking over the yard is routine for him, and the scene showed what he saw. His report to Bram is plain talk, and he gave it in full; Bram's answer is up to Bram's player.)*
````


Together after: [Pell Dunmore, Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin]

## Round 16

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Sefa first.

"A clear line's what the curve is for. You'll be behind us and up the slope, with nothing between you and the furrow but grass." He thinks. "If it gets its jaws on someone, that's when I want your push. They hold on hard, and a shove off the wet ground does more than another cut."

To Hedda: "I've two more javelins up in my room. They're yours this afternoon. Throw them first, then keep your spear." Then, dry, to Tobin: "Long stick's fine. Your job's to find hollow ground and shout. If it comes up near you, you don't hold it, you back off onto the boards and let the rest of us hold it."

When Pell comes in through the gateway, Bram waits until he's close enough for low voices.

"Ruddock pays eight gold when it's dead, and first offer on the shell. Early afternoon, after Venn's yard. Boards on the hard ground, the ewe staked below it." He sets off up the lane toward the Coast Way, walking with them. "Did the verge tell you anything? Did it come across the lane in the night, or has it stayed in the field?"

He listens to Pell all the way back. At the Burning Wizard he glances into the stable yard before he goes in, to see whether Corby's horse is back. Then he finds a table with his back to the wall and asks whoever's serving for whatever's hot, and lots of it.

Over the food, he says to the table at large:

"After this, Venn's yard. I want to look at the axles and the yokes before Tam's boys load anything, and see what spare wheels and grease he's carrying. If a wagon breaks down on that road, we're stood still where somebody's already taken two loads." He tears bread. "Then the field. Then supper, early bed. We roll at first light tomorrow."

*(Out of game: Bram fetches the two spare javelins from his room after eating and gives them to Hedda.)*

Sefa Marrin: “Breakfast sounds good. And I’ll come look at the wagons afterward.”

I take another look at the arc. “If it spits, I’ll stand off to one side behind you, not directly behind anyone. The boards may give us firm footing, but I’d rather not count on hearing it dig in time.”

At the gateway I pause as Pell arrives. “We’re heading back for food. Did you find anything along the verge?”

I walk back with the others once he’s ready, listening to what he found.

Pell Dunmore: Pell takes the tanner's measure the same way he took the yard: without moving his head, without lingering anywhere. Something about the bowed shoulders of the man over the hides and the way his eyes stay on his work puts Pell's back to the wall-side, though there is no real wall. He keeps himself set at an angle that shows him the hurdle gate and Bram both.

"Morning to you too," he calls back to the tanner, pleasant, as if the man had said it to him personally. "I'll mind the ditch."

Then, lower, to Bram and whoever's near enough: "They'll go down soon. Whichever way we do it, the boy's pole finds the roof of it—tap along the lane side, stay off the sag." A short, unhurried look out through the gap of the hurdle. "You drag those sheep out of that corner before we cross the grass. Frighten them, they'll scatter, and one will be over a soft patch in the wrong field."

His eyes find Sefa. "Just know whether an ankheg is the kind of thing that waits through the day after it's killed, or goes deep by sunup. You'd best decide how long we can wait."

He then looks back at Ruddock, and raises his voice. "Can we borrow a lantern and a pot of water? We want to see what we're walking into before one of us ends up in your next hole."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram tells Sefa her place first, and she gives him hers back. She'll stand off to one side, behind the curve but not behind any one person. Hedda hears it and gives a short nod.

"Off to the side. Good."

When Bram offers his two spare javelins, Hedda takes her time answering.

"Two." She thinks it over. "I throw them, they come back to you after. Whatever's left of them."

Tobin gets his orders and repeats them under his breath like a lesson: "Find it. Shout. Back off onto the boards." Then, louder: "I can do that."

"You'll do that," Hedda says. "Tob. Nothing else."

---

Pell's *morning to you too* goes over the yard. Ruddock answers it with a grunt and doesn't look up from the hide. He does look up when Pell talks about the sheep. Pell said it low, for Bram, but the tanner has been listening to strangers in his yard all morning and he doesn't stop now.

"My sheep stay where they are." The pole points out at the gap in the hurdles, toward the pasture. "That corner's the top of the hard ground. They've packed into the safest spot in the field. Drive them off it and you'll do the beast's work for it." He heaves the hide over. "They've more sense than most."

When Pell raises his voice for the lantern and the pot of water, Ruddock squints up at the sky over the forge roof. The sun is well clear of it now.

"It's broad day." He doesn't ask what the lantern is for. "There's a horn one on the shed post. Water's the pump, and it's free. There's a pail beside it. You'll have both with the boards this afternoon, and they come back with the boards."

He gives a short bark of a laugh and goes back to the pit.

Nobody answers Pell's question to Sefa: does it lie up through the day after a kill, or go deep by sunup? Sefa still has nothing from the hospice books on it.

---

Bram sets off up the lane toward the Coast Way. Sefa falls in beside him, with Hedda and Tobin behind, the pike shaft bobbing over their heads. The stink fades a little with every step past the forge wall, but it stays in their clothes. Pell is still standing in the mud of the tanner's yard as the bend takes them out of sight.

The Coast Way is properly awake now. Every forge on it is going, a dray loaded with charcoal is grinding north, and a dog is barking at the dray's wheels. At the Burning Wizard, Bram looks into the stable yard on his way to the door. The stall that stood open at dawn still stands open. The straw is clean and swept back, and the stall is empty. The ostler is mucking out the next one along and doesn't look round.

Inside, the common room smells of porridge and last night's ale. Orla is up and behind the bar with her sleeves rolled, and her voice reaches every corner. Bram gets a table against the wall. She comes over when he asks for something hot, and lots of it.

"Porridge, fried bread and bacon. If you mean lots, three silver." She looks at the rest of them. Her eyes stop on Tobin's collar, still pulled up over his nose. Then she sniffs. "You've been at Ruddock's. Sit by the door." She doesn't make them move. "And you?"

Hedda takes the porridge, one silver. Tobin takes the porridge with bacon on top for two, and Hedda lets him.

Bram has the plan on the table while the bowls are still steaming: Venn's yard first for the axles, yokes, spare wheels and grease, then the field, then supper and an early bed. Hedda eats with her spoon and listens with the rest of her.

"Spare wheel," she says. "Venn'll say he's one. Ask to see it."

When the bowls are empty, Bram goes up the stairs and comes down with the two javelins from beside his cot. He holds them out shaft first, and Hedda takes them. She weighs one and sights along it at the shutter. Then she slings them both through her belt beside the spear.

"They'll do."

It's a little past half seven. Venn won't be in his yard until the oxen come in at mid-morning. The Thunderhammer is two streets off, and its hammer is already going.

*(Out of game: Orla wants Bram's three silver, and Sefa still owes her for whatever she orders. Bram's player may want to remember that Col said on his doorstep he ran straight down that slope after Bess yesterday. Pell is still in Ruddock's yard and can catch up at the inn. Sefa never heard his report from the verge.)*

 *(Out of game: none of these four needed a roll.)*
1. Telling Sefa where to stand is an order between characters. The scene covered it, and she gave him her own spot back: off to one side.
2. Bram's question to Pell belongs to Pell's player to answer. Pell already gave Bram the verge report at his shoulder in the yard: nothing crossed the lane in the night, and the dew was unbroken both ways. I didn't show Bram asking it again, because Pell stayed behind in Ruddock's yard.
3. Looking into the stable yard is a routine look. The scene told it: Corby's stall is still open and empty, with the straw swept back.
4. Ordering breakfast and laying out the plan is plain talk. The scene played both. Orla wants three silver for the large breakfast, and Bram can pay her when he likes.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Sefa answers you: "Breakfast sounds good. And I'll come look at the wagons afterward." She looks at the arc again. "If it spits, I'll stand off to one side behind you, not directly behind anyone. The boards may give us firm footing, but I'd rather not count on hearing it dig in time."

Hedda hears it and gives a short nod. "Off to the side. Good."

When you offer the two spare javelins, Hedda takes her time answering. "Two." She thinks it over. "I throw them, they come back to you after. Whatever's left of them."

Tobin repeats his orders under his breath like a lesson: "Find it. Shout. Back off onto the boards." Then, louder: "I can do that."

"You'll do that," Hedda says. "Tob. Nothing else."

Pell keeps himself at an angle to the yard, where he can see the hurdle gate and you both. "Morning to you too," he calls to the tanner, pleasantly. "I'll mind the ditch." Ruddock grunts and doesn't look up from the hide.

Then Pell speaks low to you and whoever is near: "They'll go down soon. Whichever way we do it, the boy's pole finds the roof of it. Tap along the lane side, stay off the sag." He takes a short, unhurried look out through the gap in the hurdles. "You drag those sheep out of that corner before we cross the grass. Frighten them, they'll scatter, and one will be over a soft patch in the wrong field." He looks at Sefa. "Just know whether an ankheg is the kind of thing that waits through the day after it's killed, or goes deep by sunup. You'd best decide how long we can wait."

Pell spoke low, but Ruddock has been listening to strangers in his yard all morning. He looks up at the bit about the sheep. "My sheep stay where they are." His pole points out through the gap in the hurdles, toward the pasture. "That corner's the top of the hard ground. They've packed into the safest spot in the field. Drive them off it and you'll do the beast's work for it." He heaves the hide over. "They've more sense than most."

Pell raises his voice: "Can we borrow a lantern and a pot of water? We want to see what we're walking into before one of us ends up in your next hole."

Ruddock squints up at the sky over the forge roof. The sun is well clear of it now. "It's broad day." He doesn't ask what the lantern is for. "There's a horn one on the shed post. Water's the pump, and it's free. There's a pail beside it. You'll have both with the boards this afternoon, and they come back with the boards." He gives a short bark of a laugh and goes back to the pit.

Nobody answers Pell's question to Sefa.

You set off up the lane toward the Coast Way. Sefa falls in beside you, with Hedda and Tobin behind, the pike shaft bobbing over their heads. The stink fades a little with every step past the forge wall, but it stays in your clothes. Pell is still standing in the mud of the tanner's yard when the bend takes him out of sight.

The Coast Way is properly awake now. Every forge on it is going, a dray loaded with charcoal is grinding north, and a dog is barking at its wheels. At the Burning Wizard you look into the stable yard on your way to the door. The stall that stood open at dawn still stands open. The straw is clean and swept back, and the stall is empty. The ostler is mucking out the next one along and doesn't look round.

Inside, the common room smells of porridge and last night's ale. Orla is behind the bar with her sleeves rolled, and her voice reaches every corner. You get a table against the wall. When you ask for something hot, and lots of it, she comes over.

"Porridge, fried bread and bacon. If you mean lots, three silver." She looks at the rest of you. Her eyes stop on Tobin's collar, still pulled up over his nose. Then she sniffs. "You've been at Ruddock's. Sit by the door." She doesn't make you move. "And you?"

Hedda takes the porridge, one silver. Tobin takes the porridge with bacon on top for two, and Hedda lets him. Orla wants your three silver whenever you care to pay.

Hedda eats with her spoon while you lay out the plan, and she listens with the rest of her. "Spare wheel," she says. "Venn'll say he's one. Ask to see it."

When the bowls are empty, you go upstairs and come down with the two javelins from beside your cot. You hold them out shaft first, and Hedda takes them. She weighs one and sights along it at the shutter. Then she slings them both through her belt beside the spear. "They'll do."

It's a little past half seven. Venn won't be in his yard until the oxen come in at mid-morning. The Thunderhammer is two streets off, and its hammer is already going.
````

## Round 17

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram counts three silver onto the table for Orla when she comes past.

"Mine. Hedda and the lad settle their own." He nods at the pair without unkindness; it's how sellswords keep accounts straight. Then, as she gathers the coins: "Stall your ostler swept this morning. That was Corby's horse? Did he say where he was riding, or when he'd be back?" If she shrugs, he lets it go.

He looks round the table.

"Pell stayed behind at Ruddock's. He'll find us or he won't, as Hedda says." Then, getting up and slinging his shield: "Venn's not in till the oxen come, but wagons don't need him to be looked at. I'm walking over to the yard now. If his wagons are standing there, I'll start on them before Tam's boys start loading."

At the yard behind the Thunderhammer he goes over the wagons the way he would at any hiring yard, slowly, one at a time: crawls under to look at axles for cracks and wear, spins wheels to listen for a dry hub, checks spokes, felloes and tyres for loose iron, tugs the kingpins, looks over the yokes and bows for splits, and counts what's carried spare: wheels, axle, grease pot, rope, chain, a jack. Taking Hedda's advice, he wants to *see* the spare wheel, not hear about it.

If anyone is about, a yard boy or Tam himself, Bram introduces himself plainly and asks which wagon is the heaviest loaded and which has given trouble before.

*(Out of game: Bram's main concern is anything that would leave a wagon stopped on the road between Lark's Ford and Kesh's. If he finds a fault, he notes it to raise with Venn when he arrives. Also, a quick check: my carried javelins should now be 2, with Hedda holding the other 2 for the afternoon.)*

Sefa Marrin: “Porridge and bacon for me, please.” I put two silver on the table for Orla.

Over breakfast, I tell Bram, “I couldn’t answer Pell’s question about whether it stays near the surface by day. I don’t know enough about ankhegs. If it doesn’t take the bait this afternoon, let’s not go hunting through its tunnels to force the matter.”

Once we’ve eaten, I go with the others to the Thunderhammer.

“I’ll leave judging axles to you,” I tell Bram. “I’d like to check the drivers have drinking water and something clean for dressing wounds. Ruddock’s ditch was a useful reminder that not every injury comes from teeth.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla stops at the table with a cloth over one shoulder. Bram counts three silver onto the boards for her.

"Mine," he says. "Hedda and the lad settle their own."

Hedda is already putting hers down: one silver for herself and two for Tob. She slides them over without a word. Orla scoops up all six coins and drops them into her apron without counting, as she always does. Sefa orders porridge and bacon and lays two silver beside them. Orla takes those as well.

"Pot's on. It'll be quick." When Bram asks about the stall, she shrugs with one shoulder. "Corby? He paid through tonight and he owes me nothing. Stripped his own cot, which is more than most do. Said nothing to me, but I was still abed." She tips her head toward the back door and the stable yard. "Wat saddled him. If anyone saw which way he turned, it was Wat."

She's gone before anyone can ask anything else. The kitchen girl brings Sefa's bowl a minute later. It's the same grey porridge with two rashers laid across it, still spitting.

Bram gets up and slings his shield. Sefa gets her piece in before he goes: she couldn't answer Pell's question, she doesn't know enough about ankhegs, and if the ewe doesn't bring it up this afternoon, nobody goes down its tunnels after it. Hedda hears it from across the table.

"Nobody goes down holes," she says. "Agreed."

Tob looks as though he'd like to argue, then looks at his porridge.

---

Venn's yard is two streets off, behind the Thunderhammer's back wall. The forge has a full fire going now, and heat comes off the bricks as Bram passes. The yard gate stands open. Inside there's a square of packed earth with ox stalls along one side and a lean-to shed along the other. The shed is stacked to the rafters with salt in sacks under a tarpaulin, and wool bales are roped beside it. Four wagons stand in a row with their tongues down. Their beds are swept and empty.

The only person about is a boy of about fourteen, mucking out the ox stalls with a fork that's taller than he is. He straightens up when he sees the mail and shield, and he clearly doesn't know what to do with his hands.

"Master Venn's not in yet," he says. "Davy. I keep the yard." He jerks his chin at the shed. "I sleep in with the salt now. Since the second lot."

When Bram asks which wagon is heaviest and which has given trouble, Davy answers quickly, as if he's been waiting all spring for someone to ask.

"Tam's. The big one at the end, she takes the salt. The rest take wool, and wool's light." He points the fork at the third wagon in the row. It's older than the others, and its sides were painted brown once. "The old brown. Master Venn drives her himself. She threw her near hind tyre on the Gate road at the thaw, and he had the Thunderhammer put new iron on. Cost him, he said. Says it every time he looks at her."

Bram works down the row slowly, the way he has at a hundred hiring yards.

Tam's wagon is sound. The axles are dry-clean and free of cracks, and the hubs are packed with grease. Each wheel he spins runs quiet and true. The kingpin doesn't move when he pulls on it. Someone who knows what he's doing has kept this wagon, and kept it for years.

The second and fourth wagons are sound too, if more tired. One spoke on the fourth has a short split along the grain, but it's tight in both the hub and the felloe and will hold. The yokes and bows are old ash with no splits, worn smooth by necks. There's nothing in them to stop a wagon on the road.

The old brown is the one that matters.

The new tyre on the near hind wheel is good Thunderhammer work, shrunk on tight and sweated in. Bram crawls underneath on his back in the dirt and looks along the axle-tree. Where the near arm meets the body, on the shoulder, a hairline crack runs about a hand's length into the timber. It isn't new. Old grease has worked into it and turned it nearly black, so it's easy to miss unless a man is lying under the wagon looking for it. With wool aboard, on the Way, the axle might go all the way to Nashkel and back. Put the wagon in a deep rut at speed, or let her drop off a ford bank, and that arm could shear. Someone spent good money on the tyre and never looked under the bed.

Then he looks at what they carry spare. There are two coils of rope and one length of chain. The grease pot is half full. There's no jack, only a long lever pole and an oak block lashed under Tam's bed.

There is a spare wheel, standing against the back of the shed behind the wool. Bram rolls it out into the light. It's sound, freshly tyred and well greased. It's also a hand's breadth smaller across than any hind wheel in the yard. It's a fore wheel. It will fit the front axle of any of the four wagons and none of the backs.

---

Sefa finishes her porridge and comes over with Hedda and Tob about a quarter of an hour behind him. Bram is still crawling under the wagons. The forge noise comes over the wall, a steady double ring.

Davy watches the three of them come in and looks even less sure what to do with his hands.

Sefa leaves the axles to Bram and goes along the wagons looking for something else. Tam's wagon and the old brown each have a small water cask lashed to the side, and both are empty and smell of nothing. The other two have pegs where casks have hung, but no casks. Under Tam's driving seat there's a box with a lid. Davy says it's all right to look. Inside she finds a jar of goose grease, a hoof pick, a twist of horsehair cord and one grey rag, folded. She finds no linen anywhere else in the yard.

"Tam fills the casks at the pump the morning they go," Davy offers. "He says water in a cask overnight goes off."

Hedda walks to the back of the shed, where the spare wheel is leaning against the wagon Bram rolled it to. She stands it beside the old brown's hind wheel and looks from one to the other. She doesn't say anything. She doesn't need to.

Tob crouches down to see where Bram is lying under the old brown.

"Is it bad?"

The sun has cleared the Thunderhammer's roof now and the yard is warming. Venn, Tam and the oxen aren't due until the middle of the morning, about an hour off.

*(Out of game: yes, Bram is carrying two javelins, and Hedda has the other two for the afternoon.)*
````

Four wagons stand in a row with their tongues down. Bram is crawling under them.

````text
A boy of about fourteen with a manure fork taller than he is watches the three of you come in, and looks as if he doesn't know what to do with his hands.

You leave the axles to Bram and go along the wagons looking for something else. The big wagon at the end and an older one, painted brown once, each have a small water cask lashed to the side. Both casks are empty and smell of nothing. The other two wagons have pegs where casks have hung, but no casks. Under the big wagon's driving seat there's a box with a lid. The boy says it's all right to look. Inside you find a jar of goose grease, a hoof pick, a twist of horsehair cord and one grey rag, folded. You find no linen anywhere else in the yard.

"Tam fills the casks at the pump the morning they go," the boy offers. "He says water in a cask overnight goes off."

A spare wheel is leaning against one of the wagons. Hedda stands it beside the old brown wagon's hind wheel and looks from one to the other. She doesn't say anything. She doesn't need to: the spare is plainly smaller across.

Tob crouches down to see where Bram is lying under the old brown.

"Is it bad?"

The sun has cleared the Thunderhammer's roof now and the yard is warming. Venn, Tam and the oxen aren't due until the middle of the morning, about an hour off.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin]

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets the others get a few steps up the lane before he follows. He doesn't move after them right away. Instead he walks to the edge of the nearest pit, out of the way of Ruddock's pole, and hooks his thumbs into his belt.

"That boot mark on the south post," he says, not loud. "Mud on the rail, and a straight track down to the hole that doesn't come back. Was that you, after the dog?"

He watches the tanner's hands, not his face. "I'm not asking to lay blame. I want to know if someone else was on your ground in the dark, and whether he went down after the beast or after whatever it left."

A pause. He lets the stink settle around him. "If it wasn't you, then you've got a visitor with heavy boots and no wish to use the gate."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ruddock doesn't stop working while Pell asks. The pole goes down into the scum, catches the hide, turns it over and goes down again, steady as a pump handle. Pell watches the stained hands on the pole. They don't tighten at *boot mark*, and they don't stop at *in the dark*. At *a visitor* the pole stops halfway out of the water and stays there, dripping.

"Visitor." Ruddock looks up at him from the pit, the way he'd look at a hide with a hole in the wrong place. "That's Col."

He lets the pole sink back in.

"Yesterday afternoon, not the dark. His bitch went down that hole and he went over the rail at the bottom post after her. That's the post nearest his door. He ran straight down, he told me so at my pump while he was scrubbing his arm raw. Carter's boots, hobnails, and fifteen stone of him in them. He'd leave a track across a cobbled street." He heaves the hide over. "He never came back up the same way. He came round by the ditch side to me, holding that arm out in front of him like it was somebody else's."

The pole goes down and up again.

"Nobody comes on my ground in the dark. There's nothing on it but sheep and stink, and the stink you get for free." He nods at the lane past the gateway, where the others have gone round the forge wall out of sight. "If you want to know what he saw down there, ask him. He's at the end of that lane. Your big friend's already been."

Then he goes back to his hide as if the matter's settled, because for him it is.

Up the lane, the sound of the others' boots has faded under the hammers on the Coast Way. Only Pell is left in the yard now, with the tanner and the slap of the pole. Nothing else needs him here unless he wants it to.
````

## Round 18

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram slides out from under the old brown on his back, sits up in the dirt, and wipes black grease off his thumb onto his breeches.

"Not bad yet," he tells Tob. "That's the trouble with it." He taps the axle-tree where it meets the body. "Crack in the shoulder of the near arm, a hand long, old. She'd likely go to Nashkel and back on the Way with wool aboard. Drop her off a ford bank, or put her in a deep rut at speed, and that arm shears. Then she's on her belly in the road, the load's stuck, and we're stood still exactly where somebody's been waiting." He gets to his feet. "Lark's Ford has a bank."

He nods at Hedda and the two wheels side by side, his mouth twisting dryly.

"Aye. One spare, and it fits the front. Venn's got a wheel the way some men have a sword they've never drawn."

To Davy, plainly, not unkindly:

"You're not in trouble, lad. You keep a clean yard. Two things, and you'll know better than me. When the second lot went, did anyone come round the yard beforehand? A man asking questions about the route, or the day, or wanting work and not getting it? And the six wagons on Rusk's load, were they Master Venn's own, or hired?"

Whatever Davy says, Bram listens. Then he turns to the others.

"I grew up in a smithy yard. A crack like that, a smith can strap. Iron plate either side of the shoulder, bolted through, and she'll carry anything. That's a morning's work if the fire's already hot, and the Thunderhammer's is." He jerks his head at the wall, where the double ring is going. "I'm going round to ask what they'd want for it, and whether they've a hind wheel to fit, or can tyre one by tomorrow's first light. Then I'll have a price to put in front of Venn when he walks in, not just bad news. A man who's lost ten wagons takes bad news better with a number on it."

To Sefa, with a nod at the casks: "Good. Make sure there's clean linen in that box, and that Tam knows it's there. If he doesn't, it isn't."

He goes out through the yard gate and round to the Thunderhammer's front. He waits for a pause in the hammering, then asks whoever is running the forge. He describes the crack exactly (near hind arm, at the shoulder, hairline, a hand long, old grease in it) and asks two things: what to strap it, today, and what for a hind wheel to match Venn's wagons, iron on, by first light tomorrow.

*(Out of game: Bram just wants the prices and whether it can be done in time. He won't commit Venn's money; he'll take the figures back to the yard and be there when Venn and the oxen arrive.)*

Sefa Marrin: “I’m Sefa,” I tell the boy. “I’ll be travelling with them tomorrow. What’s your name?”

I put the box back as I found it. “Two wagons are missing water casks. Do you know whether Tam has them stored somewhere? And how many days is it between reliable places to fill them?”

To Hedda and Bram, I say, “There’s no clean dressing linen here, unless Tam brings his own kit. I’ll buy some today. Magic’s useful, but I can’t spend it on every scrape.”

I keep clear of the wagon Bram’s under. “Once you’ve finished, let’s make Venn one list: what needs fixing, what needs replacing, and what we need to carry. Easier for him to settle it while we’re all still in town.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram slides out from under the old brown on his back, sits up in the dirt and wipes the black grease off his thumb onto his breeches.

Tob takes in every word, crouched by the wheel. When Bram gets to *Lark's Ford has a bank*, the boy looks at the axle again with a different face.

"So it's fine till it isn't," he says.

Hedda doesn't laugh at the line about the sword. She lays the fore wheel down flat on the packed earth, as if she has finished with it, and folds her arms.

Davy stands up straighter when Bram tells him he's not in trouble. He answers the first question slowly. He's going back over the spring in his head.

"Nobody asking about the road. Not that I saw. There was the Fist lad, though. The one Master Venn paid to ride to Nashkel after the first lot. He came round twice after that, asking when the next lot went." He scuffs the fork in the straw. "I think he wanted paying to ride with it. Master Venn said he couldn't afford him and sent him off. He's not been since."

The second question he answers fast.

"Master Venn's, all but two. Two he hired in the Gate. He's still paying the hire on them, he says so at supper." He hesitates. "He says it a lot."

---

Sefa gives the boy her name and asks for his.

"Davy," he says, and goes red, as though he's already said it once this morning. He has, but not to her.

He watches her put the box back under Tam's seat exactly as she found it. About the casks, he points the fork at the empty pegs on the two middle wagons.

"Those two went with Rusk's lot. On her wagons. There's one more in the back of the shed, but it weeps at the seam. Tam won't have it." To her question about water on the road, he shrugs. "I've never been further than Lark's. Tam says there's the ford, and Kesh's well, and nothing else he'd put in his mouth."

When Sefa says she'll buy linen today, Hedda nods once.

"Buy enough for two of us down at once," she says. "Not one."

Sefa's list gets more out of her than anything else has this morning.

"Wheel goes at the top of it."

---

Bram goes out through the yard gate and round the soot-black wall to the Thunderhammer's front. The forge stands open to the street, and the heat comes out of it like a hand pushing at his chest. Two anvils are going. At the far one a big man with soot to the elbows is drawing out a bar. Bram knows him. He's the smith who pushed the black stone back across the table last night and walked out without the purse. He doesn't look up. If he knows Bram's face from the common room, he doesn't show it.

At the near anvil a grizzled man in a scorched leather apron sets down his hammer in the gap between heats. He listens with his tongs still in his hand. When Bram says *a hand long, old grease in it*, he nods slowly, like a man who has seen that crack a hundred times.

"Strap either side the shoulder, bolted through. Aye." He thinks. "Two gold and a half, if it's what you say. I'll tell you true once I've had her on her side. Get her round here by noon, empty, and she'll be done by dusk." He spits into the slack tub, and it hisses. "Hind wheel, I can't help you. We don't build wheels, we tyre them. Ames the wheelwright, three doors down toward the temple, keeps a few standing for the Gate wagons. If he's got one to fit, bring it here this afternoon and I'll shrink iron on it for a gold. His wheel's his price, not mine."

He picks up the tongs again. The bar in the fire has gone the colour he wants.

"Gethin," he says, by way of a name, and goes back to work.

Behind him the big smith at the far anvil goes on hammering and never looks round. It's a little after nine. Venn and the oxen are due in about an hour.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets out a short breath through his nose—not quite a laugh, but near enough.

"Col. That fits. Fifteen stone with hobnails, straight off the rail." He nods once to himself. "I saw the track and wondered which fool or which visitor it was. Turns out it was the one man in town with a reason." He steps back from the pit to give the tanner his workroom. "I'll ask him, then. And the lantern and water still come back when the boards come back."

Then he turns and goes up the lane at an unhurried walk, keeping to the ruts, moving so quietly that he's gone round the forge wall without any sound.

He catches up with the others before the inn, sliding into step a little behind and to the side. "Boot mark's answered. It was Col in daylight, going down after the dog, then round by the ditch to Ruddock's pump with his arm burned." He lets that hang. "So the hole was there by yesterday afternoon, and the thing was still under the grass when the carter crossed it. If he got back to the gate alive with his arm shredded, it means the ground held him, or it was already gone. The sheep know it's not far. We'll see which."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ruddock doesn't look up when Pell mentions the lantern and the pail. The pole goes into the scum, turns the hide, and comes up again.

"They come back with the boards," he says. "I said."

That's all he has to say. Pell steps back from the pit edge and leaves the tanner to his work. He goes out through the hurdle gateway and round the soot-black wall of the forge with no more sound than the steam coming off the pits.

The others aren't anywhere on the lane behind the forge, and they aren't on the Coast Way either. The road is full of the morning now. A charcoal dray is grinding north, smoke and sparks are coming out of open forge fronts, and a dog is trotting along beside a cart wheel looking pleased with itself. Pell walks the length of it to the Burning Wizard and sees nobody he knows among the carters and smiths' boys. His piece about Col, the daylight and the ground holding stays unsaid. There's nobody beside him to say it to.

Through the open stable gate he can see Wat the ostler with a barrow and fork, mucking out the stall next to the empty one. The empty stall's straw is still swept back clean. Wat doesn't look round.

Inside, breakfast is nearly over. The common room smells of porridge and fried bread. A few carters sit over their bowls, and the kitchen girl is stacking empties by the hearth. Orla is behind the bar wiping out a pot. She looks him over once and sniffs. The tan-pit smell has come in with him, the way it came in with the others.

"You're the last of them," she says. "The big one ate and went off to Venn's yard. The one with the book went after him with the two sellswords. That's behind the Thunderhammer, two streets that way." She points with the wet cloth. "Porridge is a silver if you want it. There's bacon left if you're quick."

She waits a moment for his answer, then goes back to the pot.

*(Out of game: the others' scene had already taken them through breakfast and on to Venn's yard by now, so Pell couldn't catch them before the inn. I've brought him to the inn at the same hour as them, a little after nine. If you'd rather Pell spent that gap doing something in particular, tell me.)*
````
