## Round 19

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Gethin's back. "Two and a half, by noon, empty. I'll bring you an answer before then."

He looks once at the far anvil. The big smith is still drawing out his bar and still not looking round. Bram lets him be. A man who turned down a full purse last night has earned the right to do his work in peace. Bram files the face away and goes.

Three doors down toward the temple he finds Ames the wheelwright. He describes what he needs plainly: a hind wheel to match Venn's wagons, the same diameter as the old brown's near hind and the same hub bore. He offers to walk Ames round to measure it if that's easier than guessing.

"Two questions. Have you one standing that'll fit? And what's it cost, wheel alone, with Gethin putting the iron on this afternoon?"

He doesn't buy anything yet. He gets the number, and whether the wheel can be at the Thunderhammer by early afternoon. Then he walks back to the yard.

There he crouches by the old brown's wheel with Sefa, Hedda and Tob, and lays out the list before Venn arrives:

"The axle strap's two and a half gold. The old brown goes round to Gethin by noon, empty, and she's done by dusk. The hind wheel is whatever Ames said, plus a gold for the iron. Then the two missing water casks and dressing linen." He glances at Hedda. "Enough for two down at once. And a jack, or at least a second lever and block, so we're not stuck under one wagon while another's sitting in the road."

Then, quieter, to the three of them:

"One more thing. Davy says the Fist lad Venn paid to ride to Nashkel after the first load came round twice afterward, asking when the next lot went. Wanting to ride with it, he thought." He lets that sit. "Could be a lad after wages. Could be a lad who knew what day Rusk was going. I'll ask Venn his name."

He stands, brushes the yard dirt off his knees, and waits by the gate for the oxen and their owner.

*(Out of game: if there's time before Venn arrives, Bram also asks Davy whether the Fist lad was at the yard the morning Rusk loaded.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Gethin doesn't look up when Bram answers. He gives one nod at the bar in the fire, which is all the answer a tradesman needs. At the far anvil the big smith goes on drawing out his iron. Bram looks once at the soot-black forearms and the steady hammer, notes the face and leaves him to his work.

---

Ames the wheelwright's is three doors down toward the temple. It's a deep, open-fronted shop that smells of fresh-cut ash. The floor is curled thick with shavings. Wheels hang on pegs along the back wall like shields in a hall, and a half-built one sits in the wheel pit with only six of its spokes in. Ames is a long, stooped man of sixty or so with a grey beard cut short, so it won't catch in anything. He works a spokeshave along a length of oak and keeps at it while Bram talks. He stops at *same hub bore*.

"Venn's lot. Gate-built, the four of them, out of Hobb's yard by the Basilisk Gate, all but his old brown." He sets down the spokeshave. "I've three hind wheels standing at Gate track. Whether one's right for her I'll not guess. I'll come and see."

He takes a notched ash rod off a nail and follows Bram back round the Thunderhammer wall into the yard. There he kneels in the dirt by the old brown's near hind wheel. He lays the rod across the wheel's face, then puts two fingers into the end of the hub and feels the bore like a man checking a horse's teeth. He's quiet about it, and quick. He looks along the axle arm while he's down there, and his eyebrows go up at the dark seam on the shoulder. He doesn't say anything about it. That's Gethin's trade, not his.

"Middle one of my three," he says, getting up off his knees. "Elm hub, oak spokes, ash felloes, seasoned two winters. Three gold, wheel alone, no iron. I'll roll it round to Gethin myself by noon if it's sold, and he can have the iron on it by mid-afternoon." He tucks the rod under his arm. "If it's not sold, it stays on my peg, and I'll not hold it past tomorrow for a man who hasn't paid."

He goes back to his oak without waiting to hear more.

---

Hedda and Tob are in the yard by the old brown where Bram left them. Bram crouches by the wheel and puts the list together out loud: two and a half for the strap, the wagon to Gethin by noon and empty, done by dusk. Three for Ames's wheel and one more for the iron. Then the two missing casks, the linen, and a jack or a second lever and block.

Hedda counts it on her fingers without hiding it.

"Six and a half gold, before casks." She looks over at the shed, where the wool is roped up beside the salt. "He'll grumble at six and a half. He'll pay it. He'll grumble at the jack and not pay it."

"There's a lever and block already," Tob says. "Under Tam's."

"One. For four wagons."

Then Bram lowers his voice and tells them about the Fist lad who came round twice asking when the next load went. Tob's eyes go wide at once. He looks at the yard gate as if the lad might be standing in it.

Hedda doesn't look at the gate. She rubs her thumb once over the old brand on her neck and doesn't seem to notice she's doing it.

"Fist lads ask after paid riding. All of them. Pay's late, so they ask anybody with wagons." She drops the hand. "Some of them ask for other people as well. Get his name."

---

Davy has gone back to mucking out, keeping half an ear on everything said near the wagons. When Bram asks his question, he leans on the fork and thinks hard about it, the way he did before.

"Not the morning she loaded. That was just Rusk's lot and the two from the stockyards and Master Venn." He frowns into the straw. "The second time he came was before that. Three days, maybe four. Master Venn was in the shed counting sacks, and he told him straight out. *She goes Fourthday, and I can't afford you on top of five.* He went off after that. I didn't see which way." He scuffs the fork. "Master Venn says the day to anybody who asks. He did last night as well. At Orla's."

Before anyone can answer, the street outside the yard fills up with the slow knock of hooves on packed earth and a man's voice calling *hup, hup, steady*. Through the open gate the first pair of oxen come round the corner, big and dun and swinging their heads, and then a second pair behind them. Walking beside the lead pair, with a goad over his shoulder, is a square grey man in a carter's smock who doesn't look as though he's spoken all morning. Behind the last pair, ledger under his arm and his sheepskin collar turned up against the cold, comes Aldous Venn.

He sees Bram at the gate, then the fore wheel lying flat in the dirt, then Hedda, who is plainly holding a sum in her head.

"Well," he says. "That's a face with a number on it."

*(Out of game: nothing here needed a roll. Sefa's part in the yard comes in her own scene's message.)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “Enough for two,” I agree with Hedda.

I turn back to Davy. “Do you know the Fist rider’s name, or where we could find him? We may want to ask what he saw on his ride. No trouble if you don’t remember.”

Before leaving, I ask Hedda, “Will you tell Venn about the casks when he comes? We need sound ones for those empty pegs, and Tam’s judgement on how much water to carry—not that leaking one pressed into service at the last minute.”

Then I head out to find a shop selling clean linen, soap, and a small pair of scissors, asking directions at the forge if necessary. I want enough cloth for several dressings on two wounded people, not just a strip for a cut, and I ask the price before buying.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
"Enough for two," Sefa says, and Hedda nods once, as if that settles it.

Davy has been half-listening from the ox stalls the whole time. When Sefa asks about the Fist rider he leans on his fork, and his face goes the way it did for Bram's questions while he goes back over it.

"Never heard his name. Master Venn just called him *the lad from the post*." He scuffs the straw. "The Fist keep a house up the Way, toward the temple. There's a red fist painted over the door. He'd be from there. Master Venn'd know his name, though. He paid him."

He doesn't make a fuss about not knowing, and Sefa doesn't press him.

When Sefa asks about the casks, Hedda doesn't answer right away. Out in the street the hooves are coming closer, a slow knock on packed earth, and a man is calling *hup, hup, steady*. Hedda tips her chin at the gate as the first pair of dun oxen swing their heads in through it, and at the square grey man walking beside them with a goad over his shoulder.

"I'll tell him," she says. "Tam's the one to ask how much water. Not that one." She means the weeping cask at the back of the shed. "Nobody's putting that on a peg."

Tob steps back from the old brown to give the oxen room. He watches them come in with frank interest, and he holds the pike shaft upright out of the way.

Sefa goes out as the second pair comes in. She keeps to the wall to let the beasts pass. Aldous Venn is behind the last pair, with the ledger under his arm and his sheepskin collar turned up against the cold. He gives her a short nod at the gateway, the kind a man gives someone he's already agreed terms with, and then his eyes go past her into the yard.

---

Davy had called the directions after her before she reached the gate: "Ashby's. On the Way, by the well. She does cloth and candles and everything." Sefa finds it easily. It's a narrow shop with its shutters propped open on the Coast Way, between a cooper's and a forge, opposite a stone well-head where two women are winding up a bucket. Inside it smells of tallow and wool. Shelves run to the ceiling: candles in bundles, lamp wicks, twine, cakes of soap stacked like bricks, folded cloth. Bolt ends of cloth are stuffed into pigeonholes.

The woman behind the counter is in her forties, thin and quick, with spectacles pushed up into greying hair. She is measuring twine against brass tacks hammered into the counter edge. She looks up when Sefa comes in, looks a little longer than a shopkeeper usually does, and then decides something.

"You'll be the one who did the lad's hand at Orla's," she says. It isn't unfriendly. "My sister's man was on the next bench. He's talked of nothing else since." She puts the twine down. "What can I find you?"

She listens to the list properly: linen for dressings, enough for two people hurt at once and changed more than once, soap, and small scissors. She pulls a bolt end out of a pigeonhole and unrolls a length of it across the counter. It's unbleached country linen, coarse and close-woven, the colour of oatmeal.

"This, not the fine. The fine's for shirts, and you'd be cutting up five gold a yard to bleed on." She runs her thumb across it. "Three silver the yard. For two people and changing, I'd say four yards. It's what the carters' wives buy when they've a man on the road." She reaches behind her without looking and sets a pale cake beside it. "Soap. That's two copper, and it's plain, not lye. Lye's for floors." Then she finds a small pair of iron scissors in a drawer, no longer than her hand, and snips the air with them once to show they cut. "Those are five silver. They came up from the Gate, and I'll not go lower. There's no one in Beregost makes anything that small. They all make horseshoes."

She lines the three things up on the counter and adds it on her fingers.

"Four yards, the soap, the scissors. One gold, seven silver and two copper, the lot. Less if you want less cloth." She pushes her spectacles back up. "I'll cut it now or fold it whole, as you like."
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell takes a seat at the end of the bar, where he can see both the street door and the stairs.

"Porridge, and the bacon if it's still breathing." He counts out a silver and a copper and leaves them on the bar, then lets his voice drop to something that won't carry past the nearest bench. "The grey coat from last night—the ore-seller who had a room up the left side. Did he come down for his breakfast, or go out early? You'd know what's in your own house better than anyone."

He accepts the bowl and plate when they come, but he doesn't fall on them. "I only ask because he listened to a lot of hiring talk and then walked up the stairs not long after Venn sat down. A man who sleeps through breakfast after a night of questions is either rich, or careful, or gone." A short pause. "You'll have seen which."

He eats steadily, aware of the hour, and then wipes his mouth. "If he's still up there, I won't be sorry not to have to find him by the sound of his boots in the dark."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla doesn't look up from the pot until Pell is settled on the stool at the end of the bar. From there he has the street door on one side and the foot of the stairs on the other.

"Still breathing. Just," she says about the bacon. She ladles grey porridge into a bowl, then forks two rashers from the pan at the back of the hearth onto a plate, curled and dark at the edges. "Porridge is a silver. Bacon's another. Two, and pay me when you're done. Same as everyone."

She puts both down in front of him. He drops his voice for the grey coat, and she takes a moment before she answers. She wipes her hands on the cloth, the way she would before handling money.

"Gone. Out before light. He'd paid through tonight and he owes me nothing. Stripped his own cot, so the girl had nothing to do in there but open the shutter." She tips her head at the stable door. "He said nothing to me. I was still abed. Wat saddled him. If anyone saw which way he turned out of the yard, it's Wat."

Pell says the rest: *rich, or careful, or gone*. She hears it out without much on her face.

"Careful," she says. "Tidy, anyway. Pays ahead, breaks nothing, asks for nothing." She picks up the next pot. "That's the second of you this morning wanting to know about him. Your big friend asked the same over his breakfast. I'll tell you what I told him. That's all I know, and I've a kitchen."

She goes off to the kitchen door and leaves him with the bowl.

The porridge is hot and plain, and the bacon is better than it looks. A couple of carters finish at the long bench and go out into the street noise. The kitchen girl carries the empties past. Nobody comes down the stairs. There's nobody up there now but the snorer in the first room, and the loft carters have all gone.

By the time Pell wipes his mouth, it's a little past half nine. Through the open stable door Wat is still at the barrow, forking muck out of the stall next to the empty one. Venn's yard is two streets off behind the Thunderhammer, and from the bar Pell can just hear its double ring over the street.

*(Out of game: Pell has no silver at all. His purse holds 35 copper, 2 electrum and 31 gold, so the two silver Orla wants has to come from those. Which does he pay with?)*
````

## Round 20

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram straightens up from the gate post and nods to the grey man with the goad first.

"Tam Hesketh? Bram Halloway. I'll be walking beside you to Nashkel." He doesn't hold out a hand, since the man has a goad in one and oxen to mind. "If I shout, stop. If I shout twice, get down behind the wagon. That's all I'll ask of you."

Then he turns to Venn. He keeps his voice level and gives it in order, the way he'd read a manifest.

"Two things wrong, both fixable today. The old brown has a crack in the near hind axle arm, at the shoulder. Old, a hand long, grease worked into it. She'll do the Way with wool aboard. Put her off a ford bank and that arm shears, and she's on her belly in the road at Lark's, where somebody's already been waiting twice." He nods at the wheel lying flat. "And your spare's a fore wheel. It won't go on any back axle in the yard."

"Gethin at the Thunderhammer will strap the axle for two and a half gold. He wants her round there empty by noon, and she'll be done by dusk. Ames, three doors down, has a hind wheel to fit, Gate track. He measured her himself. That's three gold for the wheel and one for Gethin's iron, ready mid-afternoon. Six and a half, all told." A beat. "Ames won't hold the wheel past noon unless it's paid for."

"Smaller things. Two water casks went with Rusk's load, and the one in the shed weeps. Sefa's buying dressing linen. And I'd like a second lever and block. One for four wagons means we're stuck if two of them get into trouble at once." He holds up a hand before Venn can object. "That last one I'll argue less hard over. The axle I won't argue over at all."

Then he drops his voice.

"One more thing, and it isn't money. The Fist lad you paid to ride to Nashkel after the first load. Davy says he came round twice after, asking when the next one went. The second time was three or four days before Rusk loaded, and you told him the day." He doesn't make it an accusation. "Could be a lad wanting wages. I'd still like his name, and whether he's still at the Fist post here."

He glances at Davy's empty shed and back at Venn.

"And from here on, don't tell anyone the day. Not Orla's room, not a Fist lad, not a friend. If anyone asks, you're still short of guards."

*(Out of game: if Venn agrees to the repairs, Bram offers to walk the money round to Ames himself so the wheel is secured before noon. Then he helps Tam's boys get the old brown emptied and round to Gethin.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam Hesketh stops the lead pair with a touch of the goad on the near ox's shoulder. He hears Bram out with his eyes on the beasts, not on Bram. When Bram finishes, he gives one nod.

"Once, stop," he says. His voice is rough from not being used. "Twice, under." Then he walks the oxen on to the stalls, and that is apparently all the conversation he means to have this morning.

Two lads come in behind the last pair, sixteen or seventeen and a year or two older, both square and grey-eyed like their father. They start backing the oxen into the stalls without being told. Davy goes to help them and looks happy to have something to do with his hands.

---

Venn listens to the whole account standing just inside the gate, with the ledger under his arm. He doesn't interrupt. At *on her belly in the road at Lark's* his mouth goes thin, and he looks past Bram at the old brown. He looks at her the way a man looks at a dog he's fond of after it has bitten someone.

"I paid the Thunderhammer for that tyre at the thaw," he says. "Nobody said a word about the axle."

"Nobody looked," Hedda says from by the wheel.

Venn lets that go. He walks over to the fore wheel lying flat in the dirt, stands it up with one hand and holds it beside the old brown's hind wheel, the way Hedda did. He looks at the gap between the rims for a long moment and then lets it fall.

"Hobb sold me that as a spare in the Gate. Four years I've carried it." He breathes out through his nose. "Six and a half. The strap and the wheel. Yes." He opens the ledger against his forearm, takes a stub of pencil from inside his coat and writes it down while he talks. "I'll not argue the axle. I'm not such a fool as to argue that."

Hedda tells him about the casks then, plainly: two went south on Rusk's pegs, the one at the back of the shed weeps, and Tam should judge how much water to carry. Venn turns to the stalls.

"Tam. Water."

Tam doesn't look round from the yoke he's lifting. "Two casks to the ford. Ford to Kesh's, one'll do. Not that one." He means the weeping cask.

"There's a cooper next to Ashby's," Venn says. "Two casks. I'll stand that as well." He writes it down. Then he comes to the lever and block, and the pencil stops.

"No." He says it mildly enough. "I've four wagons and a block under one of them, and in twenty years Tam has never needed two at once." He looks across the yard at Tobin, who is holding eight feet of grey ash upright out of the oxen's way. "And it seems the lad has brought his own lever. Ames will give you an oak offcut for a block if you ask him nicely. He has a yard full of them."

Tobin looks at his pike shaft as if it has just been given a rank.

Hedda says nothing. She doesn't need to. She had it right at breakfast.

---

When Bram lowers his voice, Venn lowers his too, and he takes a moment before answering.

"Lem Cotter," he says. "Freckled, red-haired, nineteen or twenty. He rides better than he walks. I paid him four gold to go to Nashkel and back after Dace, and he did it in five days, and he told me what I've told you. Nothing on the road." He closes the ledger on the pencil. "Then he came round asking about the next load. Twice. I took him for a lad who wasn't being paid and saw a merchant who might. Whether he's still at the post, I couldn't tell you. I've not set eyes on him since I sent him off."

He hears the last part, *don't tell anyone the day*, and his face twists.

"I told Orla's whole room the day. Last night. Like a fool." He taps the ledger once. "From now on I'm short of guards and complaining about the price. That part's no lie, at least. I'm always complaining about the price."

When Bram offers to take the money round to Ames himself, Venn thinks about it for exactly as long as it takes to count. Then he takes a small purse from inside his coat and counts three gold pieces into Bram's palm.

"For Ames, for the wheel. Gethin I'll pay myself, at dusk, when I've seen the plates on her. He won't give me a firm price before then anyway." He ties the purse again. "I'm trusting a man I met last night with three gold. I've not earned that kind of luck, but it seems I'm spending it."

---

Ames is at his spokeshave when Bram comes in through the shavings. He looks at the three gold pieces on his bench and gives one nod. "Middle one. By noon at Gethin's." He sweeps the coins into a drawer and goes back to the oak. When Bram mentions an offcut for a block, he points the spokeshave at a heap of oak ends by the door without looking up. "Take one. A square one."

*(Out of game: Ames actually said he'd hold the wheel until tomorrow. Noon was when he'd roll it to Gethin if it was sold. No harm done; it's paid now.)*

---

The old brown's bed is already swept and empty, so there's nothing to unload. Tam's elder boy backs a pair of his father's steadiest oxen onto her tongue. Bram walks beside the near wheel as she rolls out of the yard, round the soot-black wall and up to the Thunderhammer's open front. It's a little after eleven. Gethin comes out wiping his hands, has her tipped up on blocks with the boy's help, and lies under her a minute with a lamp.

"Hand long. Old grease. As you said." He crawls out. "Two and a half. Dusk."

At the far anvil the big smith keeps hammering and doesn't look round.

It's a little after eleven. Ruddock's boards, ewes and mallet will be waiting by his pump in the early afternoon.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Tam Hesketh stops the lead pair with a touch of the goad on the near ox's shoulder. He hears you out with his eyes on the beasts, not on you. When you finish, he gives one nod.

"Once, stop," he says. His voice is rough from not being used. "Twice, under." Then he walks the oxen on to the stalls, and that is apparently all the conversation he means to have this morning.

Two lads come in behind the last pair, sixteen or seventeen and a year or two older, both square and grey-eyed like their father. They start backing the oxen into the stalls without being told. Davy goes to help them and looks happy to have something to do with his hands.

---

Venn listens to the whole account standing just inside the gate, with the ledger under his arm. He doesn't interrupt. At *on her belly in the road at Lark's* his mouth goes thin, and he looks past you at the old brown. He looks at her the way a man looks at a dog he's fond of after it has bitten someone.

"I paid the Thunderhammer for that tyre at the thaw," he says. "Nobody said a word about the axle."

"Nobody looked," Hedda says from by the wheel.

Venn lets that go. He walks over to the fore wheel lying flat in the dirt, stands it up with one hand and holds it beside the old brown's hind wheel, the way Hedda did. He looks at the gap between the rims for a long moment and then lets it fall.

"Hobb sold me that as a spare in the Gate. Four years I've carried it." He breathes out through his nose. "Six and a half. The strap and the wheel. Yes." He opens the ledger against his forearm, takes a stub of pencil from inside his coat and writes it down while he talks. "I'll not argue the axle. I'm not such a fool as to argue that."

Hedda tells him about the casks then, plainly: two went south on Rusk's pegs, the one at the back of the shed weeps, and Tam should judge how much water to carry. Venn turns to the stalls.

"Tam. Water."

Tam doesn't look round from the yoke he's lifting. "Two casks to the ford. Ford to Kesh's, one'll do. Not that one." He means the weeping cask.

"There's a cooper next to Ashby's," Venn says. "Two casks. I'll stand that as well." He writes it down. Then he comes to the lever and block, and the pencil stops.

"No." He says it mildly enough. "I've four wagons and a block under one of them, and in twenty years Tam has never needed two at once." He looks across the yard at Tobin, who is holding eight feet of grey ash upright out of the oxen's way. "And it seems the lad has brought his own lever. Ames will give you an oak offcut for a block if you ask him nicely. He has a yard full of them."

Tobin looks at his pike shaft as if it has just been given a rank.

Hedda says nothing. She doesn't need to. She had it right at breakfast.

---
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “The four yards, please. Fold it whole; I’ll cut the dressings as we need them.”

I count out one gold, seven silver and two copper. “And thank you for choosing cloth for the work rather than the price.”

As I pack the purchases away, I add, “I’m glad the lad’s doing better. If anyone asks, though, please don’t promise I can do the same for everyone. There are limits to what I can manage in a day.”

Then I return to Venn’s yard with the supplies. I look for Tam, introduce myself, and show him the linen, soap and scissors before stowing them in the box beneath his seat.

“For wounds, not axle grease. May I keep them here? I want everyone to know where they are.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brigit Ashby folds the four yards into a thick square, edges inward, the way a woman folds sheets she means to keep. She lays the soap on top and the scissors on the soap, then ties the lot with a length of her own twine, measured against the brass tacks without looking. Sefa counts one gold, seven silver and two copper onto the counter. Brigit sweeps the coins into a drawer under the counter, and her mouth twitches at the thanks.

"Cloth for the work. Price for the sale. Most days they're the same thing." She pushes the parcel across.

When Sefa asks her not to promise anything, Brigit takes her spectacles down out of her hair and polishes them on her apron. It gives her a moment to think.

"I'll promise nothing for anybody. I never do." She puts the spectacles back on. "My sister's man will, though. He's that sort. By supper he'll have you mending legs." She looks at Sefa over the rims, frankly curious and not unkind. "In Beregost, folk get their healing up at the Song after dawn prayers, and they pay what they can. A stranger doing it free by Orla's fire gets talked about. Some of the talk's grateful and some of it isn't. I'd not do it by the hearth again unless you want a queue." She picks up her twine. "That's not a warning. That's just Beregost."

Two women from the well come in behind Sefa arguing about lamp wicks, and Brigit turns to them.

---

Venn's yard has changed by the time Sefa gets back with the parcel under her arm. There's a gap in the row of wagons where the old brown stood, and fresh ruts in the packed earth run out through the gate toward the Thunderhammer's front. The forge's double ring comes over the wall the same as before. Davy is forking straw into the stalls. Hedda is sitting on the tongue of the second wagon, running a whetstone along her spear head in long, slow strokes. Tob is beside her, holding a javelin and trying to look as if he isn't admiring it.

"Venn's at the cooper's," Davy calls, before anyone asks.

Tam Hesketh is at the near stall with his younger boy, working a lump of tallow into the ox yoke where the bows rub. Sefa gives him her name. He hears it with his eyes on the yoke and keeps rubbing until the bow is done. Then he wipes his hands on his smock and walks over to his wagon with her.

She opens the parcel on the seat and shows him the linen, the soap and the scissors. He looks at each one as if he's been asked to price it. Then he reaches under the seat, pulls the box out and takes the lid off. He moves the goose-grease jar, the hoof pick and the horsehair cord down to one end, and lays the folded grey rag on top of them. That leaves the other half of the box empty.

"That end," he says. His voice is a rasp. "Grease this end. Don't cross."

Sefa packs the linen, the soap and the scissors into the clean end. Tam watches her do it, then slides the box back under the seat with his boot.

"Boys'll know," he says. He nods once at the younger lad at the stall, who has been listening the whole time and nods back. Then Tam goes back to the yoke.

Hedda hasn't stopped working the whetstone. "He'll remember that," she says, to nobody in particular. "Venn said so."

It's a little past eleven. The pasture and Ruddock's boards wait for early afternoon, and nothing else needs Sefa before then unless she wants it to.

*(Out of game: Sefa's shopping and the walk back put her in the yard a little after eleven. The old brown has just gone round to Gethin's, and the others' scene is at the forge front.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell eats without hurry, but without wasting any. When the bowl is half gone, he fishes in his pouch and counts out twenty copper onto the bar in front of him, stacking them in two short rows as if to make the count obvious.

"Two silver. Counting it for you this time." He says it without flourishes, more like a man settling an old habit than making a joke. "Breakfast was worth it."

He finishes the last of the porridge, folds the bacon between the remaining bread, and rises. At the door he pauses, not looking at the stairs, as if considering a thing he has already decided.

Then he goes out through the stable gate toward Wat, swinging wide enough not to spook the barrow. "Morning. I hear you saddled the man in the grey coat." A plain voice, not pushing. "Which way out of the yard did he take?"
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell stacks twenty coppers on the bar in two short rows of ten.

Orla comes back out of the kitchen door with a pot on her hip and looks at the two rows. She doesn't count them. He's already done it for her, and she gives him a look that says she noticed. Then she sweeps the lot into her apron with the side of her hand.

"Two silver. Square," she says. "Glad it was worth it." She takes his empty bowl and is gone again before the door stops swinging.

Pell folds the last rasher into the heel of bread and gets up. At the street door he stops for a moment without looking toward the stairs. Then he turns the other way and goes out to the stable yard.

---

The yard cobbles have dried in patches since dawn. Wat is in the stall beside the empty one. He's bent over the barrow with the fork, and his lantern is still hooked on his wrist even though it's broad day. Pell swings wide of the barrow, and Wat hears him come and straightens up by halves, the way a man does when his back has been bent over muck for forty years.

He listens to the question with the fork planted in the straw. He's not slow to answer. He just doesn't rush for anyone.

"Him in the grey. Aye." He tips his head at the empty stall with its straw swept back. "Bay gelding. Good horse, better than he needed. Saddled him by lantern a bit before five." He sniffs. "Out the gate there and left, up the Way toward the temple end. Walked him, didn't trot. Didn't see past the corner. I had the gate to shut."

He takes the fork out of the straw and leans on it.

"Said hold the stall till dark. Paid for it, so it's held." He looks at the empty stall as if it might have something to add. "He asked me last night whether the west road were fit for a horse after the rain. High Hedge road, goes off past the Song. I told him it were fit if he didn't mind mud to the hocks." He shrugs. "Whether he took it or went on north I couldn't tell you. Left's left either way, up to the temple."

Then, because a man in his trade notices what's in a horse's feet and not much else about the man on top of it:

"Come in three nights back with his hooves packed full of black grit. Like coal dust, but heavier. I picked it out. Half the horses in Beregost come in black, mind." He nods at the town's chimneys. "It's that sort of town."

He bends back to the barrow. The fork goes into the straw, and that seems to be the end of what Wat has to say.

From the yard Pell can hear the Thunderhammer's double ring two streets off, toward Venn's yard. It's a little before ten by the sun. Nothing else here needs him unless he wants it to.

 *(Out of game: paying Orla and asking Wat were plain actions, so neither needed a roll. Both happened in the scene: Orla took the twenty copper and called him square, and Wat told him which way Corby rode.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**


## Round 21

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Gethin. "Dusk. Venn pays you then, and he'll want to see the plates."

He walks the oak offcut back round to Venn's yard and wedges it under the old brown's empty peg so it travels with her. Then he finds Hedda, Tob and Sefa, whoever's still about.

"Wheel's paid. Axle's on blocks at Gethin's. Block's here." He nods at Tob. "Your stick's been promoted. Don't let it go to its head."

Then, lower:

"I've an hour or two before Ruddock's. I'm going to walk past the Fist post and ask after a red-haired lad called Lem Cotter. Not to accuse him of anything. Just to see if he's still there, and if not, when he left and where he went." A glance at Hedda. "You know how Fist posts talk better than I do. Come if you like. Otherwise, Ruddock's pump at the first bell after noon. Eat first. Nobody fights an ankheg hungry."

At the Fist post, if there's one in town, Bram keeps it friendly and plain, the way a caravan man talks to the guard at a gate.

"Looking for Lem Cotter. Red hair, freckles, rides well. Did a Nashkel run for a merchant this spring and did it quick. I've wagons going south and I'm short a rider." A shrug. "Is he about, or has he moved on?"

If they ask why, that's the whole truth he'll give. If Lem's there, Bram wants to see his face and hear how he answers a question about riding south. If he's gone, Bram wants to know when he left and whether anyone knows where.

Then he gets something cheap to eat on the Coast Way, walks back to his room for nothing but a drink of water and a look at the shutter, and heads for Ruddock's yard in time to meet the others at the pump.

*(Out of game: if nobody comes with him to the Fist post, Bram goes alone. He won't press the Fist if they're short with him.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Gethin doesn't look up. He raises the tongs an inch, which counts as an answer, and goes back to the bar.

---

The old brown's place in Venn's yard is an empty strip of packed earth with fresh ruts running out through the gate. Bram sets the square oak block down there, where she'll stand again at dusk. Davy watches from the stalls and nods hard, as if he's been given a job too.

"She gets it when she's back," the boy says. "I'll see to it."

Hedda is still on the second wagon's tongue, working the whetstone along her spear in long strokes. Tob is beside her with one of Bram's javelins across his knees. When Bram says the stick's been promoted, Tob picks up the pike shaft from where it leans on the wheel and looks it over, top to butt.

"Lever," he says, pleased with himself. "Ames gave the block and I've the lever."

"You've a stick," Hedda says, but there's no edge in it.

She hears the rest with the whetstone still moving: the Fist post, the red-haired lad, no accusation. At *you know how Fist posts talk* the stone stops.

"No." She says it flatly, the way she says a price she won't pay. Then she starts the stroke again. "Not in a Fist door. You go." After a moment she gives him something anyway. "Don't ask the lad on the door. Ask whoever's eating. The serjeant's the one eating."

Tob opens his mouth.

"Tob. Eat," she says, and he shuts it again. "Pump. First bell after noon. We'll be there."

---

The Fist post is up the Coast Way toward the temple end, past the last of the big forges, where the street widens and the houses get stone foundations. It's a narrow two-storey house with a red fist painted over the door. The paint has flaked down to a pink stain, and someone has freshened the knuckles and not the rest. The door stands open on a front room with a table, a bench, a rack holding four spears, and a slate on the wall with names chalked on it. A lad of about seventeen in a tabard too big for him leans on the doorpost and straightens up when he sees mail coming.

At the table a heavy man of fifty in a sweat-dark gambeson is eating bread and cold sausage off the board with a knife. Bram takes Hedda's advice and talks to the one who's eating.

The man hears it all without stopping: Lem Cotter, red hair, freckles, rides well, the quick Nashkel run, wagons going south and a rider short. He chews and swallows.

"Brack. Serjeant, for my sins." Gate accent, gone thick with grievance. "Cotter's about. He's in the back with the horses, which is where he lives." He points his knife at the slate. Bram can read *COTTER* in chalk, with a column of strokes beside it. "That's his days unpaid. Mine's longer. Everyone's is. The paymaster's in the Gate, the money's in the Gate, and the Gate's in no hurry." He cuts more sausage. "You'll be another of them asking after Venn's wagons. We had Venn himself in here twice, red in the face. So before you start: none of ours. There's six of us and me, and we've not had a man south of the ford since the thaw. Nashkel's the same and they'll tell you so. Whoever told that woman of his they were Fist, they weren't."

He jabs the knife toward the back of the house.

"I'll tell you something else for nothing, since nobody pays me for anything. In the spring some light-fingered sod had two tabards off the line behind the wash-house. Two good ones, new from the Gate. I'm docked for them." He snorts. "Lem! Man for you."

A door at the back bangs open on a yard full of straw and horse smell. A young man comes through wiping his hands on his breeches. He's nineteen or twenty, freckled, red hair standing up on one side, and he walks like riding is the thing he's good at and walking is something he puts up with. He looks at Bram's shield, then at Bram's face, and goes a little wary, the way a man does when he's had people asking after him lately.

Bram says it again, plainly: wagons south, short a rider.

The wariness goes out of Lem all at once, and something hungrier takes its place.

"South? Venn's lot?" Before Bram can answer, he's talking. "I asked him. Twice. He said he couldn't afford me." He glances at the slate and back again. "I've not seen a copper since Ches. I'd ride for anybody."

He doesn't wait to be asked about the first run. It seems to be something people have asked him about before.

"I did Venn's ride after the first lot. Lark's in a day and a half, Nashkel in two more, and back. Old Pennick at the ford said four wagons went up the south bank before noon, Dace Morrow walking at the front. After that, nothing. Not a wheel, not a dead ox. I rode the Way. Venn paid me for the Way." He hesitates. "Should I have gone off it? Nobody said."

Brack keeps chewing. "Nobody said because nobody pays."

Lem doesn't take his eyes off Bram, and his face is open, with nothing behind it that Bram can see except want. He has the look of a lad three months without pay, standing in front of a man who has just mentioned wages.

"You said you're short a rider," he says. "Is that true? What's it pay?"

*(Out of game: Lem's question stops the turn, so Bram's meal on the Coast Way, his stop at his room and the walk to Ruddock's pump are all still to come.)*
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Gethin doesn't look up. He raises the tongs an inch, which counts as an answer, and goes back to the bar.

---

The old brown's place in Venn's yard is an empty strip of packed earth with fresh ruts running out through the gate. You set the square oak block down there, where she'll stand again at dusk. Davy watches from the stalls and nods hard, as if he's been given a job too.

"She gets it when she's back," the boy says. "I'll see to it."

Hedda is still on the second wagon's tongue, working the whetstone along her spear in long strokes. Tob is beside her with one of your javelins across his knees. When you say the stick's been promoted, Tob picks up the pike shaft from where it leans on the wheel and looks it over, top to butt.

"Lever," he says, pleased with himself. "Ames gave the block and I've the lever."

"You've a stick," Hedda says, but there's no edge in it.

She hears the rest with the whetstone still moving: the Fist post, the red-haired lad, no accusation. At *you know how Fist posts talk* the stone stops.

"No." She says it flatly, the way she says a price she won't pay. Then she starts the stroke again. "Not in a Fist door. You go." After a moment she gives you something anyway. "Don't ask the lad on the door. Ask whoever's eating. The serjeant's the one eating."

Tob opens his mouth.

"Tob. Eat," she says, and he shuts it again. "Pump. First bell after noon. We'll be there."

---

The Fist post is up the Coast Way toward the temple end, past the last of the big forges, where the street widens and the houses get stone foundations. It's a narrow two-storey house with a red fist painted over the door. The paint has flaked down to a pink stain, and someone has freshened the knuckles and not the rest. The door stands open on a front room with a table, a bench, a rack holding four spears, and a slate on the wall with names chalked on it. A lad of about seventeen in a tabard too big for him leans on the doorpost and straightens up when he sees your mail coming.

At the table a heavy man of fifty in a sweat-dark gambeson is eating bread and cold sausage off the board with a knife. You take Hedda's advice and talk to the one who's eating.

The man hears it all without stopping: Lem Cotter, red hair, freckles, rides well, the quick Nashkel run, wagons going south and a rider short. He chews and swallows.

"Brack. Serjeant, for my sins." Gate accent, gone thick with grievance. "Cotter's about. He's in the back with the horses, which is where he lives." He points his knife at the slate. You can read *COTTER* in chalk, with a column of strokes beside it. "That's his days unpaid. Mine's longer. Everyone's is. The paymaster's in the Gate, the money's in the Gate, and the Gate's in no hurry." He cuts more sausage. "You'll be another of them asking after Venn's wagons. We had Venn himself in here twice, red in the face. So before you start: none of ours. There's six of us and me, and we've not had a man south of the ford since the thaw. Nashkel's the same and they'll tell you so. Whoever told that woman of his they were Fist, they weren't."

He jabs the knife toward the back of the house.

"I'll tell you something else for nothing, since nobody pays me for anything. In the spring some light-fingered sod had two tabards off the line behind the wash-house. Two good ones, new from the Gate. I'm docked for them." He snorts. "Lem! Man for you."

A door at the back bangs open on a yard full of straw and horse smell. A young man comes through wiping his hands on his breeches. He's nineteen or twenty, freckled, red hair standing up on one side, and he walks like riding is the thing he's good at and walking is something he puts up with. He looks at your shield, then at your face, and goes a little wary, the way a man does when he's had people asking after him lately.

You say it again, plainly: wagons south, short a rider.

The wariness goes out of Lem all at once, and something hungrier takes its place.

"South? Venn's lot?" Before you can answer, he's talking. "I asked him. Twice. He said he couldn't afford me." He glances at the slate and back again. "I've not seen a copper since Ches. I'd ride for anybody."

He doesn't wait to be asked about the first run. It seems to be something people have asked him about before.

"I did Venn's ride after the first lot. Lark's in a day and a half, Nashkel in two more, and back. Old Pennick at the ford said four wagons went up the south bank before noon, Dace Morrow walking at the front. After that, nothing. Not a wheel, not a dead ox. I rode the Way. Venn paid me for the Way." He hesitates. "Should I have gone off it? Nobody said."

Brack keeps chewing. "Nobody said because nobody pays."

Lem doesn't take his eyes off you, and his face is open, with nothing behind it that you can see except want. He has the look of a lad three months without pay, standing in front of a man who has just mentioned wages.

"You said you're short a rider," he says. "Is that true? What's it pay?"

*(Out of game: Lem's question stops the turn, so your meal on the Coast Way, your stop at your room and the walk to Ruddock's pump are all still to come.)*
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “Thank you, Tam. Grease stays its end.”

I show Hedda and Tob where the supplies are, then join them by the wagon tongue without interrupting the sharpening.

“Casks being sorted, axle at the forge, dressings packed. That’s a better start than we had this morning.”

Before we head to Ruddock’s, I make sure Bram hears where the supplies are too. Otherwise, I stay with the group and am ready to go to the pasture when they are.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam doesn't look up from the yoke. He gives one short nod at *grease stays its end*, and that settles it as far as he's concerned.

Sefa shows Hedda and Tob the box under the salt wagon's seat: linen, soap and scissors in the clean end, the grease jar and hoof pick in the other. Tob crouches to look and nods seriously, the way Tam's younger boy did. Hedda glances in from where she sits on the tongue of the second wagon, sees it, and goes back to her spear.

Sefa sits down on the tongue beside them. The whetstone keeps moving in long strokes, and Hedda only lets it rest when Sefa talks about a better start than this morning.

"Better," she says. "Not started." The stone goes back to work.

Tob has a javelin across his knees and turns it over to look at the binding. "This morning we hadn't even got a wheel," he says. "Now there's a whole new one. And a block. And me with a lever." He sounds pleased about all three.

The yard gate creaks. Venn comes in backwards, pulling a cooper's handbarrow with two new casks on it. The staves are still pale and the hoops are bright iron. A cooper's apprentice pushes from the other end, and the boy looks put out at having to walk a merchant's goods two streets. Venn sets the handles down by Tam's wagon and straightens slowly, one hand at the small of his back.

"Tam."

Tam leaves the yoke. He knocks on each cask with a knuckle, tips one up to look at the head, and runs a thumb along the seam where the old one wept. Then he nods once and goes back to the yoke.

"That's a nod I paid fourteen silver for," Venn says to nobody in particular. He tips the apprentice a copper and sends him off. Then he sees Sefa on the wagon tongue, and the empty end of the box that isn't empty any more, and looks for a moment as if he means to ask what it cost. He doesn't ask. He takes the ledger out from under his arm and goes into the shed to count sacks.

Hedda runs the stone along the spear one last time, holds the blade to the light, and puts the stone away in her belt pouch.

"Pump's at the first bell after noon," she says. "We eat before that. There's a pie woman at the well by Ashby's, and it's cheaper than Orla's." She gets up, stiff from sitting, and slings Bram's two javelins through her belt. Tob is already on his feet. Hedda looks down at Sefa without asking anything, the way she'd wait for someone at a gate.

It's a quarter past eleven. Nothing else in the yard needs Sefa before the pasture unless she wants it to.

*(Out of game: Bram is at the Fist post in his own scene, so he hasn't heard where the dressings are yet. Sefa can tell him at Ruddock's pump.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell nods once to Wat, a short acknowledgement that says he's heard all of it, and turns out of the yard without another question. He doesn't hurry; he has been around horses enough to know an ostler talks better when he's not crowded.

He walks the two streets to Venn's yard with the folded bacon growing cold in his hand, eating the last of it as he goes. The Thunderhammer's double ring beats through the street noise, and when he reaches the yard he stops a moment at the gate to take in the wagons, the oxen, and the small knot of people there before he steps in and rejoins the line of talk.

He comes up at Bram's shoulder and says, low, in the space between one word and the next: "The grey coat's gone. Paid through tonight and left before five, riding a good bay, north toward the temple or the High Hedge road. Wat couldn't say which after the corner. He'd asked about the road west last night." A pause to let that settle. "Hooves came in packed with black grit. Not coal dust, Wat says. Heavier."

Then, as if that were all one matter and settled: "What does the yard look like, then?"
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Wat has already gone back to the barrow by the time Pell nods to him. The fork scrapes on the stall floor behind him as Pell goes out through the stable gate onto the Coast Way.

The bacon has gone cold in the bread, and he finishes it walking. The two streets to the Thunderhammer are full of the midday trade: drays, a knife-grinder's wheel shrieking outside a doorway, a smith's boy running with a pail of slack water. The double ring of the forge gets louder with every step. The Thunderhammer's back wall makes one side of Venn's yard, and the heat comes off the bricks as he passes.

Pell stops at the gate and counts the yard.

There are three wagons in the row where four stood this morning, by the look of the ruts. A gap of fresh-scored earth shows where the third one stood. A square oak block sits in the middle of the gap on its own. Oxen fill the stalls, big dun beasts swinging their heads at the flies. A square grey man in a carter's smock is working tallow into a yoke bow, and a grey-eyed lad of sixteen or so is at the next stall doing the same. Two new casks, pale-staved with bright hoops, stand by the end wagon. A boy of about fourteen is forking straw. In the lean-to shed someone is counting under his breath among the salt sacks.

Bram's broad back isn't anywhere in it. Neither are the shield, the sellswords, or the pike shaft.

Pell's piece is ready: the bay, before five, left toward the temple, the west road asked about, the black grit heavier than coal dust. There's no shoulder here to say it at.

The boy with the fork has seen him at the gate, and he's plainly been told something about him.

"You're the other one," he says. "The scout." He goes a little red. "Davy. I keep the yard. The big one's gone up the Way to the Fist house, with the red fist over the door. He went just now. The two sellswords went off for pies at the well." He points the fork at the gap in the row. "The old brown's round at the Thunderhammer. Cracked axle. He found it, lying under her."

The counting in the shed stops. Aldous Venn comes out with the ledger open against his forearm and a pencil stub behind his ear. He sees who's at the gate and closes the book on his thumb.

"Master Dunmore. You said you'd walk them before I paid you to." It isn't quite a complaint. "You're late for most of it. Your friend Halloway's had my day off me already." He tips his head along the row and gives it to Pell in order, the way he'd read out a bill. "The old brown's at Gethin's, being strapped across a crack I never knew she had, and back by dusk. Ames sold me a hind wheel, because it turns out the spare I've carried four years only fits the front. Two new casks, and Tam has nodded at them, so they'll do. The lady with the book has put linen and scissors in the box under Tam's seat. Clean end. Nobody's to cross it." He takes the pencil from behind his ear and puts it in his coat. "That's what my yard looks like. Ten wagons lost, and it's taken five strangers one morning to show me what was wrong with the four I've got left."

Tam doesn't look up from the yoke. The lad at the next stall does, briefly, at the word *strangers*, then goes back to his tallow.

The sun is nearly overhead, and the Thunderhammer's ring goes on over the wall. Ruddock's pump is at the first bell after noon, and nothing else in the yard is waiting on Pell before then unless he wants it to.

*(Out of game: the walk itself took Pell minutes, but his scene had been running about an hour and a half behind the others' (it was near ten at Wat's). To put all three of you back on one clock I've brought him into the yard at the shared hour, a little before noon. If Pell spent that gap doing something, tell me and I'll play it. Bram has just left for the Fist post, so Pell's report on Corby still hasn't been said to him.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**


## Round 22

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't answer straight away. He looks at the slate and the column of strokes, then back at the lad.

"Truth is, it's not my purse," he says. "Venn pays, and you know what he told you. I'll put your name to him tonight and tell him you did Nashkel and back in five days. That's the most I can promise, and I won't dress it up as more." He holds Lem's eye. "If he says yes, you'll hear it from him, not from me."

He pulls a few coppers from his pouch and sets them by Brack's board.

"For the sausage I'm about to ask him to share." Then, to Lem, easily, as if it's just road talk: "Sit a minute. You did that run faster than most would. When you came back and went round to Venn's asking about the next load, he told you the day Rusk was going. Fourthday. Did that go any further? Did you tell anyone? A mate, a girl, a man at a bar who asked after work south?" He keeps his voice level. "I'm not saying you did wrong. Half the town knew the day by the end. I'd just like to know who else was asking."

He lets Lem answer. Then he turns to Brack.

"Those two tabards off the line. When in the spring? Before Venn's second load went, or after?" A beat. "Because the woman leading it was turned off the Way at Lark's Ford by men in your colours. If somebody went to the trouble of stealing the coats, I'd like to know when they started planning."

Last, back to Lem:

"And Pennick, at the ford. When you watered there after the first load, did he talk about anyone else hanging round his yard? Men who stayed a while, didn't buy much, watched who came through?"

Whatever they say, Bram thanks them both plainly. On his way out he stops by the lad in the oversized tabard at the door.

"If anyone comes asking after Venn's wagons, or after me, the big man with the painted-over shield, tell your serjeant." He nods and goes.

*(Out of game: after this, Bram buys something cheap to eat on the Coast Way, stops at his room for water, and heads for Ruddock's pump for the first bell after noon. He'll tell Sefa, Hedda, Tob and Pell about the stolen tabards there.)*
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram looks at the slate for a while before he answers: the chalked names and the columns of strokes beside them. Lem waits the way a man waits for a coin that may not come.

When Bram says *it's not my purse*, Lem's face falls a little. He hears the rest, *your name to him tonight, five days there and back*, and some of it comes back.

"Tell him five days," he says. "And tell him I'd do it in four on a better horse. That's no boast. The post nag's got a split hoof." He wipes his hands down his breeches again, though they're already clean. "I'll be here. Or out back. Same thing."

Bram sets three coppers by Brack's board. The serjeant stops chewing and looks at the coins as if they're a joke he hasn't heard before. Then he cuts a fat round of sausage with his knife and pushes it across the board without saying a word. The coppers go into his gambeson.

Lem sits down on the end of the bench when Bram tells him to. Bram puts the question to him easily, as road talk: who else heard about Fourthday. Lem answers it the way he answers everything, straight away and without guarding any of it.

"Everyone." He says it as if it's obvious. "The lads here. I told them Venn's lot went Fourthday with five, and he couldn't stretch to six. I was sore about it." He nods at the door lad, who reddens. "And at the trough, on the Way by the temple, where the drovers water. I asked anyone with a wagon if they wanted a rider south. I said Venn's going Fourthday and won't have me, so who will." He frowns, going back over it honestly. "Nobody took me up. Nobody asked me anything special either. Folk at a trough just ask where you're bound." Then, a little quieter: "Was that wrong?"

He doesn't wait for an answer.

Brack swallows and takes the tabard question with his knife pointing at the slate, as if it might bear him out.

"Tarsakh. A week after the thaw, maybe less. Before any of Venn's lot went, a good month before the first." He saws at the loaf. "I know because they docked me for it out of a month they hadn't paid me for yet. Two new ones, still creased from the Gate. Somebody came over the back wall in the night and had them off the line, and left a shirt of mine that was hanging right next to them." He snorts. "Choosy."

He stops sawing when Bram says *men in your colours*, and his face goes darker.

"I told you. None of mine. If some sod's walking about in my coats turning wagons off the Way, I'd like a word with him and all." He jabs the knife once at the doorway. "Before you do, if it can be managed."

Bram turns back to Lem with the last question: Pennick, and anyone hanging round his yard. Lem opens his mouth to say no, then shuts it again. He thinks it over properly, eyes on the table.

"He talks money," he says slowly. "Only money. What a wheel costs to water, what the river's worth to him, how folk cheat him." He rubs his jaw. "Coming back up, I watered at his trough and paid for it, and he said, *You lot again, and still not paying for the trough.* I thought he meant me. I'd been through going down two days before, and I'd watered the nag in the shallows below his yard, because it was free." He looks up at Bram. "I took it for him being sore at me. Nobody said otherwise."

Brack has stopped eating.

"You lot," he repeats. He says nothing more, but he looks at the slate and its six names as if he's counting them again.

Bram thanks them both plainly. Lem gets up from the bench as he does, as if he means to walk him out, then remembers there's nowhere to walk him to and stays where he is.

At the door the lad in the oversized tabard straightens up. Bram tells him: anyone asking after Venn's wagons, or after the big man with the painted-over shield, goes to the serjeant.

"Yes," the lad says. Then he glances back at Brack and adds, "Serjeant," as if he isn't sure which of them he's answering.

"He'll tell me," Brack calls from the table, through bread. "He tells me everything. It's what he's for."

---

The Coast Way is at its midday loudest. A woman on a stool by a forge front is selling mutton pasties out of a cloth-lined basket, two coppers each, still warm at the bottom of the pile. Bram eats one walking. It's mostly onion, and none the worse for it.

At the Burning Wizard the common room is half empty between meals. Nobody stops him on the stairs. His room is as he left it: bar on its brackets inside, shutter on its hook, the two javelins gone and the torches and bedroll by the cot. He drinks from the jug on the peg shelf and looks at the shutter. The hook is where he put it and the sill is undisturbed. Through the plank wall the stripped room next door is silent.

---

At the first bell after noon the stink comes round the forge wall to meet him. Ruddock is as good as his word. By the pump the oak pit boards are stacked: six of them, black with soaking, each as long as a man. The mallet lies across the top with a sharpened stake and a coil of cord beside it. The horn lantern hangs off the pump handle, and the pail stands full under the spout. In the pen by the shed the old foot-rot ewe stands with her head down, chewing as if nothing in the world has ever concerned her. The other one has her head through a gap in the hurdle and is working at widening it.

Ruddock is in the same pit, in the same waders, with the same pole.

"Told you," he says, without looking round. "I'm always here."

*(Out of game: Bram reaches the pump a little before one, with the boards, ewes and tools laid out; Sefa, Pell, Hedda and Tob arrive in their own scene's message.)*
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text

When you say *it's not my purse*, Lem's face falls a little. He hears the rest, *your name to him tonight, five days there and back*, and some of it comes back.

"Tell him five days," he says. "And tell him I'd do it in four on a better horse. That's no boast. The post nag's got a split hoof." He wipes his hands down his breeches again, though they're already clean. "I'll be here. Or out back. Same thing."

You set three coppers by Brack's board. The serjeant stops chewing and looks at the coins as if they're a joke he hasn't heard before. Then he cuts a fat round of sausage with his knife and pushes it across the board without saying a word. The coppers go into his gambeson.

Lem sits down on the end of the bench when you tell him to. You put the question to him easily, as road talk: who else heard about Fourthday. Lem answers it the way he answers everything, straight away and without guarding any of it.

"Everyone." He says it as if it's obvious. "The lads here. I told them Venn's lot went Fourthday with five, and he couldn't stretch to six. I was sore about it." He nods at the door lad, who reddens. "And at the trough, on the Way by the temple, where the drovers water. I asked anyone with a wagon if they wanted a rider south. I said Venn's going Fourthday and won't have me, so who will." He frowns, going back over it honestly. "Nobody took me up. Nobody asked me anything special either. Folk at a trough just ask where you're bound." Then, a little quieter: "Was that wrong?"

He doesn't wait for an answer.

Brack swallows and takes the tabard question with his knife pointing at the slate, as if it might bear him out.

"Tarsakh. A week after the thaw, maybe less. Before any of Venn's lot went, a good month before the first." He saws at the loaf. "I know because they docked me for it out of a month they hadn't paid me for yet. Two new ones, still creased from the Gate. Somebody came over the back wall in the night and had them off the line, and left a shirt of mine that was hanging right next to them." He snorts. "Choosy."

He stops sawing when you say *men in your colours*, and his face goes darker.

"I told you. None of mine. If some sod's walking about in my coats turning wagons off the Way, I'd like a word with him and all." He jabs the knife once at the doorway. "Before you do, if it can be managed."

You turn back to Lem with the last question: Pennick, and anyone hanging round his yard. Lem opens his mouth to say no, then shuts it again. He thinks it over properly, eyes on the table.

"He talks money," he says slowly. "Only money. What a wheel costs to water, what the river's worth to him, how folk cheat him." He rubs his jaw. "Coming back up, I watered at his trough and paid for it, and he said, *You lot again, and still not paying for the trough.* I thought he meant me. I'd been through going down two days before, and I'd watered the nag in the shallows below his yard, because it was free." He looks up at you. "I took it for him being sore at me. Nobody said otherwise."

Brack has stopped eating.

"You lot," he repeats. He says nothing more, but he looks at the slate and its six names as if he's counting them again.

You thank them both plainly. Lem gets up from the bench as you do, as if he means to walk you out, then remembers there's nowhere to walk you to and stays where he is.

At the door the lad in the oversized tabard straightens up. You tell him: anyone asking after Venn's wagons, or after the big man with the painted-over shield, goes to the serjeant.

"Yes," the lad says. Then he glances back at Brack and adds, "Serjeant," as if he isn't sure which of you he's answering.

"He'll tell me," Brack calls from the table, through bread. "He tells me everything. It's what he's for."

---

The Coast Way is at its midday loudest. A woman on a stool by a forge front is selling mutton pasties out of a cloth-lined basket, two coppers each, still warm at the bottom of the pile. You eat one walking. It's mostly onion, and none the worse for it.

At the Burning Wizard the common room is half empty between meals. Nobody stops you on the stairs. Your room is as you left it: bar on its brackets inside, shutter on its hook, the two javelins gone and the torches and bedroll by the cot. You drink from the jug on the peg shelf and look at the shutter. The hook is where you put it and the sill is undisturbed. Through the plank wall the stripped room next door is silent.

---

At the first bell after noon the stink comes round the forge wall to meet you. Ruddock is as good as his word. By the pump the oak pit boards are stacked: six of them, black with soaking, each as long as a man. The mallet lies across the top with a sharpened stake and a coil of cord beside it. The horn lantern hangs off the pump handle, and the pail stands full under the spout. In the pen by the shed the old foot-rot ewe stands with her head down, chewing as if nothing in the world has ever concerned her. The other one has her head through a gap in the hurdle and is working at widening it.

Ruddock is in the same pit, in the same waders, with the same pole.

"Told you," he says, without looking round. "I'm always here."
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I get up and join them. “A pie sounds good.”

At the well, I ask the pie woman what she’s selling and the price. Over lunch, I ask Tob, “Have you and Hedda travelled together long?”

Afterward I head with them to Ruddock’s pump for the appointed bell. When Bram arrives, I tell him, “Clean linen, soap and scissors are in the box under Tam’s seat, separate from the grease. Tam and the boys know where.”

Then I check where the boards have been laid and where I can stand on hard ground, off to one side of the others, with a clear view of the bait.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The pie woman sits on an upturned crate by the stone well-head across from Ashby's. She's a broad, red-armed woman with a basket on her knees under a cloth, and a second basket at her feet that is already empty. Two smiths' boys are walking away from her with their mouths full. She looks up at the three of them coming: Hedda first, Tob with his eight feet of ash, and Sefa.

"Mutton and onion, three copper. That's all there is till tomorrow." She lifts the cloth to show them. There's a dozen or so pies, round and dark-crusted, still giving off a little steam into the cold. "The crust's lard. I'll not pretend it's butter."

Sefa pays her three copper. Hedda counts out six for herself and Tob without being asked, and the woman hands the pies over on squares of brown paper. The three of them sit on the well's stone kerb in the thin sun. The pie is hot enough to burn the roof of the mouth, and it's more onion than mutton. It's good.

Tob is halfway through his when Sefa asks how long he and Hedda have been on the road together. He chews quickly so he can answer.

"Year come Midsummer. I was carting barrels at the Friendly Arm and she wanted someone to hold the other end of a tent." He nods at Hedda as if introducing her. "She asked if I could use a spear. I said yes."

"He couldn't," Hedda says.

"I can now. Mostly." He licks gravy off his thumb, the healed one, and looks at the pink seam while he does it. "We did a winter on the Nashkel ore carts. Then the market there went quiet, and she said Beregost, so here we are." He shrugs. "She doesn't tell you where you're going till you're going. You get used to it."

Hedda eats her pie and lets him tell it. She doesn't add anything about where she was before the Friendly Arm, and nobody asks. Once she's finished she folds the brown paper into a small square and puts it in her belt pouch, as though it might come in useful.

"Bell soon," she says.

---

The Song of the Morning's bell rings for the first hour after noon as they come round the soot-black back of the last forge. The tan-pit stink meets them at the bend just as it did this morning.

Ruddock's yard has filled up since then. By the pump the six oak pit boards are stacked, each as long as a man and black with soaking. The mallet lies on top with a sharpened stake and a coil of cord beside it. The horn lantern hangs off the pump handle and a pail stands full under the spout. In the pen by the shed the old foot-rot ewe is chewing with her head down, and the second one has her head through a gap in the hurdle and is working on making it wider. Ruddock is in the same pit, in the same waders, turning the same kind of hide with his pole.

Bram is there by the pump, shield on his arm.

Sefa tells him what she came to tell him: the clean linen, the soap and the scissors are in the box under Tam's seat, kept apart from the grease, and Tam and the boys know where they are.

Then she looks for where the boards have gone, and they haven't gone anywhere yet. They're still stacked by the pump with the tools on top, waiting to be carried. You can't see the lower pasture from the yard. It's out past the gap in the north hurdles, down the slope, and the grey doorstep stone Ruddock talked about is halfway down that hurdle line, somewhere in the grass. The place she'll stand doesn't exist yet. It will be wherever the boards go down, on the hard ground above the old clay pits, off to one side of the curve. To find it she'll have to walk down to the pasture with them and see the ground.

Hedda has already put her hand on the end of the top board to test its weight. She takes it away again.

"Heavy as sin," she says. "He said so."

*(Out of game: Bram and Pell's scene comes to you as its own message; next round all of you at the pump are in one scene, and carrying the boards down to the pasture is the next thing.)*
````
