### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell steps into the yard and lets the gate stand open behind him as he comes up alongside the wagons, eyes already moving over wheels and yokes. To Davy he says, "The scout. And you keep the yard, so you'll know if anything's been out of place since the thaw. I'll come to you before I go."

He paces the line of wagons slowly, running a hand over a strap here, checking a lynch pin there, but his real attention is on the look of things—whether anything has been moved that ought not to have been, whether the ruts tell of a cart that came in when the yard was asleep. Then he turns to Venn.

"You've had a long morning of it," he says, not unsympathetically. "The cracked axle's a good thing to learn in town, not on the road." The report he gives is quick and low: the man in the mare's—grey coat—gone before five, on a good bay, riding left toward the temple or the west road. He'd asked Wat whether the High Hedge road was fit for a horse after the rain. "Wat said his hooves came in packed with black grit, heavier than coal dust. In this town half the horses come in black from the forges, but he picked it out of the hooves for a reason. That's the man who was selling black ore to the smiths and asking after the missing loads." A pause, his eyes on Venn's face. "He left before first light, before the rest of the town got up. You ever see him near your yard?"

Pell waits for Venn's answer, then says: "I promised to walk the wagons. Let me do it now while there's still daylight." He gestures toward the gate. "And tell me if anyone's been watching the yard at night—anyone new, anyone with a hood, anyone whose horse leaves hoofprints you didn't see him make."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Davy straightens up at *you keep the yard* as if he's just been handed a rank of his own.

"I'll be at the stalls," he says, goes red, and goes back to forking straw.

Pell walks the row slowly. There are three wagons where four used to stand. Tam's salt wagon is at the end, the second stands beside it, and the fourth has a short split along the grain of one spoke. It's tight in the hub and the felloe, and he can't get a fingernail into it. Every lynch pin is in its hole, with a leather thong through the head and a smear of grease on it. The harness isn't on the wagons. It hangs on pegs in the stalls, and every buckle is done up the same way, by the same hand.

The yard floor tells him what the day has done. Two fresh, deep ruts curve out through the gate where the old brown went round to Gethin's. A handbarrow's narrow single track runs from the gate to the pale new casks by Tam's wagon. The oxen have churned a broad path from the gate to the stalls, and a boy's boot-prints are everywhere. Under all of it the earth is packed as hard as a threshing floor, and nothing older shows through. In the shed the salt sacks sit square under the tarpaulin. The wool bales are roped, and every knot is the same knot.

---

Venn hears the report standing by the shed with the ledger shut on his thumb. At *black grit* he glances down at his own boots. They have white salt tide-marks on them, with the street's coal dust over the top like everyone else's.

"Long, and it's not done," he says first. "Gethin's at dusk. After that I'll sleep or I won't." When Pell gets to the man in the grey coat asking after the loads, he shakes his head. "Not of me, he didn't. He never came near my table, and you were sat at it. If he asked anybody else, I didn't hear it." He gives the rest in order, the way he gives everything. "Near my yard? No. I'd never seen him anywhere but Orla's corner, and that was last night. I told you at the stairs: him and his sort want iron, and I've none." He taps the ledger once against his palm. "The west road, though. Tell Halloway. He's choosing my road, not me."

When Pell asks to walk the wagons now, Venn moves the pencil an inch toward the row.

"Walk them. They're not going anywhere." Pell asks who watches the yard at night, and Venn tips his head at the stalls. "Ask him. He sleeps with the salt."

Davy has been listening the whole time. He comes over with the fork and thinks it through properly, the same way he did for Bram.

"Nobody. The gate squeals. You heard it." He's right. It squealed when Venn dragged the cooper's barrow through it. "I'd hear anybody. The Thunderhammer banks its fire about midnight. You hear the bellows through the wall, and Gethin's lad dropping the shovel. Then nothing till they light up again before it's grey." He scuffs the fork. "A fox got in once, after the tallow. That's all." Then, before he can stop himself: "I've got a stick."

He looks as though he wishes he hadn't said it.

Pell has already seen it on his way past the shed. There's a nest of sacking and an old blanket tucked between the salt sacks, and a spare ox goad lies along its edge where a hand can reach it in the dark.

Davy remembers something else and brightens.

"The big one said the tanner's pump at the first bell after noon. He told the sellswords. I heard."

The Thunderhammer's wall throws only a short shadow under the noon sun. The bell up at the Song hasn't rung yet. Unless Pell wants something else from the yard, there's nothing more here for him before then.

*(Out of game: no roll was needed, since reading a yard is Pell's trade and nothing in it was out of place. It's about a quarter past twelve, so the pump at one o'clock is still ahead of him.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text

## Round 23

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Ruddock's back. "Good. I'd hate to be the one who made a liar of you."

He doesn't touch the boards yet. Instead he goes to the gap in the north hurdle, the one Ruddock pointed his pole through that morning, and looks down the pasture from the yard side. He keeps his boots on the packed ground of the yard. He's looking for two things: the flat grey stone the size of a doorstep, half grassed over, and the place where the cropped grass turns long. If he can see the stone from here, good. If not, he'll find it when they come down the lane edge with the boards, by kicking along the hurdle, not by walking out to it.

He also looks at the furrow. Is it where it was this morning, or has it moved?

Then he goes back to the pen, leans on the hurdle and studies the old ewe, chewing away without a care.

"Sorry, old girl. You'll earn your keep today."

While he waits for the others he ties the cord round her neck in a loose collar that won't strangle her, leaving a long lead. He takes the round of sausage out of his pouch and puts it on top of the boards for Tob. The lad will be hungry again by the time they've carried oak down a field.

When Hedda, Tob, Sefa and Pell come round the forge wall, if Pell comes at all, Bram gives them the Fist post news first, low, by the pump.

"Lem Cotter's still at the post. Unpaid since Ches, wants work, and he told the whole drovers' trough the day Rusk was going. I don't think he's our man. I think he's just loud." A beat. "But the serjeant, Brack, says two new tabards were stolen off his wash line in Tarsakh. That's a month before Dace's load went. And Pennick at the ford said something to Lem coming back up: *You lot again, and still not paying for the trough.* Men in Fist coats, watering at Lark's and not paying for it. More than once." He lets that settle. "So somebody planned this from the thaw. And Pennick has seen their faces, even if he only remembers that they didn't pay."

Then he picks up the near end of a board and nods at the stack.

"Two to a board, three trips. They're heavy. Down the lane on the hard ruts, along the hurdle to the grey stone, and lay them just above the line where the grass changes. Nobody steps past the boards until the ewe's staked. I'll take her down and drive the stake. Everyone else on oak." He hefts it. "Tob, you tap ahead of me with the stick every step once I'm off the boards. If it sounds hollow, say so before I find out."

*(Out of game: who has actually turned up at the pump? And from the hurdle gap, can Bram see the grey stone, or whether the furrow has moved since morning?)*

Sefa Marrin: “I’ll take the cord, stake and mallet. I’m more use keeping those out from under your feet than dropping my end of a board.”

I gather the tools, leaving the heavy boards to those better able to carry them.

“Before we stake the ewe, let’s get everyone onto their footing and agree on the signal. I’ll wait for Bram’s first throw before I cast. If it catches someone, I’ll try to push it off them.”

I follow the board carriers toward the pasture, staying on the known hard ground and stopping short of the old clay-pit boundary until Bram has checked where he wants us.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ruddock doesn't turn round from the pit.

"You'd not be the first to try."

The pole goes into the scum again.

Bram crosses to the gap in the north hurdle and stands with his boots on the packed mud of the yard to look out. Ruddock was right this morning: the field can't be seen from here. Past the gap the ground drops away steeply. The overflow trench runs off downhill between a bank of nettles and the back wall of a byre, and all that shows above the fall is the grey top rail of the pasture's north hurdle, running down and away. Bram can't see the stone, the grass line or the furrow from the yard. He'll have to go down there for those.

At the pen the old ewe looks at him over the hurdle with her jaw still going.

"Sorry, old girl. You'll earn your keep today."

She lets him loop the cord over her head without stopping her chewing. He makes a loose collar that won't tighten and leaves a long tail of lead hanging over the hurdle. The other ewe has got her head right through the gap now and stops working at it to watch.

Bram takes the round of sausage out of his pouch and sets it on top of the stacked boards.

---

Boots and a pole butt come round the soot-black wall behind the forge. Hedda is first, with Bram's two javelins slung through her belt, then Tob with the pike shaft, then Sefa. The bend behind them stays empty, and Pell hasn't come round it yet.

Tob sees the sausage before anything else. "Is that—"

"For later," Bram says. "You'll want it after the oak."

Tob takes it as if it's a medal and buttons it inside his shirt.

By the pump Bram gives them the Fist post news, low. Lem is still at the post, unpaid since Ches and loud with it, and he told the drovers' trough the day. Brack had two new tabards stolen off his line in Tarsakh, a month before Dace went. And Pennick said to Lem, *you lot again, and still not paying for the trough.*

Tob's eyes go wide again. "Two coats. So there's two of them at least."

"Two coats is what was stolen," Hedda says. "Doesn't tell you how many wore them." She has her thumb on the faded brand on her neck. She takes it away. "Tarsakh. They had the coats before there was a load to wear them for." She looks at Bram. "Pennick, then. Pay him by the wheel and ask."

In the pit, Ruddock heaves a hide over and says nothing. He has heard all of it, as he hears everything in his yard.

---

Sefa says her piece and gathers up the tools. The cord is already round the ewe's neck, so what's left for her is the sharpened stake and the mallet. The mallet's head is heavier than it looks. She tucks the stake under one arm.

Bram takes the near end of the top board. Hedda takes the far end without being asked. When they lift together the board comes up off the stack as wet and dead as a drowned man, and Hedda grunts once.

"Sin," she says. "He wasn't wrong."

They take it out through the hurdle gateway, round the forge wall and down the south lane on the hard middle of the ruts. Tob walks ahead with the pole and Sefa follows with the stake and mallet. The board's weight sets the pace, and it's a slow one. The Coast Way's hammers fall behind them, and the tan-pit stink comes up the slope to meet them.

The pasture looks the way it did at dawn, except the dew has burned off. In the top corner the sheep are packed against the hurdle with their heads up. None of them is grazing even now, at midday. Down by the reeds the round dark patch shows plainly in the long grass. The mist has gone off the ditch, and the water lies grey-brown and still between the reeds.

The north hurdle runs from the lane down the slope toward the ditch. They set the board on the lane verge. Bram goes along inside the hurdle on the cropped grass, keeping a hand's breadth from the rails, and Tob goes a step ahead of him thumping the pole down. Each thump sounds dull and solid, like knocking on a table.

About halfway down, Bram's boot toe knocks on something that isn't turf. He kicks the grass back. Under it is a flat grey stone, roughly squared, about the size of a doorstep and greened at the edges, sunk level with the ground. Tob brings the pole butt down on it. It rings on stone, and he grins.

From the stone the change in the grass is plain once you're standing on the line. Above it the cropped turf is short, pale and grazed close. Below it the grass grows long, lank and dark almost at once, and the sheep's edge runs straight out across the field from the stone, near enough, as if it had been ruled. Ruddock's father set his stone well.

The furrow is where it was this morning. Bram follows it by eye. It's the one crooked trough across the slope, with the grass sagging along it, and it fades out about twenty feet below the long-grass line without ever touching the cropped ground. There's no second line and no new sag, and nothing has crossed toward the lane. Whatever made it hasn't moved since dawn, or hasn't moved close enough to the top to show.

Sefa stops at the lane verge by the hurdle with the stake and mallet. She's on ground Tob has already sounded, and she goes no further until Bram says where.

Bram and Hedda bring the board along the inside of the hurdle and lay it down on the cropped side, a pace above the stone and square to the grass line. It settles into the turf with a wet sound and doesn't move again. Hedda straightens up and puts a hand to the small of her back. She looks up the lane toward the forge, at the five boards still stacked by Ruddock's pump, at the empty bend where the scout hasn't appeared, and at the three of them who are carrying.

"One," she says. "Three of us on oak. That's five more like that, or your scout comes." She leaves it with Bram.

*(Out of game: Pell isn't at the pump yet. His own message brings him.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a roll, and the scene already played each one. Bram found the grey stone and the grass line, saw the furrow hadn't moved since dawn, tied the ewe's collar, and set the sausage on the boards, which Tob has taken for later.)*
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Ruddock doesn't turn round from the pit.

"You'd not be the first to try."

The pole goes into the scum again.

You cross to the gap in the north hurdle and stand with your boots on the packed mud of the yard to look out. Ruddock was right this morning: the field can't be seen from here. Past the gap the ground drops away steeply. The overflow trench runs off downhill between a bank of nettles and the back wall of a byre, and all that shows above the fall is the grey top rail of the pasture's north hurdle, running down and away. You can't see the stone, the grass line or the furrow from the yard. You'll have to go down there for those.

At the pen the old ewe looks at you over the hurdle with her jaw still going.

"Sorry, old girl. You'll earn your keep today."

She lets you loop the cord over her head without stopping her chewing. You make a loose collar that won't tighten and leave a long tail of lead hanging over the hurdle. The other ewe has got her head right through the gap now and stops working at it to watch.

You take the round of sausage out of your pouch and set it on top of the stacked boards.

---

Boots and a pole butt come round the soot-black wall behind the forge. Hedda is first, with your two javelins slung through her belt, then Tob with the pike shaft, then Sefa. The bend behind them stays empty, and Pell hasn't come round it yet.

Tob sees the sausage before anything else. "Is that—"

"For later," you say. "You'll want it after the oak."

Tob takes it as if it's a medal and buttons it inside his shirt.

By the pump you give them the Fist post news, low.

Tob's eyes go wide again. "Two coats. So there's two of them at least."

"Two coats is what was stolen," Hedda says. "Doesn't tell you how many wore them." She has her thumb on the faded brand on her neck. She takes it away. "Tarsakh. They had the coats before there was a load to wear them for." She looks at you. "Pennick, then. Pay him by the wheel and ask."

In the pit, Ruddock heaves a hide over and says nothing. He has heard all of it, as he hears everything in his yard.

---

Sefa speaks up: "I'll take the cord, stake and mallet. I'm more use keeping those out from under your feet than dropping my end of a board." She gathers up the tools. The cord is already round the ewe's neck, so she takes the sharpened stake and the mallet, and tucks the stake under one arm. "Before we stake the ewe, let's get everyone onto their footing and agree on the signal. I'll wait for Bram's first throw before I cast. If it catches someone, I'll try to push it off them."

You take the near end of the top board. Hedda takes the far end without being asked. When you lift together the board comes up off the stack as wet and dead as a drowned man, and Hedda grunts once.

"Sin," she says. "He wasn't wrong."

You take it out through the hurdle gateway, round the forge wall and down the south lane on the hard middle of the ruts. Tob walks ahead with the pole and Sefa follows with the stake and mallet. The board's weight sets the pace, and it's a slow one. The Coast Way's hammers fall behind you, and the tan-pit stink comes up the slope to meet you.

The pasture looks the way it did at dawn, except the dew has burned off. In the top corner the sheep are packed against the hurdle with their heads up. None of them is grazing even now, at midday. Down by the reeds the round dark patch shows plainly in the long grass. The mist has gone off the ditch, and the water lies grey-brown and still between the reeds.

The north hurdle runs from the lane down the slope toward the ditch. You set the board on the lane verge. You go along inside the hurdle on the cropped grass, keeping a hand's breadth from the rails, and Tob goes a step ahead of you thumping the pole down. Each thump sounds dull and solid, like knocking on a table.

About halfway down, your boot toe knocks on something that isn't turf. You kick the grass back. Under it is a flat grey stone, roughly squared, about the size of a doorstep and greened at the edges, sunk level with the ground. Tob brings the pole butt down on it. It rings on stone, and he grins.

From the stone the change in the grass is plain once you're standing on the line. Above it the cropped turf is short, pale and grazed close. Below it the grass grows long, lank and dark almost at once, and the sheep's edge runs straight out across the field from the stone, near enough, as if it had been ruled. Ruddock's father set his stone well.

The furrow is where it was this morning. You follow it by eye. It's the one crooked trough across the slope, with the grass sagging along it, and it fades out about twenty feet below the long-grass line without ever touching the cropped ground. There's no second line and no new sag, and nothing has crossed toward the lane. Whatever made it hasn't moved since dawn, or hasn't moved close enough to the top to show.

Sefa stops at the lane verge by the hurdle with the stake and mallet. She's on ground Tob has already sounded, and she goes no further.

You and Hedda bring the board along the inside of the hurdle and lay it down on the cropped side, a pace above the stone and square to the grass line. It settles into the turf with a wet sound and doesn't move again. Hedda straightens up and puts a hand to the small of her back. She looks up the lane toward the forge, at the five boards still stacked by Ruddock's pump, at the empty bend where the scout hasn't appeared, and at the three of you who are carrying.

"One," she says. "Three of us on oak. That's five more like that, or your scout comes." She leaves it with you.
````

````text
You come round the soot-black wall behind the forge with the others. Hedda is first, with Bram's two javelins slung through her belt, then Tob with the pike shaft, then you. The bend behind you stays empty, and Pell hasn't come round it yet.

In the tannery yard the old ewe in the pen already wears a loose cord collar, a long tail of lead hanging over the hurdle. The other ewe has her head right through a gap in the hurdle. A round of sausage sits on top of the stacked oak boards.

Tob sees the sausage before anything else. "Is that—"

"For later," Bram says. "You'll want it after the oak."

Tob takes it as if it's a medal and buttons it inside his shirt.

By the pump Bram gives you the Fist post news, low: "Lem Cotter's still at the post. Unpaid since Ches, wants work, and he told the whole drovers' trough the day Rusk was going. I don't think he's our man. I think he's just loud." A beat. "But the serjeant, Brack, says two new tabards were stolen off his wash line in Tarsakh. That's a month before Dace's load went. And Pennick at the ford said something to Lem coming back up: *You lot again, and still not paying for the trough.* Men in Fist coats, watering at Lark's and not paying for it. More than once." He lets that settle. "So somebody planned this from the thaw. And Pennick has seen their faces, even if he only remembers that they didn't pay."

Tob's eyes go wide. "Two coats. So there's two of them at least."

In the pit, the tanner heaves a hide over and says nothing.

Bram nods at the stack. "Two to a board, three trips. They're heavy. Down the lane on the hard ruts, along the hurdle to the grey stone, and lay them just above the line where the grass changes. Nobody steps past the boards until the ewe's staked. I'll take her down and drive the stake. Everyone else on oak. Tob, you tap ahead of me with the stick every step once I'm off the boards. If it sounds hollow, say so before I find out."

---

Bram takes the near end of the top board. Hedda takes the far end without being asked. When they lift together the board comes up off the stack as wet and dead as a drowned man, and Hedda grunts once.

"Sin," she says. "He wasn't wrong."

They take it out through the hurdle gateway, round the forge wall and down the south lane on the hard middle of the ruts. Tob walks ahead with the pole and you follow with the stake and mallet. The board's weight sets the pace, and it's a slow one. The Coast Way's hammers fall behind you, and the tan-pit stink comes up the slope to meet you.

The pasture lies below. In the top corner the sheep are packed against the hurdle with their heads up. None of them is grazing, even at midday. Down by the reeds a round dark patch shows plainly in the long grass. The water in the ditch lies grey-brown and still between the reeds.

The north hurdle runs from the lane down the slope toward the ditch. They set the board on the lane verge. Bram goes along inside the hurdle on the cropped grass, keeping a hand's breadth from the rails, and Tob goes a step ahead of him thumping the pole down. Each thump sounds dull and solid, like knocking on a table.

She looks up the lane toward the forge, at the five boards still stacked by the pump, at the empty bend, and at the three of them who are carrying.

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell walks the last few paces to the shed, where Davy stands with the fork, and spends a moment looking not at the boy but at the nest of sacking and the ox goad laid along its edge.

"You sleep with the salt." He says it without mockery. "Then you'll hear what the gate doesn't tell. A gate only squeals when it's used. A man who doesn't want to be heard climbs, or comes over the back wall by the forge, where the shadows lie after the fire's banked." He glances toward the Thunderhammer's wall, then back. "Have you heard anything on the boards behind the shed at night? Scraping, boots on the wall, a horse that stops but doesn't come in? Any lights in the lane after last bell?"

He steps over to the two new casks, and runs a thumb lightly along the bright hoops, just below the bung. "And these. New, and Tam nodded at them. Where did they come from?" He doesn't look up. "You'd know if a cask had been broached and rehooped. Has anyone touched them since they came in?" Then, lower, still to Davy: "Has anyone come asking for Master Venn since the thaw who didn't go to him—looked over the yard, asked whose load it was, wanted to count the wheels?"

He steps back, dusting the heel of his hand on his thigh, and takes out the last of the bread. He tears it in half and offers one piece to Davy. "Keep the stick. It's your yard too." Then he turns and makes one more slow circuit, letting his eyes travel over the gate, the back wall, the shadows between the stalls and the forge, while the morning loses its edge toward noon.

At the first bell after noon he means to be at Ruddock's pump, with the rest, before the boards go down.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Davy watches Pell look at the sacking nest. He goes red, though Pell hasn't laughed at it.

He takes the questions one at a time, the way he takes everything, leaning on the fork and thinking about each one.

"The back wall's the Thunderhammer's," he says first, pointing at the soot-black bricks. "Fifteen foot, and hot halfway up till past midnight. Nobody's coming over that, not unless they want their hands cooked." He points the fork the other way, at the lean-to. "Behind the shed is Mother Tull's byre. She's got a cow and two goats, and the goats shout at anything. Cats, mostly. They shouted at the fox." He frowns, honestly going back over the nights. "No boots. No horse stopping. After last bell there's Gethin's lad with the lantern, banking up, and that's every night. Then nothing."

When Pell gets to the casks, the boy brightens, because he knows this one.

"Cooper's, by Ashby's. This morning. Master Venn pulled the barrow in himself with the cooper's boy pushing the back end, and the boy didn't like it." He nods at the bright hoops under Pell's thumb. "Tam knocked them both and looked at a head, and that's all. Nobody's had the bungs out. They're empty. Tam fills them at the pump the morning we go."

Under Pell's thumb the hoops are clean new iron, seated evenly all the way round. The staves are pale and the seams are tight. No marks from a hoop driver show anywhere except where the cooper set them, and there's no fresh scraping by the bung. They're two new casks that have stood in a yard for an hour.

For the last question Davy takes longer, scuffing the fork in the straw.

"Since the thaw? The Fist lad. I told the big one. He came twice." He shakes his head. "Folk go past the gate all day for the forge, but they're looking at the forge. Nobody's stood and counted wheels." He considers it again, fairly. "Not that I saw. I'm in the stalls a lot."

When Pell tells him to keep the stick, he straightens like he did before.

"It's a goad," he says, very seriously. "But I will."

Then he goes back to the straw.

Pell walks the yard once more, slowly. He looks at the gate on its squealing hinge, the hot brick of the back wall, the gap between the last stall and the forge where the shadows would lie after the fire is banked, and the lean-to's back boards. Through those boards he can hear goats complaining in Mother Tull's byre. Nothing has been pried, climbed or scuffed. Nobody has been in this yard who didn't come through the gate.

Partway round the circuit, the bell at the Song of the Morning rings for the first hour after noon. It carries thinly over the hammering.

---

The walk to the tanner's is the same as this morning: the Coast Way at its midday loudest, the turning behind the last forge, and the stink coming round the soot-black wall to meet him. By the time Pell reaches Ruddock's hurdle gateway, the bell has been silent for a good while.

The yard is emptier than it was this morning. Five oak pit boards are stacked by the pump, black with soaking. The horn lantern hangs off the pump handle and a full pail stands under the spout. The mallet and stake that lay on top of the boards are gone. In the pen by the shed the old foot-rot ewe is chewing, with a loose cord collar round her neck and a long lead hanging over the hurdle. The other ewe has her head right through a gap in the hurdle and looks pleased about it.

Ruddock is in the same pit with the same pole. He looks up at Pell the way he'd look at one more wagon at his gate.

"You're late," he says. "Your lot's gone down with the first board. That's one." He points the dripping pole at the stack. "Five to go. They're heavy as sin, and they come back with the lantern."

The pole goes back into the scum. Out past the gap in the north hurdle, down the slope and out of sight, the pasture is waiting.

*(Out of game: Pell ate the bacon and bread on the walk over from the inn, so there was none left to tear in half. If he wants to give Davy something from his rations, say so. No rolls this round: reading a yard is Pell's trade, and nothing in it was out of place.)*

 *(Out of game: none of these needed a roll, and each was settled in the scene above.)*
1. Asking Davy about night noises was plain talk. He answered: there were no boots, no horse and no lights after last bell except Gethin's lad banking the fire, and nobody could come over a back wall that's 15 feet high and still hot.
2. Checking the casks is routine for Pell, so it needed no roll. The hoops are evenly seated new iron and the bungs have never been out. Davy and Tam say nobody has touched them since Venn brought them in this morning.
3. Asking who came round was plain talk. Davy says nobody counted wheels or looked the yard over since the thaw, apart from the Fist lad's two visits.
4. This can't happen: Pell ate the bacon and bread on the walk from the inn, so he has nothing left to tear. He could give Davy something from his rations if he wants to.
````

## Round 24

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram looks at the one board sunk into the turf, then at the cropped grass running out from the stone, and rubs the small of his own back.

"We don't need six," he says. "Ruddock's father did the work for us. This side of the stone is clay, and the sheep proved it before we did. The boards were to stand on if we couldn't find hard ground. We've found it." He nods at the board they've laid. "Two more, so three in all. One for me, one for you, and one for Tob to jump back onto. Sefa stands on the clay higher up. That's two more trips, not five."

He turns to Sefa, half apologetic. "Leave the stake and mallet here on the stone. Take Tob's end of a board with him. The lad can sound the way down with the butt end over his shoulder, as long as he walks in front."

Back up the lane they go. Bram and Hedda take the second board and Sefa and Tob the third. They lay them out along the cropped side of the grass line in a shallow curve, about ten feet apart, ends toward the furrow. Bram's board is the lowest and nearest the furrow's end, Hedda's on his left, and Tob's highest and furthest along. Sefa's place is up the slope behind and off to the side of them all, on bare clay.

When the boards are down, Bram walks the old ewe down the lane on her lead and in along the hurdle. She doesn't care where she's going.

"Places first," he says quietly. "Everyone on their board. Hedda, javelins in hand. Tob, pole down, and listen to it." He waits until they're set. "Signal's simple. When it breaks the turf I throw, and that's everyone's go. If I shout *back*, you get onto clay and keep going uphill. If it gets hold of someone, all of us hit it and Sefa shoves."

He takes the stake and mallet off the stone. Tob sounds every step ahead of him down into the long grass. Bram goes about twenty feet below his board, to where the furrow sags out and fades, right across the end of the line where the thing was last moving. Not one step further.

He drives the stake in with four hard blows of the mallet and ties the ewe's lead to it short enough that she can't wander off the line. Then he backs straight up the way they came, in Tob's own steps, and onto his board. He sets the shield and takes a javelin in his right hand.

"Now we're quiet," he says, barely above a breath. "Let her do the talking."

*(Out of game: Bram stands still and watches the grass around the ewe and along the furrow, waiting for any ripple or sag. Passive Perception, unless you want a roll.)*

Sefa Marrin: I lay the stake and mallet beside the hurdle on the sounded ground, where nobody will trip over them.

“I can share an end with Tob if that helps. I’m not strong, but between us we may manage it. We’ll test the weight in the yard before carrying it down.”

I go back with them for the remaining boards. If Tob and I can safely manage one end together, I help carry; otherwise I leave the lifting to Bram and Hedda and keep the route clear.

We leave the ewe in her pen until the boards are laid and everyone’s ready.
````

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Javelin: the signal throw, as the ankheg breaks the turf · roll · presses: prose, [Roll the die]

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Javelin: the signal throw, as the ankheg breaks the turf · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The second board is easier for knowing what's coming. Bram and Hedda take it between them up the lane and back down. Hedda doesn't grunt this time. She just sets her jaw.

In Ruddock's yard Sefa takes hold of one end of the third board with Tob before anyone lifts. She gets it off the stack to her knees. It's wet, dead weight and it bites into her fingers. She can hold it, but not quickly. Tob takes most of it without saying so and walks with the shaft of grey ash over his other shoulder, the butt end out in front. They go down the lane slowly, with two stops to change grip. At the second stop Tob says, "It's nothing," before anyone has asked him. Then he says it again for the board's benefit.

They lay the boards in a shallow curve along the cropped side of the grass line, ten feet apart, ends pointing at the furrow. Bram's is lowest, a pace above the grey stone, where the sunken line fades out below it. Hedda's is on his left and Tob's is highest and furthest along. Each one settles into the turf with the same wet sound. Sefa picks her place on the bare clay up the slope, behind them and off to one side. From there she can see all three boards, the long grass below them and the end of the furrow without anyone standing in her line.

Bram walks the old ewe down the lane on her cord and in along the hurdle. She comes the way she does everything, chewing. In the top corner the rest of the flock watches her go past with their heads up, and one of them gives a thin bleat. She doesn't answer it.

"Places first."

Hedda steps onto her board and pulls one javelin from her belt, then the other, and holds them both in her right hand. Tob goes onto his and sets the pole butt down on the turf in front of him. He stands there with his head on one side like a man listening at a door.

Bram takes the stake and mallet off the stone. Tob comes back down off his board to sound the way. Every step goes into the long grass a pole's thump ahead of Bram's boot. The ground is wetter at once. It gives under the boot heel and smells of the ditch. The thumps sound different down here, duller and slower to come back, but none of them sounds hollow. Twenty feet below his board, where the furrow sags and fades out, Bram stops right across the end of the line.

He sets the stake and brings the mallet down.

The first blow goes into the wet ground like a hand slapping a table. The second drives the stake to a third of its length. The ewe stops chewing for the first time all day.

The third blow and the fourth. The stake stands knee-high and won't move when he leans on it. He ties the ewe's lead off short, so she can turn but can't walk off the line. Then he backs straight up the slope in Tob's own steps, Tob going backwards ahead of him with the pole. The long grass gives way to cropped turf and Bram's boots are on oak. Tob climbs onto his own board. Bram brings the shield round and takes a javelin in his right hand.

"Now we're quiet. Let her do the talking."

For a while she doesn't talk at all. She stands at the end of her cord, looking up the slope at the four of them, and her jaw doesn't move. Then she pulls once against the stake, finds it holds, and bleats. It's a long, cracked, put-upon sound that carries across the whole field. In the top corner the flock answers her all at once. Then they go quiet, and so does she.

The hammers carry faintly from the Coast Way. A fly finds Hedda's neck, and she lets it stay.

The ewe bleats again.

This time Bram sees it before anyone else does, because he's been watching the end of the furrow and nothing else. A low shiver runs through the long grass about fifteen feet beyond the stake, on the ditch side. It isn't wind, because the wind is coming down the slope and this goes the other way. The grass tilts over in a short line and stands up again. A stretch of turf the width of a door sags a finger's depth, the way the furrow sags, except that this sag is new, and it's moving toward the stake.

The ewe has stopped bleating. She has all four feet braced, and she's pulling back against the cord with her eyes rolling white.

The ground under her opens.

It doesn't heave. It *drops*. The turf caves in on itself in a ring around her hind legs, and up out of the dark comes something the size of a pony, all brown-black chitin, wet with the ditch clay. The head comes first. It's a wedge of plated shell with two mandibles longer than a man's forearm, and they close on the ewe across her back with a sound like a green branch breaking. A smell comes up with it, sharp and sour as vinegar in a cut, and the grass round the hole curls brown where the clay off its shell touches it. Six legs claw up over the edge of the hole, with the long segmented body hunched behind them. The ewe makes no sound at all.

The cord snaps taut against the stake. The thing heaves backward toward its hole, and the stake holds.

That's Bram's signal, and the javelin is already in his hand.

The order settles like this: Bram first, then Sefa, then Hedda, then the ankheg, and Tob last.

The thing is hunched at the lip of its hole twenty feet below Bram's board, on the soft ground, with the ewe clamped in its mandibles. It's hauling backward against the cord, and the stake is leaning now, a hand's width out of true. Hedda is about twenty-two feet from it, with both javelins up and her weight on her back foot. Tob is about thirty feet away on the highest board, white to the lips, with the pole held across his body like a quarterstaff. He hasn't moved off the oak. Sefa is up on the bare clay, about forty-five feet back and off to the side, with nothing between her and the hole but grass.

That sour vinegar smell is coming up the slope now, stronger than the ditch.

It's Bram's turn.

*(Out of game: Bram's signal javelin hit for 1d6+3 = 5 piercing. The ankheg is at 34 of 39 hit points. Everyone else is unhurt: Bram 44/44, Sefa 38/38. Order: Bram, Sefa, Hedda, ankheg, Tob. Bram has one javelin left in hand; the thrown one is stuck in the beast.)*

*(Out of game: by Bram's sheet I think that natural 19 should have been a critical, because his Improved Critical crits on 19 or 20. The server counted a plain hit. If you agree, the extra 1d6 is owed, and I'd ask the table owner to correct it.)*

 Bram's eye and his hand are quicker than the beast's. Before the ankheg can set its legs to haul again, he is already balanced on the oak with his last javelin drawn back.

Sefa is slower to move. By the time the thing tears at the turf she has the Book of Shadows open in her hands on the clay. She's ready before Hedda, but not before Bram.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram looks at the one board sunk into the turf, then at the cropped grass running out from the stone, and rubs the small of his own back.

"We don't need six," he says. "Ruddock's father did the work for us. This side of the stone is clay, and the sheep proved it before we did. The boards were to stand on if we couldn't find hard ground. We've found it." He nods at the board already laid. "Two more, so three in all. One for me, one for you, and one for Tob to jump back onto. Sefa stands on the clay higher up. That's two more trips, not five."

He turns to you, a little apologetic. "Leave the stake and mallet here on the stone. Take Tob's end of a board with him. The lad can sound the way down with the butt end over his shoulder, as long as he walks in front."

You all go back up the lane.

The second board is easier for knowing what's coming. Bram and Hedda take it between them up the lane and back down. Hedda doesn't grunt this time. She just sets her jaw.

In Ruddock's yard you take hold of one end of the third board with Tob before anyone lifts. You get it off the stack as far as your knees. It's wet, dead weight, and it bites into your fingers. You can hold it, but not quickly. Tob takes most of it without saying so and walks with the grey ash shaft over his other shoulder, the butt end out in front. You go down the lane slowly and stop twice to change grip. At the second stop Tob says, "It's nothing," before anyone has asked him. Then he says it again for the board's benefit.

The boards go down in a shallow curve along the cropped side of the grass line, ten feet apart, ends pointing at the furrow. Bram's is lowest, a pace above the grey stone, where the sunken line fades out below it. Hedda's is on his left, and Tob's is highest and furthest along. Each one settles into the turf with the same wet sound. You pick your place on the bare clay up the slope, behind them and off to one side. From there you can see all three boards, the long grass below them and the end of the furrow, and nobody stands in your line.

Bram walks the old ewe down the lane on her cord and in along the hurdle. She comes the way she does everything, chewing. In the top corner the rest of the flock watches her go by with their heads up, and one of them gives a thin bleat. She doesn't answer it.

"Places first," Bram says quietly. "Everyone on their board. Hedda, javelins in hand. Tob, pole down, and listen to it."

Hedda steps onto her board, pulls one javelin from her belt and then the other, and holds them both in her right hand. Tob gets onto his and sets the pole butt-down on the turf in front of him. He stands with his head on one side like a man listening at a door.

"Signal's simple," Bram says. "When it breaks the turf I throw, and that's everyone's go. If I shout *back*, you get onto clay and keep going uphill. If it gets hold of someone, all of us hit it and Sefa shoves."

Bram takes the stake and mallet off the stone. Tob comes back down off his board to sound the way, and every step Bram takes into the long grass lands a pole's thump behind Tob's. The thumps sound different down there, duller and slower to come back, but none of them sounds hollow. About twenty feet below his board, where the furrow sags and fades out, Bram stops right across the end of the line.

He sets the stake and brings the mallet down.

The first blow goes into the wet ground like a hand slapping a table. The second drives the stake in a third of its length. The ewe stops chewing for the first time all day.

The third blow, and the fourth. The stake stands knee-high. Bram leans on it, then ties the ewe's lead off short, so she can turn but can't walk off the line. He backs straight up the slope in Tob's own steps, with Tob going backwards ahead of him with the pole, until Bram's boots are on oak. Tob climbs onto his own board. Bram brings the shield round and takes a javelin in his right hand.

"Now we're quiet," he says, barely above a breath. "Let her do the talking."

For a while she doesn't talk at all. She stands at the end of her cord looking up the slope at the four of you, and her jaw doesn't move. Then she pulls once against the stake, finds it holds, and bleats. It's a long, cracked, put-upon sound that carries across the whole field. In the top corner the flock answers her all at once. Then they go quiet, and so does she.

The hammers carry faintly from the Coast Way. A fly finds Hedda's neck, and she lets it stay.

The ewe bleats again.

The ewe stops bleating. She has all four feet braced, and she's pulling back against the cord with her eyes rolling white.

The ground under her opens.

It doesn't heave. It *drops*. The turf caves in on itself in a ring around her hind legs, and up out of the dark comes something the size of a pony, all brown-black chitin, wet with the ditch clay. The head comes first. It's a wedge of plated shell with two mandibles longer than a man's forearm, and they close on the ewe across her back with a sound like a green branch breaking. Six legs claw up over the edge of the hole, with the long segmented body hunched behind them. The ewe makes no sound at all.

The cord snaps taut against the stake. The thing heaves backward toward its hole, and the stake holds.

Bram's javelin is already in the air. It strikes and sticks in the shell. Before the thing can set its legs to haul again, he's balanced on the oak with his last javelin drawn back.

The order settles like this: Bram first, then you, then Hedda, then the thing, and Tob last.

The thing is hunched at the lip of its hole twenty feet below Bram's board, on the soft ground, with the ewe clamped in its mandibles. It's hauling backward against the cord, and the stake is leaning now, a hand's width out of true. Hedda is about twenty-two feet from it, with both javelins up and her weight on her back foot. Tob is about thirty feet away on the highest board, white to the lips, with the pole held across his body like a quarterstaff. He hasn't moved off the oak. You're up on the bare clay, about forty-five feet back and off to the side, with nothing between you and the hole but grass.

A sour vinegar smell is coming up the slope now, stronger than the ditch.

By the time the thing tears at the turf you have the Book of Shadows open in your hands on the clay. You're ready before Hedda, but not before Bram.

*(Out of game: you are at 38/38. Order: Bram, Sefa, Hedda, the creature, Tob.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

*Pell Dunmore pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't rise to *late*. He steps into the yard, takes in the stack of boards, the lantern, the pail, the ewe with her head through the hurdle, and Ruddock's pole going back into the scum.

"Late, but I'm here for the rest of the boards, not the talk," he says, easy. "Which way did they go down—north gap?" He is already moving toward the stack, but his eyes cut up the slope, measuring the light, the wind, the look of the grass beyond the hurdle.

He stops at the pit edge just long enough to ask: "And did they spread out? I'll take a board and the next leg, but I'd rather walk onto it knowing where their line is than come over the edge into an argument."

Then he leans to lift the next pit board, ready to follow.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ruddock doesn't look up at *late*, and he doesn't look up when Pell crosses to the stack. Three boards are stacked by the pump, black and wet. The horn lantern hangs off the handle and the pail stands full under the spout. Only the hurdle-breaker is left in the pen. Her head is right through the gap and her jaw is working. The old foot-rot ewe and her cord collar are gone.

Pell asks his two questions at the edge of the pit. Ruddock answers them in order and keeps the pole moving.

"Not the gap." The dripping end of the pole points out through the north hurdles, where the ground drops away behind the nettles. "That's a steep drop and my ditch, and it comes out at the bottom by the reeds, in the wet. I'd not. Go round the forge and down the lane, and in over the top of the north hurdle. Follow it halfway down to my father's stone. That's where they've put my oak."

He turns the hide.

"Spread out? Your big one drew it in my mud this morning. When they came back up they were talking at my pump, and it's what he drew. A curve on the clay above the stone, ten foot apart, not a row. He said three boards would do, not six, and they took two more down." He jerks his chin at the empty half of the pen. "Then he came back again for my old girl and walked her down on the cord himself. That was a while since. Long enough to drive a stake."

Pell bends to the stack and takes hold of the end of the top board. It comes up off the others as far as his knees and no further. It's a man's length of soaked oak, cold and dead heavy, and the wet bites into his fingers. One man could drag it. Carrying it takes two.

A sound comes up over the byre wall from below. It's a sheep's bleat, long and cracked and sorry for itself, and it carries across the whole hillside. The flock in the top corner answers all at once in a ragged chorus, and then they all go quiet.

Ruddock's pole stops in the water.

A second bleat starts and breaks off in the middle.

For a moment there's nothing but the hammers on the Coast Way. Then Bram's voice comes up the slope from somewhere out of sight, flat and hard, one word: *"Now!"*

Below it, fainter, the sheep in the top corner start up again all together, and this time they don't stop.

Ruddock heaves himself up onto the brick lip of the pit with water pouring off his waders, and there he stops. He stands on the edge with the pole in his fist and stares at the gap in the north hurdles, as if he could see through the nettles and the byre wall to his field.

"That's it up, then," he says. "Go on. The board'll keep."

Pell stands by the pump holding one end of the oak up off the stack. The lane round the forge wall is on his left, and the gap in the north hurdles is straight ahead.

*(Out of game: I got the yard wrong last round. By the time Pell walked in, the others had already been back for the second and third boards and Bram had taken the old ewe down. So three boards are left by the pump and only the hurdle-breaker is in the pen.)*

*(Out of game: by the lane it's about two hundred feet to the boards. Running with Dash plus Cunning Action's Dash, Pell covers 90 feet a round and gets there in the fight's third round. The fight is in round 1 now, on Bram's turn.)*
````

*Bram Halloway pressed [Roll the die] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram lets the last javelin fly from the board the moment the first one sticks, at the same seam behind the head-plate.

Then he drops the shield to his guard, draws the longsword and goes down the slope. He doesn't charge blind. He goes the way he came up, in Tob's sounded steps toward the stake. He closes on the ankheg from the uphill side, so that he's between it and the others, and he angles a little off Hedda's line so he isn't standing in front of her throws.

"Keep throwing, Hedda! Tob, stay on the oak!"

He reaches it and swings at the joint where the mandibles meet the head, trying to make it open its jaws on the ewe.

This is the moment it matters: it's out of its hole and busy, and if it gets back down it's gone. So Bram doesn't stop. He puts everything into it and keeps hacking: two more cuts, at the same joint or wherever its shell has opened.

*(Out of game: Attack action. First attack is the javelin from the board at 20 feet. Then I move about 15 feet down Tob's sounded path to melee, staying uphill of it and off Hedda's line, and make the second attack with the longsword. Then **Action Surge**: Attack action again, two more longsword attacks, 1d8+3 one-handed with the shield. Action Surge 1 → 0. If it drops before all the attacks are used, the rest go unused. On the 19: thanks for passing it on. Whatever the table decides is fine with me.)*

Sefa Marrin: I brace my feet on the clay and raise a hand over the open book.

I cast **Eldritch Blast**, sending both beams into the creature’s exposed body. I **don’t use Repelling Blast**—I want it caught above ground, not shoved back into its hole.

I hold my position off to the side, keeping my line clear of Bram and Hedda.
